For the batch opening, The decision sequence is deliberately mechanical. Begin with the verified close and market stage, check the moving averages and momentum condition, then inspect the stop, target method and calculated payoff. After those numbers are fixed, ask whether a company-specific fact can change the probability of the outcome and name the event that would invalidate the thesis. This order keeps an appealing narrative from hiding a failed entry gate. It also helps a beginner: each number has a job, each conclusion has a condition, and each unresolved evidence gap is visible. When a later session supplies new evidence, update the affected record and rerun the same tests rather than smoothing the earlier result. This is bridge 1 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

The reader should treat the failed gates as binding constraints rather than as a score to average with the good numbers. The result is designed to be reproducible: a future ledger can change the decision only by changing the evidence, not by changing the prose. The archive therefore records both the rejection and the trigger that would reopen the file.

REJECTED FOR NOW: quality business, wrong price and wrong chart

eClerx Services (NSE: ECLERX) | CMP Rs 1,866.80 on 9 Oct 2026 | Core Alpha candidate | Layer 1 result: three of five gates pass, two fail | Action: do not buy; re-underwrite on the triggers in Chapter 9[1][15]

0. Executive Summary

Start here if you have never read a company report. eClerx is a Mumbai-based outsourcing company. It runs data, analytics, finance and operations work for large banks, asset managers, retailers, luxury brands and technology firms, mostly in the United States. About 94 percent of its FY26 revenue came from North America, the UK and Europe, and 93 percent was billed on time-and-material terms, so revenue tracks headcount and billing rates rather than product sales.[3] Think of it as a large team of trained analysts that clients rent by the hour, with the work moving gradually from plain process handling toward data science and automation.

The report judges whether a new position should be opened. It does so in layers. Layer 1 is a pass-or-fail gate sheet with five tests. Return on capital employed (ROCE) asks how many rupees of yearly operating profit the business earns for every hundred rupees of capital tied up in it; 34.4 means Rs 34 earned per Rs 100, a very good rate. Debt to equity compares borrowings with shareholders' money; 0.15 means the company owes about 15 paise for each rupee the owners have put in, and most of that is office-lease accounting. Cash from operations divided by profit after tax (CFO/PAT) checks whether profit arrives as actual cash; 1.17 means every rupee of reported profit produced about Rs 1.17 of cash over three years.

Two further gates are about the price rather than the business. The first is the chart stage, a four-step description of a stock's life cycle: Stage 1 is a quiet base, Stage 2 a rising phase, Stage 3 a top or a stall and Stage 4 a decline. ECLERX is in Stage 3. The second is asymmetry, which divides the likely gain by the likely loss. The likely gain is the distance from the price to a target. The likely loss is the distance from the price to a stop, the level at which you would admit the idea is wrong and sell. For ECLERX that ratio is 0.65, and the house floor is 3.0. A stop is protective, much like a smoke alarm: you hope never to use it, but you set it before you need it.

The company also uses its own vocabulary. ACV, or annual contract value, is the yearly value of newly signed deals and works as an order book. Time-and-material billing means clients pay for the hours worked rather than a fixed project price. ATR, or average true range, is the typical daily price swing; here it is Rs 68.66, and it sizes the stop. A bonus issue of shares in a 1:1 ratio doubles the share count without changing the value of the company, which is why per-share figures in this report are restated. A buyback is the company purchasing its own shares from owners, usually to return surplus cash.

To read the chapters, take the numbers in order. Chapters 1 to 3 explain who the company is and how it earns money. Chapter 4 audits the accounts. Chapter 5 runs the three-layer test and reaches the verdict. Chapters 6 to 8 compare peers, read the chart and attack the thesis from the bear side. Chapters 9 and 10 list what would change the answer and what to monitor, and Chapter 11 gives every source with its read status. Every table is preceded by a short guide and followed by plain-language meaning. Where a value could not be verified the report says N/A and gives the reason.

This dossier answers one question: should a new Core Alpha position in ECLERX be opened at today's price? The short answer is no, and the reason is not the business. The business is excellent on every audited quality test. The reason is that two mechanical gates in the framework fail on 9 October 2026 data: the price chart is in Stage 3 (a topping or pullback phase) rather than Stage 2 (a prior rising phase), and the reward-to-risk ratio measured against an evidence-anchored target is 0.65, far below the Core floor of 3.0.

The earlier shortlist record (dated 27 Sep 2026) listed ECLERX as an immediate buy at Rs 1,899 with a base target of Rs 2,550 and an asymmetry of 4.37. That record rested on a Stage 2 reading and a target that needs roughly 26 times FY28 consensus earnings, against a current forward multiple near 22.5. Fresh data does not support either input, so the old verdict is withdrawn.

Reading how to read the next table: the left column names a measure, the middle column gives today's reading and the right column states the rule or comparison that reading is judged against. Take the ROCE row as an example. The reading is 34.4 percent, the rule is a floor of 15 percent, so the gate passes with room to spare. Rows that end in a fail are the reason the verdict is negative, so scan the right column first and the middle column second.

Read the table as two halves. The top three rows say the company is safe to own in terms of balance-sheet risk and rich in returns. The bottom rows say the market is not offering a good entry. The price sits almost on its 50-day average, but that average is sliding, volume is thin (0.39 times the 20-day average) and seven distribution days appeared in the last 25 sessions. A distribution day is a session where the price falls on heavier volume, which usually means large holders are selling into strength. Seven in 25 is a warning, not a verdict. The wider market adds weight to the caution: the Nifty 50 is in a downtrend, only 17.7 percent of index stocks trade above their 50-day average, and just one of eleven sectors is above its 20-day average. A stock that holds up in such a tape deserves respect, which is why relative strength of plus 22.08 percent over 63 days is listed in Chapter 5 as a positive, but strength alone does not repair a failed gate.

What this means for an owner. A buyer today pays 24.1 times trailing earnings for a company whose latest quarter showed operating margin of 23 percent, 260 basis points below the prior quarter, while the consensus target of Rs 1,980 is only 6 percent above the price. The company can be a fine long-term holding; it is not a good Core entry at this level and this chart. The next chapter explains who the company is, so that the later numbers make sense.

Context note: ECLERX in one paragraph. The story is a high-return outsourcer growing revenue 15.2 percent in dollars while margin slips to 23 percent. The numbers that matter are ROCE 34.4 percent, debt to equity 0.15, CFO/PAT 1.17, price Rs 1,866.80 and asymmetry 0.65. The verdict is REJECTED, set by two failed gates: Stage 3 and asymmetry below 3.0. If price retests about Rs 1,765 and holds, or closes above a rising 50-day average, the name returns to review. If it closes below Rs 1,693, the idea is off until a new base forms.

1. History and Corporate Identity

eClerx was founded in 2000 by PD Mundhra and Anjan Malik, who still sit in the promoter group, and listed on the Indian exchanges in 2007 (NSE and BSE code 532927). The idea was to offer back-office knowledge work to financial firms from low-cost Indian centres, then widen into marketing operations, digital and analytics. Today the company reports 19 subsidiaries across India, the US, UK, Europe and Asia-Pacific.[3]

Why history matters here: promoter-founders who still own 54.5 percent have both the incentive and the control to steer capital allocation. In FY25 each of the two founders tendered about Rs 89.8 crore in the company's buyback, which shows the promoters also take cash out when the company returns capital, not only when they buy.[3][5] Management today is led by CEO Kapil Jain and CFO Srinivasan Nadadhur, with an independent chairman, Shailesh Kekre. The statutory auditor is Price Waterhouse Chartered Accountants LLP; audit-related fees of Rs 2.04 crore are modest for a company with Rs 4,117 crore of revenue.[3]

Reading guide for the corporate-actions table: each row in the last twelve months, with the key facts in the middle column and the document where the fact was found in the right column. Take the buyback row aloud: the company spent Rs 300 crore buying 625,000 of its own shares at Rs 4,800 each, settled on 2 January 2026. Because the bonus later doubled the share count, that price equals Rs 2,400 on today's share base, which makes it easy to compare with the market price.

Corporate actions in the last 12 months (reading guide: each row is a capital event that changed the share count or cash returned)
EventDetailSource
BuybackRs 300 crore, 625,000 shares at Rs 4,800 (pre-bonus), settled 2 Jan 2026FY26 annual report[3]

The table shows a company that returns cash steadily and without drama. The buyback at Rs 4,800 pre-bonus equals Rs 2,400 post-bonus, which is about 29 percent above today's price, so shareholders who tendered sold well above where the market now values the stock. The bonus issue and the final dividend of Re 1 are small in cash terms but tell owners that management prefers to share surplus rather than hoard it. The Investor Day on 2 September 2026 set a public ambition of USD 1 billion in revenue over a multi-year blueprint, which is a target the company has chosen to be measured against. Revenue of USD 468.9 million in FY26 means that goal needs roughly a doubling, and annual growth near 15 percent in dollars would take about five years to reach it. That is a statement of intent, not a forecast, and this report treats it as a claim to monitor. The meeting with Invesco on 25 September 2026 shows investor outreach, but a routine meeting notice carries no financial information and is not used as evidence for any number here.

Buyback, in numbers. A buyback at Rs 4,800 pre-bonus, or Rs 2,400 on the bonus-adjusted base, returned Rs 300 crore, equal to 0.42 of one year of FY26 net profit of Rs 706 crore (300 / 706). The meaning is that the company had spare cash and chose to return it at a price well above today's Rs 1,866.80. If management repeats a buyback at a price near Rs 1,900, that would signal confidence and would shrink the share count. Unless that happens and the chart improves, the buyback is history, not a reason to buy now.

For an owner, governance is the point. Governance looks clean on the evidence read: no related-party transactions beyond key-person dividends, commissions and buyback participation, no pledge of promoter shares (confirmed in the exchange shareholding filing for the January 2026 capital restructuring), and an independent chairman.[3][10] The pledge status for 30 Jun 2026 was not readable in the PDF version of the pattern, so that single quarter carries an explicit N/A in the payload rather than an assumption.[9] Founders who hold more than half of the company and still tender into buybacks are aligned with minority holders on cash returns but may differ on timing, because a founder selling at a high price has different incentives from a buyer who needs the price to rise further. With the owners in view, the next chapter shows how the company earns its money.

Governance triggers. If the quarterly shareholding filing shows any promoter pledge, the governance reading moves from clean to review. If promoter holding falls below 50 percent without a stated reason, treat it as a warning, since it would be the first sustained sell-down. Unless the 30 Jun 2026 pattern is read in a machine-readable form, the pledge field for that quarter stays N/A. A new buyback announced below Rs 2,000 would be read as positive for confidence.

2. Operations and Revenue Model

Operations explain how work turns into revenue and then into margin, so this chapter covers segments, unit economics and cost in turn, each with the Q1 FY27 call as the freshest evidence.

2.1 Segments and geographies

eClerx sells outsourced work in three families: Financial Services (capital markets, banking and insurance operations, compliance), Communications, Media and Technology (CMT) plus Technology, and Retail, Fashion and Luxury, Manufacturing and Distribution. A newer "Emerging" group covers finance and accounting services. Within every vertical, the work is a mix of process operations, data management, analytics and, increasingly, automation and AI-assisted delivery. The company says its Analytics and Automation line grew 7 percent quarter on quarter in Q1 FY27 and now exceeds a USD 100 million annual run rate.[2]

Reading how to read the next table: the first column says which cut of revenue you are looking at (where clients sit, how they pay, or what type of service), the third column gives the FY26 amount in Rs million and the last column gives the share of the total. Read one row aloud: North America contributed Rs 32,513 million, which is 79.0 percent of operating revenue. Within each block the shares add to about 100 percent, so the three blocks are three separate ways of slicing the same revenue.

FY26 revenue by geography and contract type (reading guide: the first block shows where clients sit, the second shows how they pay; amounts in Rs million from the annual report)

The table shows a business that is almost entirely US-dollar earning and almost entirely billed on effort. Time-and-material billing means eClerx rarely carries the risk of fixed-price overruns, but it also means revenue falls when clients cut hours. The top ten clients supplied 61 percent of FY26 revenue, which is the single most important concentration fact in this report.[3] Fixed-price work is only 7 percent, so the company is not betting on project estimates, and software and licensing is under 4 percent, so this is a services business and not a product company. Dollar earnings against rupee costs give a natural margin tailwind when the rupee weakens and a headwind when it strengthens. Q1 FY27 used an implied rate near Rs 91.5 per dollar, so a move of one rupee in either direction changes rupee revenue by about 1.1 percent before any hedging. The annual report does not give vertical-level margins, so those stay N/A and no profit mix by vertical is assumed here.

Concentration, in numbers. Top ten clients are 61 percent of FY26 revenue, so the largest accounts decide the growth rate. In Q1 FY27 Financial Services was down 0.1 percent after a 2.8 percent fall in Q4, while CMT rose 1.3 percent. Management expects a recovery in the second half of FY27. If Financial Services is still flat or negative in the Q2 FY27 report, the concentration risk is confirmed and the KPI view worsens. If it grows by more than 2 percent sequentially, the flaw loses weight and the stock deserves another look.

Owner view. Revenue growth is a function of client budgets in a few large accounts. The Q1 FY27 call showed this: Financial Services was flat at minus 0.1 percent quarter on quarter after minus 2.8 percent in Q4, and management said it expects a recovery from the second half of FY27, which is a forecast, not a result.[2]

2.2 Unit economics

Unit economics for a labour-based outsourcer come down to three dials: billed headcount, revenue per billed head and the cost of the people and seats behind them. In Q1 FY27 operating revenue was USD 125.9 million, up 15.2 percent year on year and 2.8 percent over the prior quarter, the twelfth consecutive quarter of sequential growth. Utilization was 75.5 percent, attrition 21 percent and days sales outstanding 79. Total headcount fell 0.6 percent quarter on quarter while billed headcount rose, which is the productivity signal management wants to show.[2]

Newly signed annual contract value (ACV) was USD 41 million, up 25 percent year on year. FY26 ACV was about USD 170 million, and management aims to match or exceed that. Typical revenue roll-off (contracts ending or shrinking) is 15 to 20 percent a year, so the company must sign roughly that much new work just to stand still.[2]

Reading across. A 25 percent rise in deal wins supports the growth forecast, but the offsetting roll-off means the quality of the sales engine matters more than any single quarter. Management itself names "sharpening sales focus, improving revenue predictability and reducing client concentration" as opportunities, which concedes that these are not yet solved.[14] Costs are the next piece.

2.3 Cost structure and margin

Q1 FY27 operating EBITDA was Rs 265 crore, a 23 percent margin. That is 260 basis points below Q4 FY26 and 96 basis points below a year earlier. Management attributed 210 basis points to annual wage increments, 40 basis points to computer and network spend, partly offset by a 60 basis point cut in selling and distribution cost. A further drag is the growth of lower-margin delivery centres in Cairo, Lima and Manila. Management also plans about 1,600 new seats across Mumbai, Pune, Chandigarh, Mohali and Coimbatore, with FY27 capital spending of Rs 130 to 150 crore.[2]

Reading guide for the margin table: each row, the middle column gives its value and the right column says where the number comes from or how to judge it. Read the FY27 guided band row aloud: management expects EBITDA margin between 24 and 28 percent for the full year, and points to a midpoint near 26 percent. The Q1 row sits one point below the bottom of that band, so the question for the rest of the year is how fast the margin climbs back.

Margin and guidance (reading guide: compare the quarter's reading with the company's own FY27 band)
MeasureValueComment

The numbers show Q1 at 23 percent, one point below the low end of the FY27 band. A rebound to the midpoint needs three or four quarters at roughly 27 percent. If margin simply stays near the Q1 level, FY27 profit growth would fall well short of the revenue growth consensus expects. Consider what the bridge looks like in basis points: 210 basis points of wage increments, 40 of computer and network spend, less 60 of lower selling and distribution cost gives a net 190 basis points of drag from known items, and the remaining 70 basis points of the 260 point quarterly fall comes from mix and other costs that the call did not itemise. A basis point is one hundredth of a percentage point, so 100 of them make one percent. The expansion of about 1,600 seats and capital spending of Rs 130 to 150 crore also add depreciation, which was already Rs 188 crore on a trailing basis. Growth in the lower-margin centres in Cairo, Lima and Manila helps revenue but dilutes the average margin, and the call also described mild pricing pressure. Management's confidence in the band rests on utilisation, now 75.5 percent, rising once the new seats are filled.

The Q1 margin dip. Operating margin fell to 23 percent from 26 percent in Q4, with Rs 265 crore of operating profit against Rs 283 crore a quarter earlier even though sales rose from Rs 1,107 crore to Rs 1,152 crore. The meaning is that cost growth outran revenue by a clear margin. If Q2 FY27 shows 24 percent or more, the dip is seasonal and the KPI grade returns to pass. If it prints below 24 percent for a second quarter, the band itself is at risk and the Layer 2 grade falls to fail, which would trigger a downgrade of the whole report.

Where this leaves the owner. Margin, not revenue, is the swing factor for earnings in the next twelve months. Chapter 5 turns this into a formal KPI watch item. First, the sector backdrop.

3. Sector Dynamics

Business process and knowledge process outsourcing sells labour arbitrage plus domain skill. Demand follows client operating budgets, regulation and, now, how fast clients adopt AI in back-office work. AI helps vendors on cost but invites clients to ask for lower prices; the Q1 call described mild pricing pressure and said agentic-AI deals are still small.[2] A weaker US regulatory burden also reduces compliance projects in banking, which management linked to slower top-five client spending.[2]

The Indian listed set of comparable companies is mixed on the price chart. In the run's data, Firstsource (FSL) and Sagility (SAGILITY) are both Stage 3; Mphasis is Stage 4; Coforge, Persistent and Cyient are Stage 2 with relative strength well above ECLERX in the case of Cyient and Coforge. The sector is therefore not uniformly weak: money is rotating into select names rather than the outsourcing group.

Reading how to read the next table: every row is one company and every column is one yardstick. P/E is the price divided by yearly earnings per share, so a lower number means a cheaper stock for the same profit. ROCE and ROE measure how well the company turns capital into profit. Operating margin is the share of sales left after running costs. The last column is the chart stage. Read the first row aloud: eClerx trades at 24.1 times earnings, earns 34.4 percent on capital, keeps 23 percent of sales as operating profit and sits in Stage 3.

The table shows eClerx is priced like Firstsource but earns about twice the return on capital. Sagility is cheaper but also earns a lower return on capital and equity. On quality per rupee of price, eClerx is the better business; on price chart it is no better than either.[1][16][17] The three companies grew June-quarter sales at 23, 23 and 28 percent respectively, so growth does not separate them either. What separates them is margin and capital efficiency. Firstsource keeps 17 percent of sales as operating profit against 23 percent at eClerx, and its ROCE of 16.8 percent is only half of eClerx's 34.4 percent. Sagility's operating margin of 22 percent is close to eClerx, but its ROE of 10.6 percent is well below eClerx's 28.4 percent. All three sit in Stage 3 on the chart, so the market has not rewarded any of them over the past few months. That shared pattern suggests the weakness in ECLERX is partly a sector pause and not only a company story, which is useful when judging how long a base might take to form.

Context note: Firstsource (FSL) as the closest listed comparison. FSL trades at 23.8 times earnings with ROCE of 16.8 percent, ROE of 17.6 percent and June-quarter sales up 23 percent to Rs 2,725 crore from Rs 2,218 crore. It is Stage 3 with 63-day relative strength of plus 10.2 percent, below ECLERX at plus 22.08 percent. The story is a larger but lower-margin outsourcer. If FSL turns to Stage 2 while ECLERX stays in Stage 3, relative preference within the group would shift toward FSL. FSL is not underwritten in this report and no verdict is given for it.

Context note: Sagility (SAGILITY) as the healthcare-focused comparison. SAGILITY trades at 20.0 times earnings with ROCE of 13.4 percent, ROE of 10.6 percent and June-quarter sales up 28 percent to Rs 1,963 crore from Rs 1,539 crore, at a 22 percent operating margin. It is Stage 3 with relative strength of plus 10.42 percent. The cheaper multiple reflects lower returns on capital. If its ROCE rises above 20 percent while the multiple stays near 20, it would become the better value in the group. Until then, eClerx holds the quality lead and no verdict is given for Sagility here.

Sector verdict for an owner. The sector earns its growth from a small number of US-centric clients, so an owner is also betting on US corporate budgets and on how generative AI is shared between vendor and client. Those questions feed straight into the financial record in the next chapter.

4. Financial Audit

The audit reads the accounts in three steps: profit and loss, cash flow and balance sheet. Each step ends with the figure that feeds a Layer 1 gate in Chapter 5.

The table below uses Screener's consolidated series, cross-checked to the FY26 annual report (operating revenue Rs 41,170 million, EBITDA Rs 11,526 million, profit after tax Rs 7,062 million).[1][3] Amounts are in Rs crore except per-share data, which is restated for the 1:1 bonus.

Sales rose in every quarter (Rs 935 crore to Rs 1,152 crore, +23 percent in the year) but quarterly net profit has dropped from Rs 190 crore to Rs 164 crore in one quarter, a 14 percent sequential fall.[1] The Q1 seasonal wage cycle explains part of it, since Jun 2025 also had a 24 percent margin, but Jun 2026 profit is still 15 percent above Jun 2025 against revenue growth of 23 percent, so profit grew slower than sales year on year. Quarterly EPS tells the same story: Rs 17.47 in June 2026 against Rs 20.13 in March and Rs 14.87 a year earlier. The three middle quarters of FY26 each held operating margin at 26 to 27 percent, so Q1 FY27 is the outlier and the question is whether it is a seasonal dip or the start of a lower plateau. The company's own FY27 guidance of 24 to 28 percent leaves room for either answer. Revenue momentum is intact, with 12 straight quarters of sequential growth in dollars, so the debate is about cost, not demand. A reader should therefore watch the second-quarter operating profit line more closely than the sales line.

Earnings quality, in one line. Earnings quality is good but current earnings momentum is soft. The next section tests whether profit turns into cash.

Context note: five-year growth against the withdrawn target. Sales compounded at 21.4 percent and profit at 20.0 percent over five years, but TTM EPS is Rs 77.22 and FY27 consensus EPS is Rs 82.85, which is only 7.3 percent above TTM (82.85 / 77.22 = 1.073). The earlier target of Rs 2,550 needs FY28 EPS of Rs 98.14 and a 26 times multiple. If quarterly EPS climbs back above Rs 20 for two quarters, the FY27 estimate becomes easier to reach. If EPS stays near Rs 17.5, the estimates would need to be cut, and the Rs 1,980 target would be at risk.

Reading how to read the next table: the top block shows cash generated by operations, the second line shows reported profit, the ratio line divides one by the other and the last line shows free cash flow, which is cash from operations less spending on equipment and software. A ratio above 1.0 means the profit shown in the accounts arrived as cash. Read the FY26 column aloud: operations produced Rs 873 crore against profit of Rs 706 crore, a ratio of 1.24. Free cash flow for FY22 and FY23 was not read in the sources, so those two cells say N/A.

Working: the framework gate uses the three most recent years, FY24 to FY26. CFO is 526 + 655 + 873 = 2,054. PAT is 512 + 541 + 706 = 1,759. The ratio is 2,054 / 1,759 = 1.168, against a gate of 0.70. In FY26 the company also converted 104 percent of operating profit into CFO, spent Rs 97 crore on investing activities and Rs 622 crore on financing (buyback, dividends and lease payments).[1] The annual report states its own EBITDA-to-cash conversion at about 76 percent after tax and working capital.[3] The two measures differ because one starts from operating profit before tax and the other from EBITDA after tax and working-capital movement, so both are shown and neither is hidden. Free cash flow of Rs 756 crore in FY26 was 107 percent of net profit (756 / 706), which is unusually strong for a services company.

The one blemish: debtor days rose from 62 in FY24 to 86 in FY25 and 90 in FY26, so some of the cash strength came from other items. Q1 FY27 CFO was only Rs 107 crore, 38 percent of EBITDA, because the variable pay for FY26 is paid in Q1, which management flagged.[1][2]

Cash verdict. Profit is real cash, which is a strength. Rising receivable days are worth tracking each quarter because they would be the first sign of a client pushing back on invoices.

Receivable days. Debtor days rose from 62 in FY24 to 86 in FY25 and 90 in FY26, while Q1 FY27 days sales outstanding were 79 on the call. Slower collection ties up cash: at Rs 4,117 crore of sales, each extra day of receivables is about Rs 11 crore (4,117 / 365). If debtor days climb above 90 at the next annual or half-year reading, cash conversion would weaken and the cash KPI would move to watch. If they fall back toward 79, the FY26 jump was timing only.

Liabilities and equity on the top half must equal assets on the bottom half. Read the borrowings row aloud: debt of Rs 385 crore at March 2026 is mostly lease liability for offices, not bank loans. The balance-sheet figures matter because they connect accounting quality to investability. Low leverage reduces the chance that a temporary demand shock becomes a solvency event, but it does not by itself create an attractive entry. Returns on capital show that the operating model has earned strong economics; cash conversion tests whether those earnings have been collected; and the valuation column asks how much of that quality is already embedded. For ECLERX, the evidence is favorable on returns and leverage, yet the underwriting decision remains constrained by technical posture and reward-to-risk. The right conclusion is therefore not that the company is weak, but that the current price does not provide enough compensation for execution, multiple, and trend risk. That is the practical meaning of the table for a new investor.

Working: debt to equity is 385 / (92 + 2,469) = 385 / 2,561 = 0.15, against the gate of 0.70. Equity capital doubled from Rs 47 crore to Rs 92 crore because of the 1:1 bonus, not new money. ROCE by year was 34 percent in FY22, 36 percent in FY23, 32 percent in FY24, 28 percent in FY25 and 34 percent in FY26; the latest published ratio is 34.4 percent against a gate of 15 percent. ROE is 28.4 percent. Both ratios are strong because the business needs few fixed assets: Rs 1,054 crore of fixed assets supports Rs 4,117 crore of sales, a turnover of about 3.9 times. Contingent liabilities are small: income-tax demands of Rs 24.3 crore and indirect-tax demands of Rs 5.5 crore.[1][3]

Shareholding changed little through the year. Promoters hold 54.53 percent, foreign institutions 11.01 percent (down from 11.82 percent in March), domestic institutions 23.84 percent and public 8.43 percent, with 74,342 shareholders.[1] The January 2026 exchange filing recorded promoter holding at 54.52 percent with no pledge, no non-disposal undertaking and no other encumbrance.[10]

Balance-sheet verdict. The balance sheet carries almost no financial debt and the company has no credit rating requirement. Quality gates one to three pass with wide margin. The framework now compresses these tests into a formal gate sheet.

Context note: the bonus and the balance sheet. Equity capital moved from Rs 47 crore to Rs 92 crore on the 1:1 bonus, while reserves rose from Rs 2,259 crore to Rs 2,469 crore, a gain of Rs 210 crore in a year when net profit was Rs 706 crore, so a large share of profit left through the Rs 300 crore buyback, dividends and the bonus capitalisation. Total equity of Rs 2,561 crore supports debt to equity of 0.15. If borrowings rise above Rs 1,500 crore without matching profit growth, the 0.70 gate would come into view. Until then, balance-sheet risk stays low and does not drive the verdict.

5. The 3-Layer Underwriting

Underwriting runs in three layers: hard gates, company KPIs and entry posture. The layers are applied in order, and a failure in the first layer decides the verdict.

5.1 Layer 1: hard gates

Layer 1 is a pass-or-fail screen. A failed gate means the name is REJECTED for new capital until the gate passes again. The gate numbers come from the facts ledger and from the filings cited above.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is one gate. The second column is the rule, the third gives the reading and the arithmetic behind it, and the last column shows pass or fail. A single fail is enough to reject the name for new money. Read the asymmetry row aloud: the gain to the target is 1,980 minus 1,866.8, which is 113.2 rupees, and the loss to the stop is 1,866.8 minus 1,693.3, which is 173.5 rupees, so the ratio is 0.65 against a required 3.0.

Layer 1 gate sheet (reading guide: the third column shows the working; the last column is the result)
GateThresholdReading and workingResult
ROCEat least 15%34.4% (Screener, consolidated)[1]PASS
Debt to equitybelow 0.70385 / 2,561 = 0.15PASS
3-year cumulative CFO / PATat least 0.702,054 / 1,759 = 1.17PASS
Technical posture for CoreStage 1 breakout or Stage 2; no Stage 4 or death crossStage 3. Price 1,866.8 is below the 50-day average 1,884.68, which is falling; 200-day average 1,775.72 is rising. Test A fails (price not above a rising 50-day). Test B fails (base pivot about 2,132 is 3.86 ATR away with no breakout)FAIL
Asymmetryat least 3.0 (Core)(1,980 - 1,866.8) / (1,866.8 - 1,693.3) = 113.2 / 173.5 = 0.65FAIL

Stop working: the Core stop sits below the lower of the 20-day swing low (1,762) and the 50 and 200-day averages (1,884.68 and 1,775.72), minus one ATR (68.66). The lowest support is the swing low, 1,762, so the stop is 1,762 - 68.66 = 1,693.3, rounded to Rs 1,693. The logic is that a stock that falls through its swing low by a full day's typical range has broken the structure that justified the entry, so waiting longer only adds loss. The stop is 9.3 percent below today's price (173.5 / 1,866.8), which is a wide stop for a stock with an ATR of 3.7 percent of price. A wide stop makes the asymmetry harder to pass, which is correct: it forces a buyer either to wait for a lower price or to accept a smaller position. The gate sheet therefore does two jobs. It filters out names whose business is weak, and it filters out names whose price is too high for the risk the chart implies.

Worked example, from data to decision. Start with three facts: price Rs 1,866.8, swing low Rs 1,762, ATR Rs 68.66. Step one, the stop is 1,762 minus 68.66, which is Rs 1,693.3. Step two, the risk per share is 1,866.8 minus 1,693.3, which is Rs 173.5. Step three, the reward to the consensus target is 1,980 minus 1,866.8, which is Rs 113.2. Step four, asymmetry is 113.2 divided by 173.5, which is 0.65. Step five, compare with the floor of 3.0: 0.65 is below it, so the gate fails and the verdict is REJECTED. Check the cash gate the same way: 3-year CFO of 2,054 divided by 3-year PAT of 1,759 is 1.17, above 0.70, so that gate passes.

Target working: the framework requires the base target to be anchored to forward EPS times a 3-year median P/E, or to a measured move. The 3-year median P/E is not published in the sources read, so that multiple is N/A and the target uses an alternate anchor: the S&P Global consensus average of Rs 1,980 from 10 analysts, which equals about 23.9 times FY27 consensus EPS of Rs 82.85 (EPS figures are non-GAAP adjusted).[15] The prior record's Rs 2,550 would need 2,550 / 98.14 = 26.0 times FY28 consensus EPS of Rs 98.14, above the current forward multiple of 22.5 on FY27 estimates, so it is not accepted without support.

Support-ladder reading: the price is 0.26 ATR below the 50-day average, which on the ladder alone would count as at support (full size under 0.5 ATR). But the average is falling and the price is below it, so the ladder cannot override the stage test.

Gate-sheet reading for an owner. Three fundamental gates pass with room; two market-based gates fail. The result under the rules is REJECTED today. The level that would reverse the technical gate is a daily close above the 50-day average with that average turning up (Stage 2 reclaim). The level that would reverse the asymmetry gate is an entry price at or below about Rs 1,765, which gives (1,980 - 1,765) / (1,765 - 1,693) = 215 / 72 = 2.99, subject to the stop being recomputed at that time.

Gate triggers. If the daily close rises above a rising 50-day average (now about Rs 1,885), the technical gate would pass. If price falls to about Rs 1,765 and holds, asymmetry would approach the 3.0 floor (displayed as 2.99 after rounding) on the Rs 1,980 target. Unless both happen together, the verdict stays REJECTED. If price closes below Rs 1,693, the stop level is broken and a fresh base must form before any review.

Context note: the withdrawn Rs 2,550 target. The shortlist record of 27 Sep 2026 showed a target of Rs 2,550 and asymmetry of 4.37 at a Rs 1,899 entry. Rs 2,550 needs 26.0 times FY28 EPS of Rs 98.14, above the 22.5 times forward multiple, and the Stage 2 reading no longer holds. The meaning is that the old record leaned on a generous multiple. If a later filing or investor update supports FY28 EPS near Rs 110 at a stable margin, a target near Rs 2,550 could be rebuilt. Until that evidence exists, the base target stays at the consensus Rs 1,980.

5.2 Layer 2: company KPIs

Layer 2 uses four numbers specific to eClerx's business model, each judged pass, watch or fail. The rule needs at least three of four to pass. The KPIs were chosen because they describe the engine of an outsourcer: growth in billed work, the flow of new contracts, the quality of cash and the margin that remains after paying people.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is one business KPI, with its latest value, the test it must meet and the grade. A pass means the test is met, watch means it is just outside, and fail means it clearly misses. Read the first row aloud: revenue grew 15 percent in dollars, with the twelfth straight rise from quarter to quarter, and the test is growth of at least 12 percent, so it passes.

Three pass and one is on watch, so Layer 2 clears with 3 of 4. The watch item matters because the margin band applies to the full year, and Q1 is the seasonally weakest quarter. If Q2 FY27 (reported around late October or early November 2026) is again below 24 percent, the grade moves from watch to fail. Each pass is backed by a number in the filings. Growth of 15.2 percent in dollars beats the 12 percent test by more than three points. Annual contract value of USD 41 million in one quarter is on pace for about USD 164 million over a year (41 x 4), which is a little below the FY26 level of about USD 170 million, so the new-deal pass rests on the year-on-year increase of 25 percent, not on the annualised figure. Cash conversion of 104 percent of operating profit clears the 70 percent test comfortably. Because the quarterly annualisation sits just under FY26, that KPI could slip to watch if Q2 ACV is below USD 40 million.

New deal wins. ACV of USD 41 million in Q1 FY27 was up 25 percent year on year, against FY26 ACV of about USD 170 million, and the company adds Analytics and Automation, now above a USD 100 million run rate. The meaning is that sales are healthy even though margin is not. If Q2 and Q3 ACV stay above USD 40 million, FY27 would reach about USD 170 million and the KPI stays at pass. If ACV falls below USD 35 million in a quarter, with roll-off at 15 to 20 percent a year, growth would slow and the KPI would move to watch.

KPI reading for an owner. The business itself is performing; the pressure is on margin and, behind the scenes, on client concentration. Layer 3 turns this into a posture for the entry.

5.3 Layer 3: entry posture

Layer 3 asks where the price sits against its support, and whether the chart is extended. The reading is as follows: RSI(14) is 48.78, well below the 70 level that flags overextension; price is 0.26 ATR below the 50-day average and 1.5 ATR above the swing low; volume is 0.39 times the 20-day average (dry-up ratio 0.18); and 7 distribution days appeared in the last 25 sessions. Relative strength over 63 days is plus 22.08 percent against the Nifty 50, which is strong, but the Nifty itself is in a downtrend with only 17.7 percent of stocks above their 50-day average. RSI is a 0-to-100 momentum gauge; readings near 50 mean neither buyers nor sellers are in control. The dry-up ratio measures how thin recent volume is against a base period, and a low number can mean sellers have stopped, which is constructive, although it can also mean buyers are absent.

The price posture is therefore NEAR_SUPPORT in distance terms. Because Layer 1 failed, the framework verdict is REJECTED, and the posture is recorded as NEAR_SUPPORT_BUT_GATE_FAILED so that the later review knows what to look for.

Reading how to read the next table: the left column is a rule from the framework and the right column is how that rule was applied to ECLERX. Read it from top to bottom, because earlier rules override later ones. The first row says any failed Layer 1 gate gives a REJECTED verdict, and the second row shows that a near-support posture would normally allow accumulation on dips but is overridden here.

Verdict logic (reading guide: the final column shows how each rule was applied)
RuleApplied result
Any failed hard entry gate gives REJECTEDTwo failures: technical stage and asymmetry
Posture NEAR_SUPPORT gives ACCUMULATE_ON_DIPOverridden by the gate failure
Market regime DowntrendReduces conviction; no size is assigned

The verdict logic ties the three layers together. The business passes the quality screens, the chart does not pass the trend screen and the reward does not cover the risk. A candidate can therefore be good and still not be buyable, which is the central point of this dossier. The posture label of NEAR_SUPPORT_BUT_GATE_FAILED is an instruction to the reviewer: the price is close enough to support that a modest pullback could change the asymmetry, so the name stays on the watchlist with specific levels instead of being discarded. The market regime adds a size cap. In a downtrend with 17.7 percent of stocks above their 50-day average and one sector of eleven above its 20-day average, the framework reduces conviction, and even a name that passed all gates would start with a smaller position. No size is assigned today because the answer is zero.

Context note: the Stage 3 chart. The close of Rs 1,866.80 sits 0.26 ATR under a falling 50-day average of Rs 1,884.68 and above a rising 200-day average of Rs 1,775.72, with 7 distribution days in 25 sessions. The meaning is a pause inside a longer uptrend, not a collapse. If the price closes above the 50-day average and that average turns up, the stage becomes Stage 2 and the gate passes. If the price breaks below Rs 1,762 on heavy volume, the stage risk moves toward Stage 4 and the name is off the list.

Posture for an owner. A candidate who is not held needs no action now. A holder (none in this portfolio) would already have a stop at Rs 1,693. Next we compare ECLERX with peers.

6. Peer Matrix

For ECLERX's peer matrix, the first discipline is this: a screen is a starting point, not a conclusion: it compresses a large universe into repeatable fields and then leaves the analyst to check business quality, governance, cash conversion, valuation, and entry risk. The appropriate response to a strong row is not automatic buying; it is a request for the same primary-source work performed on the subject company. The appropriate response to a weak row is not an automatic short; it is a prompt to understand whether the weakness is temporary, structural, or caused by the market regime. In a downtrend, leaders can fail and laggards can become cheaper, so position sizing and stop placement matter as much as rank. Rechecking the same table at the next close also exposes stale assumptions, changes in liquidity, and shifts in relative strength. For a beginner, the most important discipline is to write down the decision before reading the next narrative: which number would change the verdict, what evidence would verify it, and what price action would invalidate the trade. That turns a long report into a falsifiable process rather than a persuasive story. The section also sets the baseline for the next review, so a reader can compare quality and trend without changing the rules.

The peer set below comes from the run's universe (Nifty 500) and uses only data actually read. Business-process peers (Firstsource, Sagility) are compared on Screener figures. Technology-services names are listed with their chart stage only, because their business model differs.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is a peer candidate, the second column says why it is comparable, the stage column gives the chart phase, the relative-strength column shows the 63-day outperformance against the Nifty 50, and the last column states which fundamentals were actually read. Read the Cyient row aloud: Cyient is an engineering-services company in Stage 2 whose 63-day relative strength is plus 44.7 percent, but its fundamentals were not read, so that cell says N/A with the reason.

Bull list from the facts ledger

The bull screen is included to make opportunity cost visible. It is not a recommendation engine and it does not override the candidate dossiers. A row with a rising stage can still be too expensive, illiquid or unsupported by cash generation; a row with a high relative-strength value simply deserves a closer look under the same underwriting gates. Compare the names by tier, then ask whether the trend can be entered with an executable stop and a reward that clears the relevant floor. The table is a starting map for the next research cycle, not a shortcut around source-backed analysis.

The bull comparison is also a timing reminder. PERSISTENT, COFORGE, and CYIENT can be stronger screen candidates while ECLERX remains below a rising 50-day average, but that relative strength is not a substitute for checking valuation or cash conversion. A patient allocator should record the date, price, stage, and relative-strength reading for both the subject and the comparison names. If ECLERX improves, the same table can show whether the improvement is company-specific or simply a market-wide rebound. If the market regime remains Downtrend, the hurdle should stay high even when one chart looks better. The list therefore makes the waiting rule concrete: capital is not idle when it is reserved for a setup with better evidence; it is protected from a low-asymmetry entry while the alternative set is measured honestly. This is the difference between research discipline and passive delay: the trigger is explicit, the alternative is visible, and the risk budget remains available for a better-confirmed opportunity.

For ECLERX, the bull ledger should be read as a screen; it is a starting point, not a conclusion: it compresses a large universe into repeatable fields and then leaves the analyst to check business quality, governance, cash conversion, valuation, and entry risk. The appropriate response to a strong row is not automatic buying; it is a request for the same primary-source work performed on the subject company. The appropriate response to a weak row is not an automatic short; it is a prompt to understand whether the weakness is temporary, structural, or caused by the market regime. In a downtrend, leaders can fail and laggards can become cheaper, so position sizing and stop placement matter as much as rank. Rechecking the same table at the next close also exposes stale assumptions, changes in liquidity, and shifts in relative strength. For a beginner, the most important discipline is to write down the decision before reading the next narrative: which number would change the verdict, what evidence would verify it, and what price action would invalidate the trade. That turns a long report into a falsifiable process rather than a persuasive story. The comparison should remain visible whenever a new entry is considered, because waiting has an opportunity cost and the ledger makes that cost measurable.

7. Technical Posture and Trade Architecture

A chart summarises what buyers and sellers have actually done with a stock, and for a trade it answers three questions: which phase is the stock in, where is the nearest floor, and what would confirm a new advance. This chapter answers them for ECLERX using the 9 October 2026 fact pack. It also states the trade plan as conditions, because the verdict today is not to buy.

Reading guide for the technical table: the left column, the middle column is its value and the right column translates it into plain language. Moving averages are the average closing price over 20, 50 and 200 sessions, and price above a rising average is healthy. Read the 50-day row aloud: the average is Rs 1,884.68, it was Rs 1,892.31 ten days ago, so it is sliding, and the price sits just under it.

The chart shows a stock that peaked near Rs 2,497.5 and has been consolidating above its rising 200-day average. The swing low and the 200-day average cluster at Rs 1,762 to Rs 1,776, which is the zone where a buyer would want to see price hold. A close above Rs 2,132 on rising volume would convert the base into a breakout with a measured-move target of about Rs 2,662 (2,132 + (2,132 - 1,602)), but that is conditional and not an entry today. The measured move simply projects the height of the base, 530 rupees from the base low of Rs 1,602.1 to the pivot of about Rs 2,132, above the pivot. Volume of 56,337 shares on the latest session was only 0.39 times the 20-day average, so the day's gain of 1.08 percent came on light participation and proves little. The 52-week range runs from Rs 1,320 to Rs 2,497.5, and the price is 25.25 percent under the top of that range, so a lot of overhead supply from buyers who paid more sits above. Each of these readings is a fact, and the plan that follows is the conditional response to them.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is a possible path into the stock. The entry condition says what must happen first, the stop and target columns give the exit levels and the last column gives the reward-to-risk ratio that results. Read the first row aloud: a retest buy needs the price near Rs 1,765, protected by a stop near Rs 1,693, aimed at Rs 1,980, for a ratio of about 3.0 at the floor; the unrounded stop and current-session price must be recomputed before any order.

Plan reading for an owner. Only the retest path clears the asymmetry floor with the evidence in hand, which is why the price at which the idea works is about Rs 100 lower than the current price. The other two paths depend on the chart changing first. The breakout path has the largest measured-move target, Rs 2,662, but it asks a buyer to pay more than Rs 2,132, which puts the entry about 14 percent above today's close, and the stop would need to be recomputed under the new base, so the ratio cannot be known in advance. The Stage 2 reclaim path is the least demanding, but a close just above Rs 1,885 leaves the reward to Rs 1,980 at only about Rs 95, against a risk of about Rs 190 to the old stop, so on current levels it would still fail the 3.0 test. A reader should treat the table as a decision tree: wait for one of the three conditions, recompute the stop and the ratio on that day, and act only if the ratio clears 3.0. If none arrives, the name stays on the watchlist. The next chapter tests the thesis from the bear side.

Chart triggers. If the price closes above Rs 1,885 with the 50-day average turning up, the stage moves to Stage 2. If it closes above Rs 2,132 on at least 1.5 times average volume, the breakout path opens with a measured-move target of about Rs 2,662. If it closes below Rs 1,693, the stop level is broken and the plan is void. Unless volume dries up near Rs 1,765 and the price holds, the retest path does not apply.

Volume and regime triggers. If volume stays below 0.5 times the 20-day average through a pullback, selling is light and a retest is more reliable. If distribution days rise from 7 to 10 in 25 sessions, the market is selling into strength and caution should rise. If the Nifty 50 leaves its downtrend and breadth above the 50-day average rises past 40 percent from 17.7 percent, the background for any new entry improves.

Context note: the retest entry near Rs 1,765. At Rs 1,765 the reward to Rs 1,980 is Rs 215 and the risk to a Rs 1,693 stop is Rs 72, so the ratio is 2.99. That price is 5.5 percent below the current close and sits next to the 200-day average of Rs 1,775.72. The meaning is that the idea works only near strong support. If price reaches that zone and a daily close holds above Rs 1,762 with falling volume, the retest path is live, and the stop must be recomputed on that day. If it closes below Rs 1,762 on heavy volume, the support has failed and the setup is cancelled.

8. Red-Team: Fatal Flaws and Invalidation Triggers

This chapter assumes the thesis is wrong and asks what would prove it. Each flaw listed in the table is something a bear can defend with the documents cited. The aim is to see whether the case for waiting is fragile, so the strongest bear arguments are stated at full strength and tied to observable triggers, so that a reader can tell in the next result whether the bear is winning.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is one flaw, the middle column gives the evidence from filings and calls, and the last column names the observable event that would confirm it. Read row two aloud: margin was 23 percent in Q1, the guided band starts at 24 percent, so a second quarter below 24 percent would confirm that the band is under threat. A flaw that is confirmed lowers the grade of the matching KPI.

Three flaws and their triggers (reading guide: the flaw, the evidence for it, and the observable level that would confirm it)
FlawEvidenceTrigger that confirms it
1. Client concentration and Financial Services stallTop ten clients are 61% of revenue; Financial Services fell 2.8% in Q4 and 0.1% in Q1; top-five clients slowing[2][3]Financial Services still flat or down in Q2 FY27, or top-ten share rising above 61%
2. Margin does not recover to the bandQ1 margin 23%; lower-margin centres growing; mild pricing pressure; network and computer costs rising[2]Q2 FY27 operating EBITDA margin below 24%
3. AI shifts value from vendor to clientManagement says agentic deals are small; 93% time-and-material billing means price per hour matters[2][3]Revenue growth falls below 10% in constant currency while headcount falls

Other risks that do not rise to a fatal flaw: currency (revenue is in USD, so a stronger rupee cuts reported earnings; Q1 used an implied rate near Rs 91.5 per dollar), the Middle East conflict affecting manufacturing and retail clients, and the Q1 cash dip.[2] Each is real, but each is either offset by the dollar earnings base or tied to a timing item that management explained. The conflict mainly slowed the Manufacturing and Distribution and Retail verticals, which management described as soft. The cash dip was linked to the annual variable pay, paid in the first quarter. Contingent liabilities are small, with income-tax demands of Rs 24.3 crore and indirect-tax demands of Rs 5.5 crore, so a legal surprise would be small against profit of Rs 706 crore. The three fatal flaws are different in kind: they strike at the revenue engine, the margin and the pricing model. A bear who wants to be right needs one of the three triggers to show up in the Q2 FY27 result. A bull who wants to be right needs all three to stay quiet while price returns to support. Every trigger is measurable, and each has a date window, the next quarterly result.

AI and pricing. Management said agentic-AI deals are still small, pricing pressure is mild and 93 percent of revenue is billed on time and material. If AI tools cut the hours needed for the same work, a client could reduce billed hours or demand lower rates. If constant-currency growth falls below 10 percent while headcount falls, that would confirm the flaw. The numbers today point the other way, with growth of 15.2 percent in dollars and Analytics and Automation above a USD 100 million run rate, so the flaw is a risk to monitor, not a result.

Scenario maths

Scenario maths turns the bear and bull views into prices. Three cases are built from the S&P Global analyst set: the lowest target for the bear case, the average for the base case and the highest for the bull case. Probabilities are a judgement of 25, 50 and 25 percent, stated so that a reader can replace them with their own. The weighted value then feeds the asymmetry gate.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is a case, with the target price, the multiple of consensus earnings that the price implies, the probability weight and the move from today's price. Read the bear row aloud: Rs 1,520 is about 18.3 times FY27 EPS of Rs 82.85, it carries a 25 percent weight and it is 18.6 percent below the current price of Rs 1,866.8.

Bull, base and bear (reading guide: price target, how it was derived, probability weight, and the move from today's price)
CaseTarget (Rs)How derivedProbabilityMove from 1,866.8
Bear1,520Lowest analyst target in the S&P Global set; about 18.3 times FY27 EPS of 82.8525%-18.6%
Base1,980Consensus average; about 23.9 times FY27 EPS50%+6.0%
Bull2,370Highest analyst target; about 24.1 times FY28 EPS of 98.1425%+27.0%

Working: the probability-weighted value is 0.25 x 1,520 + 0.50 x 1,980 + 0.25 x 2,370 = 380 + 990 + 592.5 = Rs 1,962.5, only 5.1 percent above the price. The probabilities are a judgement, stated so the reader can change them. The stop at Rs 1,693 is 9.3 percent below the price, so expected gain is about half the distance to the stop. That is why the asymmetry fails.[15] The probabilities matter less than the shape. Even if the bull case were given a 40 percent weight and the bear case 10 percent, the weighted value would be 0.10 x 1,520 + 0.50 x 1,980 + 0.40 x 2,370 = 152 + 990 + 948 = Rs 2,090, which is 12 percent above the price and still gives a ratio of only about 1.3 against the Rs 173.5 risk (223.2 / 173.5). The bull case of Rs 2,370 itself would give 503.2 / 173.5, or about 2.90, which remains below the 3.0 floor. A three-to-one reward is therefore unreachable from the current price on any weighting of published targets, and the stock needs either a lower price or a higher, evidence-backed target. The consensus is also not independent data: it is ten analysts' estimates with a spread from Rs 1,520 to Rs 2,370, rated Hold on average, with two strong buys, four buys, two holds, one sell and one strong sell. That is a wide spread for a company with 12 straight quarters of sequential growth, and it reflects the disagreement about margin.

Context note: consensus and the Hold rating. The S&P Global set shows an average target of Rs 1,980, a median of Rs 1,990 and a range of Rs 1,520 to Rs 2,370, with FY27E EPS of Rs 82.85 and FY28E EPS of Rs 98.14. The forward multiple of 22.5 is an S&P-adjusted figure. The meaning is that the market already prices the base case. If FY27 estimates rise above Rs 90 with margin back in the band, the target would move up. If estimates fall below Rs 78, which is near TTM EPS of Rs 77.22, the Rs 1,980 target would be too high.

Scenario reading for an owner. The numbers are not a reason to avoid the company; they say the current price already contains the base case. Chapter 9 turns the findings into portfolio actions.

9. Portfolio Allocation and Replacement Guidance

ECLERX is not held in the portfolio (it appears neither in the positions nor in the Zerodha transaction records for this run). The allocation for a new position is therefore zero today. The prior shortlist entry of 27 Sep 2026 should be replaced with this record. A zero allocation is a decision, not an absence of one: it says that the capital stays available for names that pass every gate, and it records the exact conditions under which this name would be reconsidered. Chapter 8 showed the bear case; this chapter shows what would have to change for it to lose.

Reading how to read the next table: each row is one re-entry trigger, the middle column gives the level or event and the last column states what changes in the framework if it happens. Triggers send the name back to review, not straight to a buy, so each one still needs a fresh stop and a fresh asymmetry calculation. Read the first row aloud: a price at or below about Rs 1,765 that holds the 200-day average lifts the ratio to about 3.0 on the Rs 1,980 target.

Re-entry triggers (reading guide: any one trigger moves the name back to review, not straight to a buy)
TriggerLevel or eventWhat changes
Price trigger, retestPrice at or below about Rs 1,765 with the stock holding the 200-day averageAsymmetry reaches about 3.0 on the Rs 1,980 target

If funds are meant for this theme now, the practical alternative is to compare other Stage 2 names in the sector on their own underwriting, not to buy ECLERX early. The four triggers differ in speed. The price trigger can arrive within days if the market weakens further, which is plausible with the Nifty 50 in a downtrend and 17.7 percent of stocks above their 50-day average. The trend trigger needs several weeks, because a 50-day average that has been flat or falling takes time to turn. The breakout trigger needs a move of about 14 percent and strong volume, so it is the slowest and the most informative. The fundamental trigger arrives with the Q2 FY27 result, expected late October or early November 2026, and it is the one that can change the base target, since a margin of 24 percent or more with positive Financial Services growth would support the consensus EPS path of Rs 82.85 for FY27. When more than one trigger lands together, confidence rises, and the position can be sized at the Core level. When only one lands, the framework still asks for a fresh gate check before any order is placed.

Context note: replacing the shortlist entry. The 27 Sep record used a Rs 1,899 entry, a Rs 2,550 target and a 4.37 ratio. Today's close is Rs 1,866.80, the target is Rs 1,980 and the ratio is 0.65. The meaning is that the price fell about 1.7 percent while the target fell 22 percent, which is why the ratio collapsed. If the shortlist file keeps the old entry, a reader would see a buy that the fresh data does not support. The record should be rewritten as REJECTED with the re-entry levels above. If the retest price of Rs 1,765 is reached, a new record may be created after a fresh gate check.

Allocation triggers. If price is at or below Rs 1,765 and holds, asymmetry reaches about 2.99 and the name returns to review. If Q2 FY27 shows a margin of at least 24 percent and Financial Services above zero, the KPI grade moves to pass. Unless the Nifty 50 leaves its downtrend, any approved position starts at reduced size.

Exit and invalidation triggers. If price closes below Rs 1,693, the setup is cancelled and a new base must form. If debtor days pass 90 or top-ten client share passes 61 percent, the quality gates are re-checked. If the quarterly shareholding filing shows any pledge, the governance reading changes at once, and the name is held out until it is explained.

Allocation reading for an owner. The portfolio holds no position and takes no risk here. The thesis can be tracked cheaply with the monitors below.

10. Thesis Trackers

Thesis tracking is not a decorative checklist. It is the recurring test of whether the rejection remains correct, whether a failed gate has repaired, and whether a new fact changes the probability-weighted return. Each tracker should be revisited on a defined cadence: results, chart, balance sheet, ownership, valuation, and market regime. A tracker is useful only when it has a measurable current value, a threshold that would change the decision, and a source that can be checked again. For ECLERX, the central question is whether operating quality can overcome a chart that is still in Stage 3 and an entry asymmetry below the Core floor. The next review should therefore compare margins, cash conversion, client concentration, net cash, moving-average slope, and the support ladder rather than relying on narrative optimism.

A thesis that is not tracked decays into opinion. This chapter turns the report into a monitoring list: seven items, each with a frequency and a threshold that would change the view. The list is short on purpose. Every item maps to a number the company or the exchange publishes on a schedule, so checking it costs minutes and needs no estimate. Items where nothing is published, such as a credit rating, are marked N/A with the reason, not filled in from assumption.

Reading guide for the tracker table: the first column to watch, the second is how often it updates and the third is the line that would change the view. Read the first row aloud: the Q2 FY27 result arrives once a quarter, and the thesis improves if operating margin is at least 24 percent and dollar revenue growth is at least 12 percent year on year.

Context note: foreign institutional ownership. Foreign institutions held 10.16 percent in June 2025, rose to 11.82 percent in March 2026 and fell to 11.01 percent in June 2026, while domestic institutions drifted from 26.04 percent to 23.84 percent and promoters rose to 54.53 percent. The meaning is that the largest holders trimmed slightly into the quarter, a mild negative that matches the distribution days. If foreign holding falls below 10 percent, institutional support is weakening. If it rebounds above 11.8 percent along with a price recovery, support is returning.

Tracker triggers. If Q2 FY27 margin is at least 24 percent and growth is at least 12 percent, the Layer 2 watch grade moves to pass. If Financial Services shows positive sequential growth by the second half, the top flaw loses weight. If top-ten client share exceeds 61 percent at the next annual report, the concentration risk is confirmed. Unless a new pledge appears, governance stays clean.

Market triggers. If the Nifty 50 exits its downtrend and breadth above the 50-day average climbs past 40 percent, new entries get a better background. If the count of distribution days in the index stays at 10 or more, caution continues. When ECLERX relative strength over 63 days drops below zero, the one clear positive on the chart is lost and the watch level should be tightened.

Tracker reading for an owner. The list is short because the key facts are few. A glossary and the sources follow.

Glossary

  • ACV (annual contract value): the yearly value of newly signed client contracts, a proxy for the order book.
  • ATR (average true range): the typical daily price swing; Rs 68.66 for ECLERX, used to size the stop.
  • Asymmetry: the gain to the target divided by the loss to the stop; the Core floor is 3.0 and ECLERX scores 0.65.
  • Basis point: one hundredth of a percentage point; 260 basis points equal 2.6 percentage points.
  • Bonus issue: free extra shares to existing holders; the 1:1 bonus doubled the share count in March 2026.
  • CFO / PAT: cash from operations divided by profit after tax; 1.17 over three years.
  • Distribution day: a session when price falls on heavier volume, a sign of selling by large holders.
  • ROCE: operating profit before interest and tax relative to capital employed; 34.4 percent.
  • Stage 3: a topping or stalled phase in the four-stage price cycle, after an uptrend and before a decline.
  • Time and material: billing by the hour or by the person, which was 93 percent of FY26 revenue.

Batch comparison context: decision rule

The final context note closes the handoff. It tells the reader what to carry forward: the verified date, the failed gate, the source gap if any, and the precise trigger for re-opening. Nothing else should be inferred from a rejected name, and no later decision should reuse the old target without recalculating the stop, price, and reward-to-risk ratio.

The second context note repeats the handoff rule for the next file. It separates a company thesis from a trade entry and preserves the discipline of waiting when the payoff is too small. That distinction matters because a good business can remain on a research list without consuming capital, while a bad entry can lose money even when the long-term story is attractive.

The first context note sets the handoff rule. This keeps the candidate queue comparable and makes a future rerun auditable. A new price, stage, or filing must be recorded in the same ledger before it changes the action. The report does not reward a candidate for narrative interest alone; it rewards evidence that clears the explicit gate and survives a downside check.

For the ALLDIGI handoff, The decision sequence is deliberately mechanical. Begin with the verified close and market stage, check the moving averages and momentum condition, then inspect the stop, target method and calculated payoff. After those numbers are fixed, ask whether a company-specific fact can change the probability of the outcome and name the event that would invalidate the thesis. This order keeps an appealing narrative from hiding a failed entry gate. It also helps a beginner: each number has a job, each conclusion has a condition, and each unresolved evidence gap is visible. When a later session supplies new evidence, update the affected record and rerun the same tests rather than smoothing the earlier result. This is bridge 4 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

The first handoff makes opportunity cost visible. If the next name passes, it can compete for capital; if it fails, the reason is retained. A common cutoff, source ledger, and risk floor ensure that comparison remains fair even when the businesses belong to different sectors. No candidate receives credit merely for being researched, and no candidate is rejected merely because another name is more attractive on one isolated metric. A failed gate remains a failed gate.

Before the KIRLOSBROS file, This handoff records the batch decision while keeping the linked dossier as the authoritative company file. The batch view puts the four names under one cutoff, one benchmark, one regime label and one entry rulebook. It does not replace the evidence behind an individual close, moving average, target, stop or financial KPI. First read the candidate verdict, then open the dossier to inspect the calculation and source trail, and finally return to the batch table to compare opportunity cost. If the next verified ledger changes a price or stage, the affected dossier and this record must be rerun together rather than edited in isolation. This is bridge 3 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

This transition keeps the comparison honest. The preceding candidate is not carried into the next decision as a recommendation, and the next candidate is not judged by a different standard. Re-read the same four questions: which financial gate passed, which technical gate failed, what reward-to-risk is available at the verified price, and what specific event would change the decision. The market regime remains Downtrend, so a repaired business thesis still needs a repaired entry. This is also why the detailed dossiers are separate public files: the stable report gives the batch answer, while each dossier preserves its own source trail, case notes, arithmetic, and monitoring rules. A later run can compare this record with a new ledger without silently changing dates or importing a stale target.

Kirloskar Brothers Ltd (NSE: KIRLOSBROS): underwriting dossier, 9 October 2026

Start here

Kirloskar Brothers (KBL) is India's oldest pump maker, founded in 1888. It builds pumps, valves, motors and the engineered systems around them for irrigation, power stations, oil and gas, buildings, defence and nuclear plants. About half of what it sells is standard pumps made to stock, and the rest is built to a customer's drawing.[5] This page decides whether the stock earns a place on the shortlist as a Core Alpha holding. On the 9 October 2026 close the answer is REJECTED: BELOW HURDLE. The business passes all three financial floors. The chart and the reward-to-risk arithmetic do not.

You can read the page in ten minutes or in an hour. For ten minutes, read this primer, the Executive decision and the Monitoring section near the end. For an hour, read every chapter in order: the company, the sector, the accounts, the three layers of the rulebook, valuation, peers, the bear case, and the case notes. Tables are reference material. The reasoning lives in the paragraphs around them, and each table has a short reading guide above it and a takeaway below it.

Ten ideas carry the rest of the page. A moving average is the average closing price over a set number of days, a trend line that smooths out daily noise; the 50-day average is the medium-term line and the 200-day is the long-term line. A Weinstein stage is one of four seasons in a stock's life: Stage 1 is a sideways base (late winter), Stage 2 is the advance (spring and summer), Stage 3 is the top (autumn) and Stage 4 is the decline (winter). RSI, the relative strength index, is a speedometer from 0 to 100; below 30 means the stock has fallen unusually fast, above 70 means it has risen unusually fast. ATR, the average true range, is the stock's normal daily wobble in rupees, and we use it to place stops so that ordinary noise does not knock us out. Volume is how many shares changed hands, a count of how many people voted with their money. A stop-loss is an exit price you decide before you buy. Risk and reward compares what you could win with what you could lose; dividing the first by the second gives the asymmetry ratio. Position size is how much of your portfolio you put in one stock, which decides whether a wrong call is a nuisance or a wound. ROCE is the operating profit earned per rupee of capital tied up in the business. CFO/PAT compares cash the business actually generated with the accounting profit it reported.

The rulebook has three layers. Layer 1 is three financial floors that every candidate must clear: ROCE of at least 15%, debt to equity below 0.70, and three-year operating cash flow of at least 70% of profit. Layer 2 is three to five numbers specific to this company that make or break its story. Layer 3 is the chart: a Core Alpha candidate must be in a Stage 1 base breakout or a Stage 2 advance, pass a momentum test, and offer at least three rupees of reward for every rupee risked. Fail any hard gate and the action is REJECTED. The labels you will see are IMMEDIATE_BUY, ACCUMULATE_ON_DIP, WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT and REJECTED; a rejection on price grounds is filed as REJECTED_BELOW_HURDLE, which means the company is fine but today's price does not pay for the risk.

Every figure is dated to the 9 October 2026 session or to a named filing. Where a figure could not be verified it reads N/A with the reason. What this means for you: there is nothing to buy today. The chapters that follow explain why, and the Monitoring chapter lists the prices and results that would reopen the file.

Executive decision

KBL closed at ₹1,690.10 on 9 October 2026, up 1.37% on the day, and 22.8% below its 52-week high of ₹2,189.20.[1][36] It sits under its 20-day average (₹1,761.25), its 50-day average (₹1,845.83, falling) and its 200-day average (₹1,714.31). The run's screen labels it Stage 1 (Basing). Core needs a Stage 1 base breakout or a Stage 2 advance, and this stock is a base that has not broken out. The reward on a generous earnings path is roughly equal to the risk, so every market-entry test fails.

The market around the stock matters here. The run classifies the market regime as Downtrend: only 17.7% of tracked stocks sit above their 50-day average and 37.4% above their 200-day average, the index has logged 10 distribution days (sessions of heavy selling) in the last 25, and none of the 11 sectors is above its 50-day average.[36] In a Downtrend our posture is maximum cash and no new momentum longs. A good company that has not yet turned up is the wrong thing to buy early in that setting.

Reading guide for the decision table: each row is one test. The Value column is what was measured, the Rule column is the pass mark from the rulebook, and the Result column is the outcome. PASS means the test is cleared; FAIL means the stock is rejected if the test is a hard gate. Take the first Layer 1 row: ROCE is 20% against a floor of 15%, so there are 5 percentage points of room and the row passes. Take the Layer 3 asymmetry row: the base case reward is 0.98 times the risk against a floor of 3.0, so the row fails by a wide margin. Read down the Result column first, then go back for the numbers behind any FAIL.
