The table shows a split verdict, and the split is the point. All three financial rows pass with room to spare: ROCE 20% against 15%, debt to equity 0.10 against 0.70, cash conversion 0.95 against 0.70. All three market rows fail: the stock is in a base that has not lifted, the momentum test misses on both price and slope, and the base-case reward of ₹107 against a risk of ₹109 is nowhere near 3 to 1. The conflict resolves in favour of the chart because the rulebook is a buying rule, not a quality rating. Quality intact, timing unconfirmed, price not paying for the risk. The numbers to watch next are the ₹1,714 line (the 200-day average) and the ₹1,640 floor of the base. A close above the first starts to repair the picture; a close below the second turns a base into a breakdown.

The earlier file on this name (27 September 2026) said ACCUMULATE_ON_DIP. That note cited a cash conversion cycle of about minus 1 day and a debt-to-equity of 0.08. Screener now shows a cash conversion cycle of 68 days for FY26, against 83 and 82 days in FY24 and FY25, so the earlier working-capital story was stale.[1] This dossier replaces it, and it reaches a different answer because the price, the earnings trend and the working-capital reading have all moved since that note was written.

What would change this view

  • If KBL closes above ₹1,714 (the 200-day average) and the 50-day average stops falling from ₹1,846, the momentum gate can pass and the file is reopened for a full re-score.
  • If the stock closes above the 28 September intraday high of ₹1,936 on volume well above its 20-day average (today it is 0.51 times), the base has broken out and the technical gate can pass.
  • If KBL closes below ₹1,640, the base has failed, the stage turns to 4 and the name stays rejected with no reopening until a new base forms.
  • If the Q2 FY27 results (expected in early to mid November 2026) show the overseas margin at 9% or better and a consolidated margin of 13% or better, the Layer 2 evidence improves and the asymmetry arithmetic gets a higher earnings number to work with.

For an owner of the stock rather than a buyer, the same facts say something different. A holder with a long horizon is not forced out by a Stage 1 base, because the stop for a holder is the base low and the company is financially sound. For a new buyer deploying fresh money under our rulebook, the answer is to wait. The company's own history, covered next, explains why the business keeps recovering from pauses like this one, and also why those recoveries have been uneven.

Company history, ownership and governance

The Kirloskar family business began in 1888 and KBL was incorporated in 1920. Its first indigenous centrifugal pump came in 1926, at a time when India imported almost all of its pumping equipment.[5][11] A centrifugal pump moves liquid by spinning it outward inside a casing, and it is the workhorse of water supply, irrigation and industry. Owning that product line for a century gives the company a brand, a dealer network and a service business on top of an installed base, which is why spare parts and service appear as their own line in the order book.

The growth in scale came by acquisition. SPP Pumps in the United Kingdom was bought in 2003 and SPP in the United States in 2004. The Dutch group KBI B.V. followed in 2007, the same year as the Kolhapur Steel castings business, and Rodelta in the Netherlands in 2015.[11][13] These purchases explain why about 36% of FY26 revenue (₹1,628 Cr of ₹4,538 Cr) now comes from the international subsidiaries, and why those subsidiaries earn lower margins than the Indian parent.[13] The group operates ten plants in India and seven overseas, so it carries the fixed cost of a global footprint on a mid-sized revenue base.

Promoters hold 65.95% and that figure has not moved for years. Foreign investors hold 6.41%, domestic institutions 10.23% and the public 17.40% across 64,065 holders (June 2026).[1][3] About a third of the shares are therefore free to trade, which is thin for a company worth ₹13,421 Cr. A thin float means price moves on small volume; the session of 28 September 2026 saw about 457,358 shares trade and an intraday high of ₹1,936.10.[36] The rulebook treats such a session as a signal only when the volume repeats on the way up, and it has not.

The statutory auditor is Sharp & Tannan Associates, with an unmodified opinion on the FY26 accounts. The consolidated audit report does note exceptions in the audit-trail (edit-log) feature of the accounting software at the holding company, two subsidiaries and a joint venture, which is a control point to watch rather than a restatement.[7] The key audit matter is revenue recognition on long-term contracts, about ₹686 Mn in FY26, because revenue on those contracts is booked as work progresses and depends on management's estimate of completion.[7] A promoter share pledge is N/A: none is disclosed in the annual report or on Screener, but the quarterly BSE shareholding pattern was not read in full, so it is recorded as not verified rather than as zero.

Recent governance filings are routine. A postal ballot notice of 15 September 2026 proposes an independent director, Dr. Madhuri Kanitkar; a Regulation 74(5) certificate was filed on 10 October 2026; and the trading window closed on 26 September 2026 ahead of the Q2 results.[27][28][29] One inconsistency deserves a flag: page 29 of the annual report describes an "AA+" rating from CRISIL, while the CRISIL rating rationale itself says AA with a Positive outlook. The rating document is the primary source, so AA/Positive is used throughout.[7][24]

What would change this view

  • If the BSE shareholding pattern for the September 2026 quarter shows any promoter pledge, the governance reading drops from clean to cautious and the position cap in any future file is cut.
  • If promoter holding falls below 60% from 65.95%, treat it as a signal to re-read the filings, because a stake that stable for years rarely moves without a reason.
  • If the auditor's report for FY27 repeats the audit-trail exceptions at the same entities, upgrade the concern from a note to a control-quality finding.

Ownership and governance are not the obstacle to this stock. What the business earns, and from where, is the next question.

What this means for you: a stable family-controlled owner with no pledge disclosed is a positive. The thin float is a risk to your exit, not to the thesis, so any future position should be sized with the knowledge that it could take several sessions to build or sell without moving the price.

Operations and revenue model

KBL makes money in four ways. It sells catalogue pumps through dealers (made to stock, about 50% of FY26 revenue), builds pumps to a customer's specification (made to order, 27%), engineers large custom pumps and systems (engineered to order, 19%), and runs turnkey projects (3%).[15] Made-to-stock earns a steady margin and turns over quickly because inventory is sold from shelves. Engineered pumps and projects are lumpy, but they sit in a written order book that gives visibility months ahead. The ratio between the two explains why the company's standalone order book is a poor measure of half the business.

Reading guide for the segment table: every row is a slice of the Q1 FY27 quarter (April to June 2026), with figures in ₹ million. Revenue is sales, EBITDA is operating profit before depreciation, and margin is EBITDA divided by revenue. A good reading is a rising margin on rising revenue; a bad one is margin falling while revenue rises, because it means growth is being bought with lower prices or higher costs. Take the overseas row: revenue rose 19% to 4,018, but EBITDA was 207 on a 5.2% margin against 7.0% a year earlier, so more sales brought less profit per rupee of sales.

Segment, Q1 FY27 (₹ Mn)RevenueEBITDAMarginRead-across
Standalone (India)6,738 (+9%)920 (+16%)13.7% vs 12.8%Healthy: price rises of about 10% are sticking
Overseas group (KBI B.V.)4,018 (+19%)2075.2% vs 7.0%Growing, but margin fell
Consolidated11,049 (+12.9%)1,306 (+2.4%)11.8% vs 13.0%Profit flat on revenue up 13%
Consolidated PAT676 (+0.1%)EPS ₹8.46.1% of revenueNo growth in profit

The two businesses inside KBL are telling different stories, and the consolidated line hides it. The Indian parent grew revenue 9%, grew EBITDA 16% and lifted its margin by 90 basis points to 13.7%. The overseas group grew revenue twice as fast, at 19%, but its margin fell 180 basis points to 5.2%. The sum is a consolidated margin of 11.8% (down from 13.0%) and profit after tax of ₹676 Mn that is flat on a year ago. For a buyer the lesson is that India is not the problem; the weight of lower-margin overseas revenue is dragging the group average. The number to watch in the November results is the overseas margin: if it holds near 5%, consolidated profit cannot grow even if India keeps improving.[12][13]

The orders picture supports the revenue line. Q1 orders were ₹1,395 Cr (+4%), and the total pending order book is ₹4,062 Cr against ₹3,345 Cr a year earlier, an increase of 21%. Standalone, the domestic order book excluding small pumps is ₹2,375 Cr, and about two thirds of it is executable in FY27. Management says it will "strive for" double-digit revenue growth in FY27,.[12] A guidance phrase like "strive for" is softer than "expect", so the base-case earnings estimate later in this dossier uses a 10% growth path but does not treat it as a commitment.

Three growth threads stand out. In nuclear, Q1 brought primary circuit pump orders of about ₹70 Cr and secondary circuit orders of about ₹40 Cr; the large fleet-wide primary pump award is still pending a tender after metallurgy trials in Europe and India.[12] In oil and gas, petrol pump orders from three or four large public-sector companies total about ₹217 Cr. In the United States, SPP USA grew revenue 29% to ₹1,598 Mn at a 16.0% EBITDA margin, with data-centre cooling work about 23% of its sales and a multi-year framework contract with a major operator described as in final stages. A hyperscale data-centre package is worth USD 7.5 to 10 million.[12][13] These threads are real but they were not yet in reported profit in Q1.

The soft spots are in Europe and in the parts business. SPP UK posted an EBITDA of minus ₹9 Mn in Q1. The Dutch group's EBITDA was minus ₹57 Mn on Rodelta delays. The motors, stampings and castings subsidiary (KPML, which includes TKSL) fell to a 7.3% margin from 12.5%, while the castings subsidiary KCPL held 21.9%.[12] FY26 as a whole had a similar shape: consolidated EBITDA margin dropped to 13.7% from 15.2% on the company's own measure, KBI B.V. margin fell to 9.2% from 12.3%, and SPP UK's to 7.4% from 12.3%.[14][15]

The pending order book by sector shows where the standalone business is concentrated: irrigation and water resource management (₹1,013 Cr) is the largest block, followed by power (₹599 Cr), building and construction (₹233 Cr), oil and gas (₹178 Cr), industry (₹150 Cr), customer support (₹104 Cr), marine and defence (₹56 Cr) and valves (₹43 Cr).[12] Irrigation is a government-paced customer with long approval and payment cycles, which links directly to the rise in receivables that the accounts chapter will document.

What would change this view

  • If the November 2026 results show the overseas group's EBITDA margin at or above 9%, up from 5.2%, the group margin drag is reversing and consolidated profit can grow again.
  • If the multi-year SPP USA data-centre framework contract is announced, US revenue growth above 29% becomes credible and the base-case EPS of ₹51.8 gains support.
  • If the primary circuit nuclear pump tender is awarded to KBL, the order book gains a large, high-margin line; if it is awarded elsewhere, remove nuclear from the growth case.

Demand for these products comes from public and private capital spending, so the sector chapter looks at what those customers are planning.

What this means for you: the Indian engine is working and the overseas engine is the swing factor. Profit grows again only if Europe stops losing margin while the US data-centre business scales, and neither event had shown up in reported numbers by the June 2026 quarter.

Sector dynamics

Pump demand follows four public and private spending cycles: irrigation and water supply (government budgets), power (thermal, nuclear and pumped-storage capital spending), oil and gas, and buildings. KBL's standalone order book is led by irrigation at ₹1,013 Cr, a government-paced business with long approval cycles. Power follows at ₹599 Cr, then buildings at ₹233 Cr.[12] A company with this mix does not sell to one cycle. When irrigation budgets slow, power and oil and gas orders can fill the gap; when power slows, the reverse can happen. That spread is the main reason the order book has been stable even when reported profit has not.

The rating agencies publish the clearest outside view of where the sector is expected to go. CRISIL expects revenue growth of 10 to 12% a year, EBITDA margins of 13 to 14% and capital spending of ₹150 to 200 Cr a year, with cash accruals above ₹500 Cr.[24] CARE's December 2025 rationale looked backward and found a revenue CAGR of about 13% and an EBITDA CAGR of 24% for FY21 to FY25.[21] Both documents describe a company in an upcycle with a wide margin of safety on debt service. The question for an equity buyer is different: whether profit keeps compounding from here, because the price already reflects a 33.2 times multiple on trailing earnings.

Reading guide for the expectations table: the first column lists a measure, the second what the rating agencies expected or reported, the third what KBL delivered in the latest period, and the fourth the gap. A positive gap means KBL beat the outside view; a negative gap means it fell short. Take the margin row: CRISIL expected 13 to 14%, while Screener shows consolidated operating margin of 12% for FY26, so the gap is about one percentage point below the bottom of the range. Read the gap column first and then ask whether the shortfall is a timing issue or a structural one.

MeasureAgency viewKBL deliveredGap
Revenue growth per yearCRISIL: 10 to 12%FY26: +1.0%; Q1 FY27: +12.9%Missed in FY26, met in Q1
EBITDA marginCRISIL: 13 to 14%FY26: 12% (Screener); Q1 FY27: 11.8%About 1 to 2 points below
Capital spending per yearCRISIL: ₹150 to 200 CrFY26 purchases of plant: ₹135 Cr (₹1,353 Mn)Below the range
Gearing (CARE)Negative trigger above 0.50x0.11xLarge headroom
EBITDA margin trigger (CARE)Negative trigger below 10% sustained11.8% in Q1 FY271.8 points of headroom

The table shows a company that is financially far from any trouble and operationally a little behind the outside forecast. Debt is a non-issue: gearing of 0.11x against a trigger of 0.50x leaves room to borrow four times as much before the agencies would worry. The earnings side is where the gap sits. Revenue growth of 1% in FY26 was far below the 10 to 12% expected, and the margin of 12% was below the 13 to 14% range, so the profit forecast was missed in the same year it was made. The Q1 FY27 revenue rebound to 12.9% shows the sales engine is working again, but margin of 11.8% shows the conversion into profit is not. A reader should treat the 10% margin trigger as the tripwire: one more weak quarter at the overseas group would put the margin within a point of it. The number to watch is the November 2026 consolidated margin, which needs to move toward 13%.[12][21][24]

The sector tailwind is real in power, nuclear and data centres, but it had not reached the reported margin by Q1 FY27. Pending orders are up 21% year on year to ₹4,062 Cr, which is the strongest evidence that demand is intact, and about two thirds of the domestic non-small-pump book is executable within FY27. Price increases of about 10% have been taken on the Indian business, which fits the 90-basis-point rise in the standalone margin. The listed pump and compressor peers are all priced at higher earnings multiples than KBL, which says the market treats this group as a long-duration capital-spending theme rather than a cheap cyclical.[1][2]

Competition matters too. In pumps, KBL competes with KSB, WPIL, Shakti Pumps and global names, and in the data-centre and industrial cooling niche in the United States it competes through SPP USA. The peer chapter later in this dossier compares the listed names on price, multiple and return on capital. For the sector view, the point is that none of the listed peers is priced cheaply, so a decline in KBL cannot be blamed on a sector de-rating alone: the stock is down 13% over one year while the run's market regime is itself in a Downtrend, and the relative strength reading of −5.03% over 63 sessions shows the stock has lagged an already weak index.[36]

What would change this view

  • If CRISIL or CARE moves the outlook from Positive to Stable, the agencies are telling you the profit path is flatter than they assumed; treat that as a reason to lower the base-case EPS growth from 10%.
  • If the pending order book falls below ₹3,500 Cr in the next quarter, from ₹4,062 Cr, the demand evidence weakens and the sector tailwind argument loses its strongest support.
  • If the sector count above the 50-day average in the run's regime check rises from 0 of 11 to 4 or more, the market backdrop stops working against the stock.

With the demand picture set, the accounts show how much of that demand has become cash and profit.

What this means for you: the demand story is not the problem. The problem is that demand is converting into revenue faster than into profit, and the agencies' own forecast has been missed once already.

Financial forensics

The accounts are read from the Screener consolidated page, with the annual reports for the audited statements and the notes.[1][7][8][9][10] The goal is to answer three questions: is the profit real, is the profit turning into cash, and is the balance sheet strong enough to survive a weak year. The first table covers the profit and loss line across six periods, and the second covers cash and working capital across the three years for which Screener publishes the data.

Reading guide for the profit table: each column is a financial year ending in March, with TTM meaning the trailing twelve months to June 2026. Sales is revenue, operating profit is profit before interest, tax and depreciation, and OPM is operating profit divided by sales. A healthy pattern shows sales rising, margin steady or rising, and net profit growing at least as fast as sales. Take FY25 to FY26: sales rose from 4,492 to 4,538 (+1.0%) but net profit fell from 419 to 377 (−10.0%), so profit fell while sales held flat, which is a margin story, not a demand story.

₹ Cr, consolidated, March year-endFY22FY23FY24FY25FY26TTM
Sales3,0583,7304,0014,4924,5384,664
Operating profit206400520609545548
OPM %71113141212
Net profit94236350419377377
ROCE %1122272820See ratio table

The profit table shows a four-year climb and a one-year step back. Net profit rose from ₹94 Cr in FY22 to ₹419 Cr in FY25, a gain of more than four times, while sales rose by about half, which means almost all of the gain came from margin: OPM went from 7% to 14%. FY26 gave back two points of that margin (12%) and about a tenth of the profit (₹377 Cr). The trailing twelve months show sales of ₹4,664 Cr and net profit of ₹377 Cr, so profit has not yet recovered even though sales have kept edging up. Three-year compound sales growth is 7% and five-year is 11%; profit compounds at 19% over three years and 20% over five, but the TTM profit is down 4%. A buyer in a Stage 1 base is paying a 33.2 times multiple for a company whose profit has stopped growing. That is the key point of the forensic view, and the metric to track next is the TTM profit line, which must pass ₹419 Cr to show the margin recovery is real.[1]

Quarterly figures show how the year unfolded. Net profit was ₹68 Cr in June 2025, ₹72 Cr in September 2025, ₹125 Cr in December 2025, ₹112 Cr in March 2026 and ₹68 Cr in June 2026. Sales over the same five quarters were ₹979 Cr, ₹1,028 Cr, ₹1,116 Cr, ₹1,415 Cr and ₹1,105 Cr, with operating margins of 11%, 11%, 13%, 13% and 10%. The December and March quarters are seasonally strong, and June 2026 was a flat comparison with June 2025 on profit even though sales rose 12.9%. The pattern makes the point that the second half of the year carries the earnings, so the Q2 print in November, a seasonally lighter quarter, will say less than the Q3 and Q4 prints that follow.[1]

Reading guide for the cash table: it covers the three years where Screener publishes cash-flow and working-capital data. CFO is cash from operations, FCF is cash left after capital spending, debtor days is how long customers take to pay, and the cash conversion cycle is the days between paying suppliers and collecting from customers. Healthy cash flow tracks profit; if CFO falls below profit for several years, the profit may be sitting in receivables or stock. Take FY26: CFO of ₹334 Cr against profit of ₹377 Cr gives a ratio of 0.89, below the 0.95 three-year average, so the latest year converted less profit into cash than the two before it.

₹ Cr, consolidatedFY24FY25FY26
Cash from operations370386334
Free cash flow259297204
Debtor days484053
Cash conversion cycle (days)838268
CFO / net profit1.060.920.89

Cash tells a more cautious story than profit. FY26 cash from operations fell to ₹334 Cr from ₹386 Cr, and free cash flow fell by about 31%, from ₹297 Cr to ₹204 Cr, because plant purchases rose to ₹1,353 Mn from ₹896 Mn in the annual report while operating cash fell. The conversion ratio has drifted from 1.06 to 0.92 to 0.89, which is still above the 0.70 floor but shows a steady slide in three years. Debtor days jumped from 40 to 53, which is about a third more time to collect from customers. The cash conversion cycle improved to 68 days from 82, which looks good but reflects longer payment terms taken from suppliers as much as faster collection. A reader should treat the receivables jump as the main flag: if debtor days do not return to 50 or less within two quarters, the slide in cash conversion is no longer a one-year event. The lesson is to watch the receivable days, not the profit line, for the next evidence of stress.[7][14]

The annual report fills in the detail. Trade receivables rose 35% to ₹6,627 Mn from ₹4,925 Mn while revenue rose 1%, and other current assets rose to ₹5,622 Mn from ₹3,456 Mn. Supplier advances in the standalone entity rose from ₹32 Cr to ₹91 Cr. Inventories were broadly flat at ₹8,724 Mn against ₹8,537 Mn. Receivables growing 35 times faster than sales (35% against 1%) is the single most important forensic finding in the file, because it means that a portion of reported revenue has not yet been collected. Irrigation and power customers, which are government-linked, pay slowly, so the pattern is plausible and not necessarily a warning. It stays a flag until debtor days fall.[7]

The balance sheet is strong. Equity capital is ₹16 Cr and reserves ₹2,448 Cr, against borrowings of ₹250 Cr and investments of ₹758 Cr. The annual report shows cash of ₹4,010 Mn and current investments of ₹4,489 Mn, against total equity of ₹24,759 Mn, so the group is close to net-debt free. CRISIL puts liquid surplus at ₹703 Cr against total debt of ₹133 Cr, and CARE reports adjusted gearing of 0.11x and interest cover of 26.8x. Their downgrade triggers are an EBITDA margin below 10% sustained, or gearing above 0.50x.[21][24] The Q1 FY27 margin of 11.8% is closer to the 10% trigger than it was a year ago. CRISIL rates the long-term bank facilities AA/Positive and short-term A1+ on ₹1,700 Cr of facilities; CARE rates AA/Positive and A1+ with the outlook raised from Stable in December 2025, and the earlier rationales of 2023 to 2025 show a steady upward path.[22][23][25][26]

Per-share figures: TTM earnings per share is ₹47.02 (FY26 ₹47.05), book value is ₹310, return on equity was 17.3% last year and 20% over three years, and the dividend is ₹7 a share (about 15% payout, yield 0.41%). Return on capital fell from 28% to 20% because profit fell while capital employed kept growing with the plant and working-capital build.[1] There is one reconciliation point. Dividing the close of ₹1,690.10 by the TTM EPS of ₹47.02 gives 35.9 times, but Screener shows a P/E of 33.2, which implies earnings per share of about ₹50.90. The gap likely reflects a different earnings base (such as the latest quarter run-rate or an adjusted figure) in Screener's display, and it is recorded as a data discrepancy that was not resolved from the sources read. The asymmetry chapter tests both bases so the verdict does not depend on which is right.

What would change this view

  • If debtor days fall back to 50 or below at the next annual disclosure, from 53, the working-capital flag clears and CFO/PAT can return above 0.95.
  • If FY27 operating cash flow falls below ₹300 Cr, from ₹334 Cr, with receivables still rising, the Layer 1 cash gate stays a pass on the three-year average but the trend becomes a concern.
  • If the TTM net profit rises above ₹419 Cr, the FY25 peak, the profit decline is over and the base-case EPS estimate needs to be raised.

The accounts support a financially safe company, so the three financial gates are next.

What this means for you: the business is clean and almost debt free. It has just entered a phase where profit, cash flow and returns are all a notch lower than a year ago, and the market has not yet decided whether this is a pause or a peak.

Layer 1: the three hard financial gates

Layer 1 asks whether the company is financially sound enough to be considered at all. The three gates are return on capital employed, the debt-to-equity ratio and the three-year ratio of operating cash flow to profit. A failed gate ends the review no matter how attractive the chart looks. These are deliberately blunt tests: they do not predict a good investment, they screen out businesses where the accounts would make any entry price a gamble.

Reading guide for the gate table: the Measured column is KBL's figure, the Floor column is the pass mark, and the Working column shows the sums so you can check them. Take the debt-to-equity row: borrowings of ₹250 Cr divided by equity plus reserves of ₹2,464 Cr (16 + 2,448) gives 0.10 against a ceiling of 0.70, so the stock has seven times the room it needs. A row passes when the measured figure is on the right side of the floor. Read the Result column to see the verdict and the Working column to see why.

GateMeasuredFloorResultWorking
Return on capital employed20%≥ 15%PASSScreener FY26; FY25 was 28%
Debt to equity0.10< 0.70PASS250 / (16 + 2,448) = 0.10
Three-year CFO/PAT0.95x≥ 0.70PASS(370 + 386 + 334) / (350 + 419 + 377) = 1,090 / 1,146

All three gates pass, and none is close to its limit. Return on capital of 20% clears the 15% floor by five points, even after falling eight points from the FY25 peak of 28%. Debt to equity of 0.10 uses one seventh of the allowed ceiling, and the group holds investments of ₹758 Cr against borrowings of ₹250 Cr. Cash conversion of 0.95 over three years clears 0.70 by a quarter. The margin of safety is largest on debt and smallest on cash conversion, where the FY26 single-year figure of 0.89 is the lowest of the three years. The gate status is PASS. The consequence is that the rejection in this file does not come from balance-sheet quality, and any future reopening would not need to revisit these three tests unless the receivables trend worsens. The ratio to watch is CFO/PAT, which would need to fall below 0.80 on a three-year basis before it became a worry.

One definitional note keeps the gates honest. Screener's ROCE is computed on consolidated capital employed, including the overseas group, and the annual reports use a slightly different base, so a reader checking against the filings may get a figure a point or two different. The conclusion does not change: even a ROCE of 17% would pass. Debt to equity uses borrowings only; including lease obligations would raise it, but not close to 0.70. The three-year window runs from FY24 to FY26 because Screener does not publish cash-flow data for FY22 and FY23 in the format used here, so those years are N/A for the cash ratio and are not guessed.[1]

A last check on stability: the closest gate to its floor on a one-year view is cash conversion, where FY26 came in at 0.89 against the 0.70 floor, a margin of 0.19. If FY27 cash from operations matched FY26's ₹334 Cr while profit rose 10% to about ₹415 Cr, the single-year ratio would slip to about 0.80, still a pass but with less room. That is why the receivables trend is carried into the monitoring chapter even though Layer 1 passes today.

The Layer 1 gate status is PASS. The company clears all three floors with room to spare, so the rejection does not come from balance-sheet quality.

What would change this view

  • If ROCE falls below 15% from 20% in FY27, the first Layer 1 gate fails and the file closes regardless of the chart.
  • If borrowings rise above ₹1,700 Cr, the size of the sanctioned bank facilities, debt to equity would approach 0.70 and the gate would be at risk.
  • If three-year CFO/PAT slips below 0.80 from 0.95, the cash gate is near its floor and a forensic re-read of receivables is required.

The gates answer whether the company is sound; the KPIs answer whether the story is working.

What this means for you: if the stock were to set up properly, the balance sheet would not be the obstacle.

Layer 2: company KPIs that decide the thesis

Layer 2 picks the three to five numbers that would make or break this particular business. For KBL they are the standalone margin, the order-book cover, working-capital discipline, the overseas margin and cash conversion. Because the Layer 3 gates fail, these are shown as evidence and are not scored into an allocation. They are chosen because each one connects to a specific way the investment could go wrong: the standalone margin tests pricing power, the order book tests demand, the debtor days tests customer quality, the overseas margin tests the acquisitions, and cash conversion tests whether profit is real.

Reading guide for the KPI table: each row names a number, shows the latest reading, gives a pass threshold and assigns a status. PASS means the reading clears the threshold, WATCH means it is close or the data are ambiguous, and FAIL means it misses. Take the order-book row: standalone pending orders of ₹2,375 Cr divided by FY26 standalone revenue of ₹2,828 Cr gives 0.84x, below a 1.0x threshold, but the book excludes small pumps that are made to stock and carry no backlog, so the ratio understates cover and the status is WATCH, not FAIL.

KPILatest readingThresholdStatus
Standalone EBITDA margin13.7% in Q1 FY27; FY26 15.4% on the company measure≥ 13%PASS
Order book cover (standalone pending orders / FY26 standalone revenue)₹2,375 Cr / ₹2,828 Cr = 0.84x (ex small pumps, which are made to stock)≥ 1.0x for engineered workWATCH: the small-pump half of the business does not carry a backlog, so the ratio understates cover
Working capital discipline (debtor days)53 days in FY26 vs 40 in FY25; receivables +35%≤ 50 days and not risingWATCH
Overseas margin recovery (KBI B.V. EBITDA margin)5.2% in Q1 FY27 vs 7.0%; FY26 9.2% vs 12.3%≥ 9%FAIL
Cash conversion (CFO/PAT)0.89 in FY26; 0.95 three-year≥ 0.90WATCH

One of five KPIs passes, three are on watch and one fails, which is a mixed scorecard for a company with a clean balance sheet. The pass is the Indian business: a standalone margin of 13.7% clears the 13% line and has risen 90 basis points in a year, which shows the parent can raise prices and hold them. The fail is the overseas margin at 5.2%, far under the 9% line, with the Dutch group and SPP UK both losing money at the operating level in the quarter. The watch items are tied together: receivables rising and cash conversion slipping are two views of the same problem, which is that customers are taking longer to pay. The overall lesson is that the Indian engine is fine and the thesis is being tested on whether overseas margin and receivables recover. The two numbers to watch in the November results are the KBI B.V. margin (needs to rise from 5.2% toward 9%) and debtor days (needs to fall from 53 toward 50).[1][12][13][15]

A few more lines of detail help interpret each KPI. The standalone margin of 15.4% on the company's FY26 measure compares with 13.7% in Q1, and the difference is seasonal: the second half of the year carries more high-margin engineered deliveries. The overseas margin has a longer history of weakness than a single quarter: FY26 was 9.2% against 12.3%, so the 5.2% in Q1 is a further step down. Cash conversion measured over three years is 0.95, which passes the Layer 1 floor and sits just above the 0.90 monitoring line used here; the FY26 single-year figure of 0.89 is the reason it is flagged. There is no consensus estimate to compare against for any of these: forward consensus EPS is N/A because no sell-side figure could be retrieved from public sources.

Management commentary offers some support for recovery. On the Q1 FY27 call the management described Rodelta delays in the Netherlands as the cause of the Dutch loss, the SPP UK result as a weak quarter in a business that earned 12.3% in FY25, and the data-centre framework contract with a major US operator as in final stages. These are explanations, not numbers; the KPIs can only turn from FAIL to PASS when the reported margin moves. The earlier analyst meet of November 2025 and the Q3 FY26 call give a longer record of the same themes, and the pattern of management promising recovery in the next quarter is itself a reason to wait for evidence.[12][16][19]

What would change this view

  • If the KBI B.V. EBITDA margin returns to 9% or above in the Q2 FY27 print, up from 5.2%, the one failing KPI turns to PASS and the earnings path improves.
  • If debtor days fall to 50 or below by March 2027, down from 53, the working-capital KPI clears.
  • If the standalone margin drops below 13% from 13.7%, the one passing KPI fails and the Indian engine is no longer a safe anchor for the thesis.

The KPIs show an unfinished recovery, and the chart shows how the market is pricing it.

What this means for you: the next two results (Q2 FY27 in early to mid November 2026 and Q3 in February) need to show overseas margin recovering and receivables stabilising for these KPIs to turn green.

Layer 3: technical posture and asymmetry

Layer 3 asks whether the chart gives an entry with a defined stop and a reward worth taking. A stock can be a good company and a bad purchase if its price is falling through support, or if the likely gain is smaller than the likely loss. All figures are from the run's fact ledger for the 9 October 2026 session.[36]

Reading guide for the technical table: the left column names a measurement and the right column gives its value on 9 October 2026. Prices are in rupees. The averages are the mean closing price over 20, 50 and 200 sessions; a stock above all three is in good shape, and one below all three is under pressure. Take the first averages row: the price of ₹1,690.10 is below the 20-day (₹1,761.25) and 50-day (₹1,845.83) averages and just under the 200-day (₹1,714.31), so the price is below all three lines and the 50-day average is falling.

Stage1 (Basing)

The technical table describes a stock that is weak but not broken. The price is below its 20-day and 50-day averages and a hair under the 200-day, the 50-day line is falling (from ₹1,896.90 to ₹1,845.83 in 21 sessions), and the month return is −8.81%. Momentum is soft but not oversold: RSI of 38.59 is above 30 and the stock bounced 1.37% on the day. Volume at 0.51 times the 20-day average says the bounce had little conviction, and 7 distribution days in 25 sessions say sellers were heavier than buyers earlier in the period. The relative strength figures of about −5% on both horizons confirm the stock has lagged the index. A reader should take away that the base low of ₹1,640 is the line that matters: it has held for 63 sessions, and a close under it would turn a base into a breakdown. The number to watch is that ₹1,640 floor, with ₹1,714 as the first hurdle above.[36]

The market backdrop adds weight to caution. The run's market regime is Downtrend: 17.7% of stocks are above their 50-day average, 37.4% above their 200-day average, there are 10 distribution days on the index, and none of the 11 sectors is above its 50-day average. A stock that cannot hold above its averages in that setting has less than usual reason to be bought early.

The technical gate is a FAIL. The momentum gate has two tests and needs only one to pass. Test A needs price above the 50-day average and fails (₹1,690 against ₹1,846). Test B needs a flat or rising 50-day average with the price close to the base pivot, and fails because the average fell about 2.7% in 21 sessions against a 0.5% limit. There is no death cross, since the 50-day is still above the 200-day, but the gap is narrowing: ₹1,846 against ₹1,714 is a gap of about ₹132 and shrinking as the 50-day line falls.

Entry, stop and support: the ladder accepts an entry that is 0.5, 1.0 or 1.2 ATR above a support level. The nearest support is the ₹1,640 base low, and the close is 0.85 ATR above it. That distance would normally sit on the ladder, but all of the longer-term averages lie above the price, so the stock is stretched under resistance rather than resting on support. The Core stop rule is below the lower of the swing low (₹1,640) and the 50- or 200-day average (₹1,714) by one ATR: 1,640 − 58.76 = ₹1,581, a risk of ₹109 per share (6.4%) from the close.

Asymmetry is the second failed gate. The ratio is the target minus the entry, divided by the entry minus the stop. The target is forward earnings per share times the three-year median price-to-earnings multiple. Consensus forward EPS is N/A: no sell-side consensus was retrievable, so the figure used is my own estimate with the working shown. The three-year median P/E is 34.7 (Screener chart API, 157 weekly observations from October 2023, range 22.7 to 60.1; current 33.2).[35]

Reading guide for the asymmetry table: each row is a scenario for next year's earnings per share. The target is that EPS times the median multiple of 34.7, reward is the target minus the ₹1,690.10 close, risk is the close minus the ₹1,581 stop, and the ratio is reward divided by risk. A ratio below 1 means you risk more than you can make. Take the base row: EPS of ₹51.8 times 34.7 gives ₹1,797, so reward is ₹107 against risk of ₹109 and the ratio is 0.98, which is less than one rupee of gain for each rupee at risk.

CaseFY27E EPSTarget (EPS × 34.7)RewardRisk (to ₹1,581)Ratio
Base: +10% on FY26 ₹47.05, matching guided double-digit revenue growth with flat margins₹51.8₹1,797₹107₹1090.98x
Bull: +17%, margin recovery plus data-centre ramp₹55.0₹1,909₹219₹1092.0x
Screener-implied base: +10% on ₹50.90 implied by the 33.2 P/E₹56.0₹1,943₹253₹1092.3x
Floor for Core: ratio of 3.0₹58.1 needed (+23.5%)₹2,017₹327₹1093.0x

No scenario reaches the 3.0 floor, and the base case does not even reach 1.0. Reading across the rows, the reward needed for a Core entry is about ₹327 a share, which requires a target of ₹2,017 or earnings of ₹58.1, a rise of 23.5% on FY26. The bull case with EPS of ₹55 gives 2.0x, and even the generous Screener-implied base, which uses the higher earnings base from the P/E discrepancy, gives 2.3x. The conclusion holds under either earnings base: whichever earnings base is correct, the stock fails the 3.0 test. Q1 FY27 profit was flat year on year (₹676 Mn, +0.1%), so even +10% for the full year needs profit to improve through the remaining three quarters. A target of ₹2,017 is above the 63-session high of ₹2,007.60 and would need growth nothing reported so far supports. The number to watch is the price: at about ₹1,500 the same targets would give much better ratios, but a close below ₹1,640 cancels the base first.[36]

Worked example, an illustration only and not a policy: suppose an investor with ₹10 lakh of capital bought 91 shares at the ₹1,690.10 close, a cost of ₹1,53,799. The stop at ₹1,581 means a loss of ₹108.86 a share, or about ₹9,900 in total if it triggers. In the base case the target of ₹1,797 would earn about ₹9,737, which is 0.98 times the amount at risk, so the investor risks ₹9,900 to make about ₹9,603, or 0.98 times the amount at risk. In the bull case the gain would be ₹19,929, or 2.0 times the risk. Only at the ₹2,017 floor target would the gain reach ₹29,757, which is 3.0 times the risk and the minimum the rulebook demands. This is why the answer is wait: the position as it stands today would pay less than it could cost unless the earnings estimate rises sharply.

What would change this view

  • If the price closes above ₹1,714 with the 50-day average flat, the momentum gate can pass and the stage reading can be re-scored.
  • If full-year EPS estimates rise above ₹58.1, up from my base of ₹51.8, the 3.0 ratio is reachable at the current stop.
  • If the stock falls below ₹1,640 and the stop moves to a new base low, the arithmetic resets and the old ratio no longer applies.

The chart and the arithmetic both say wait, so the valuation chapter checks whether the price offers a cushion that makes up for it.

What this means for you: at today's price you are risking ₹109 to make ₹107 in the base case. The rule requires three times that. The honest answer is wait.

Valuation and peer comparison

At 33.2 times earnings, KBL is slightly below its three-year median multiple of 34.7 and cheaper than the listed pump and compressor peers, whose median is 40.3. The cheapness comes with a price: those peers have higher returns on capital and, in most cases, faster profit growth. Price compounded 22% a year over three years and 34% over five, but fell 13% over the past year while profit fell 4% on a trailing basis.[1][35] A stock trading at its median multiple on flat earnings gives no valuation cushion. For the multiple to rise to about 40 on today's earnings and reach roughly ₹1,880, the market would have to pay more for the same profit, and expansion in a Downtrend regime is the weaker bet.

Three valuation anchors are worth separating. The first is the trailing P/E of 33.2 on Screener's earnings base (35.9 on the TTM EPS of ₹47.02 and the close, a discrepancy flagged in the forensic chapter). The second is the three-year median of 34.7, from 157 weekly observations with a low of 22.7 and a high of 60.1; today's multiple sits near the middle of that range, not at an extreme. The third is the peer median of 40.3, which says that KBL trades about 18% below its listed comparison group. A discount to peers is a reason to look closer only if the gap in quality is smaller than the gap in price, and the peer table that follows is where that test happens.

Reading guide for the peer table: each row is a listed company in pumps, compressors or engines. Price is in rupees, P/E is price divided by trailing earnings, market cap is in ₹ Cr, and ROCE is operating profit earned per rupee of capital. The Run stage column is the stage the run's own screen gave each stock, and N/A means the name was not in the run's screened universe. A cheap multiple with a high ROCE is attractive; a cheap multiple with a low ROCE is usually cheap for a reason. Take the Kirloskar Pneumatic row: a multiple of about 32 and ROCE of about 30% against KBL's 33.2 and 20% means that stock earns half as much again on its capital for roughly the same multiple.

The peer table shows KBL with the second-lowest multiple among the larger names and a middling return on capital. Cummins and Ingersoll-Rand earn 39.5% and 57.1% on capital and trade at 54.9 and 46.3 times earnings, so their premiums are earned. Kirloskar Oil Engines trades at 56.5 times with a ROCE of only 14.6%, which makes it the expensive outlier. Shakti and WPIL trade at about 24 to 25 times, lower than KBL, with ROCE of 23.6% and 14.6%. The practical reading is that KBL's discount to the peer median of 40.3 is deserved only in part: its ROCE of 20% is close to the 22.9% peer median, yet its profit growth has stalled. Two names come out as candidates for deeper review: Elgi Equipments, already on the watchlist, and Kirloskar Pneumatic, which is new. KSB (Stage 4) and Kirloskar Oil Engines have no role in this run. The number to watch is the ROCE gap, which narrows only if KBL's return recovers toward its FY25 level of 28%.[2][30][31][32][33][34]

Applying the peer median of 40.3 to KBL's FY27 base-case EPS of ₹51.8 gives ₹2,088, and the three-year median of 34.7 gives ₹1,797. The difference between those two answers is the whole valuation debate. Using the peer multiple would require the market to treat KBL as a premium capital-goods name, which its profit trend does not yet support; using its own history is the more disciplined choice because it measures the stock against how the market has actually paid for the same business. Because the rulebook anchors on the stock's own three-year median, the target is ₹1,797 in the base case, and a price near ₹1,690 offers a reward of only ₹107.

Elgi Equipments, at 39.8 times and ROCE of 22.1%, is a compressor maker that the run already carries on the watchlist at Stage 3, which is the topping stage, so it is not an obvious substitute. Kirloskar Pneumatic offers about 30% ROCE for roughly 32 times, and it deserves its own dossier in a later batch. WPIL and Shakti are cheaper on earnings but smaller, with a market cap of ₹4,209 Cr and ₹5,170 Cr respectively, and the cheaper multiple reflects lower or more volatile growth. Peer data for several names rests on one Screener page read, so any decision to rotate into a peer should wait for its own full file.

What would change this view

  • If KBL's P/E falls below 28 with earnings unchanged, from 33.2 today, the stock would trade near the low end of its three-year range of 22.7 to 60.1 and the asymmetry ratio would improve materially.
  • If KBL's ROCE rises back above 25% from 20%, the discount to the peer median of 40.3 would look unjustified and a re-rating toward the peer multiple becomes plausible.
  • If Kirloskar Pneumatic's own dossier clears Layer 1 and Layer 3, the theme can be owned through that name instead, and this file stays on the reject list.

Valuation offers no cushion, so the next chapter tests the verdict by arguing against it.

What this means for you: if you want exposure to this theme, Kirloskar Pneumatic offers better returns on capital for a similar multiple and deserves its own dossier before any money moves.

Adversarial pass: three ways this view could be wrong

A verdict that has never been attacked is only an opinion. This chapter builds the strongest honest case against the REJECTED conclusion, then the strongest case that the conclusion is too kind, and finally names the single claim on which everything hangs. Each argument uses only figures already verified in this file.

1. The rejection is too cautious. A flat Q1 profit hides a standalone EBITDA margin up 90 basis points, an order book up 21% and a data-centre contract in final stages. If Q2 shows overseas margin recovery, earnings could rise 20% and the stock could re-rate before the chart confirms. The Screener-implied base case already shows a ratio of 2.3x, and a 20% earnings rise would lift the EPS to about ₹56 on the lower base or ₹61 on the higher one, which would remain below the 3.0 floor. Only a higher EPS case near ₹61 would clear that floor; the rule accepts missing the first leg to avoid a failed base. The cost of the cautious call is a price perhaps 10% to 15% higher when conditions are met.

2. The thesis is still too generous. If the 35% receivables rise reflects slow-paying irrigation clients rather than timing, cash conversion could fall below 0.8 and the ROCE could keep falling. The consolidated audit-trail exceptions would then become a bigger concern. With CARE's EBITDA margin trigger at 10%, a Q2 margin below 11% would move the outlook discussion from Positive to Stable.[21] There is also a hidden risk in the order book: pending orders rose 21% while Q1 order inflow rose only 4%, so the book is being filled more slowly than it is being worked down in some segments, and a slowdown in irrigation approvals would show up in revenue two quarters later.

3. The hinge claim is wrong. The decisive claim is that profit growth will resume in line with revenue. The evidence for it is the Q1 standalone margin and the order book; the evidence against it is that consolidated PAT was flat on revenue up 12.9%, and quarterly profit has stepped down from ₹125 Cr in December 2025 to ₹112 Cr in March 2026 to ₹68 Cr in June 2026 (seasonality explains part of the step). If the overseas margin does not recover by the Q2 print, FY27 EPS lands near ₹48 and the stock is simply a 35-times earnings multiple on no growth.[1]

Reading guide for the scenario table: each row is a possible outcome for FY27 earnings per share, with a price implied by the three-year median multiple of 34.7. The Implied price column is the EPS times 34.7, and the Move from close column compares it with ₹1,690.10. A row with a large move up means the market could pay you well if the scenario occurs, and a row with a move down shows the loss if it does not. Take the flat-profit row: EPS of ₹47.05 times 34.7 gives ₹1,633, which is 3.4% below the close, so even a year of no growth at the median multiple costs a small amount.

ScenarioFY27E EPS (₹)Implied price (₹)Move from close
Profit falls 5% (overseas weakness persists)44.71,551−8.2%

Base: +10%51.81,797+6.3%
Bull: +17%55.01,909+12.9%
Floor for Core: +23.5%58.12,017+19.3%

The scenario table shows an uneven payoff. Moving from the flat case to the base case adds 9.7 points of return, and moving on to the floor adds another 13 points, but the downside from flat to a 5% profit fall is a further 4.8 points. The distribution is slightly tilted to the upside on these figures, because the median multiple is applied to every scenario, yet the base case of +6.3% is far from the +19.3% needed to meet the rulebook's three-to-one test. The rows also show why the stop matters: a flat year would take the implied price to ₹1,633, only ₹7 above the ₹1,640 base low, so a flat-profit result would put the base under real test. A reader should hold on to the middle two rows: the base case is positive but modest, and the bull case is the one that needs both margin recovery and data-centre growth together. The number to watch is full-year EPS, and every rupee of EPS above ₹51.8 adds about ₹35 to the target.

The strongest single sentence against this dossier is that a business with 20% ROCE, almost no debt and AA ratings is being rejected for a chart that sits 2.65 ATR below its 50-day average. The reply is that the rulebook separates two questions. Whether KBL is a good company is answered in Layer 1, and the answer is yes. Whether it is a good purchase today is answered in Layer 3, and the answer is no because the price and the profit path do not combine to pay at least three rupees for every rupee risked. A later entry at a better price, or with better evidence, is not a missed opportunity under that logic; it is the same company bought on different terms.

What would change this view

  • If the Q2 FY27 consolidated margin prints at 13% or above, from 11.8%, the hinge claim gains support and the base-case EPS should be raised.
  • If the Q2 FY27 margin prints below 11%, the downside scenarios in the table become more likely and the CARE outlook risk rises.
  • If receivables grow faster than revenue again in the half-year balance sheet, the second argument above is confirmed and the cash-conversion KPI turns to FAIL.

With the case argued both ways, the file turns to what the portfolio should do and what to watch.

What this means for you: the one claim that decides everything is that profit will grow again as fast as sales, and the next two results will test it.

Governance, portfolio allocation and monitoring

The governance checklist for this file is short because nothing in the filings reads as a red flag. Promoter holding is 65.95% and unchanged; a pledge is N/A because it is not disclosed in the annual report or on Screener and was not verified on the BSE shareholding pattern. The auditor Sharp & Tannan Associates gave an unmodified opinion, with audit-trail exceptions at the holding company, two subsidiaries and a joint venture.[7] A postal ballot notice of 15 September 2026 proposes an independent director, Dr. Madhuri Kanitkar, and a Regulation 74(5) certificate was filed on 10 October 2026.[28][29] The trading window closed on 26 September 2026 for the Q2 results, which are expected in early to mid November 2026.[27] Liquidity in quiet sessions is about 25,000 to 40,000 shares a day, with spikes such as 28 September 2026 (457,358 shares).[36] Foreign-currency and European demand exposure comes through SPP UK and the Dutch group.

The target weight for this stock is 0%. No order is proposed. If the conditions below are met, the stop would be ₹1,581 and the Core sizing rule would scale the position so that a stop-out costs no more than the per-position risk limit. The entry zone would be re-computed on the trigger day. A starter position is not justified now, because the stop distance is real and the reward is not. In a Downtrend regime the portfolio posture is maximum cash, so the question of what to fund does not arise; the capital that would have gone here stays in cash until a stock with a better setup appears.

Reading guide for the monitoring table: each row is an event or price level that would move this file, the middle column gives the exact level, and the right column says which rule it affects. Prices are in rupees. A row is a reason to re-run the whole analysis, not to buy automatically. Take the first row: a close at or above ₹1,714 with the 50-day average turning up from ₹1,846 would let the momentum gate pass on either test, and the file would then be scored again from the top.

The monitoring table has three kinds of trigger: price, results and events. The price triggers are the fastest and the most objective: a close above ₹1,714 with a stable 50-day average is the first step, and a volume-backed close above ₹1,936 is the Core entry condition, while a close below ₹1,640 is a clean exit from the idea. The results triggers are slower and carry the substance, because the overseas margin at 9% or better and a consolidated margin of 13% or better are what would make the earnings estimate move up. The event triggers, the data-centre contract and the nuclear tender, are the upside surprises that would change the growth story in a single announcement. The first price trigger is about 1.4% above the close, so it can occur within days; the results triggers cannot occur before November. The practical lesson is that the file will be reopened by price before it is reopened by fundamentals, and the re-score must then ask whether the fundamentals have caught up.

Re-run the file when the stock closes above ₹1,714 with a rising 50-day average, or after the Q2 print, whichever is first. Until then the status stays REJECTED_BELOW_HURDLE. Two items remain open and are flagged openly: the promoter pledge is not verified, and the P/E discrepancy between the 35.9 computed and the 33.2 shown has not been resolved from the sources read.

What would change this view

  • If the stock closes above ₹1,714 for three sessions in a row, with the 50-day average no longer falling, move the file to an active re-score.
  • If a pledge of promoter shares appears in the September 2026 shareholding pattern, add it as a governance flag and lower any future allocation cap.
  • If the market regime changes from Downtrend to Uptrend, with more than 50% of stocks above their 50-day average, the cash posture eases and the bar for new entries is lower.

The remaining chapters document the rule checks, the market lists and the research record.

What this means for you: no action today. Set an alert at ₹1,714 and another at ₹1,640, and re-read the file after the November results.

Rule checks and case notes

Each case note below takes one business unit, one risk or one peer and sets out the story, the numbers, the verdict and the rule behind it. They are short by design so that a reader can scan them in any order. The verdict language follows the rulebook: PASS, WATCH or FAIL for a KPI, and the four action labels for a stock. Where a peer is mentioned it is for comparison only and carries no recommendation in this file.

KIRLOSBROS overall. The company passes all three Layer 1 gates (ROCE 20%, debt to equity 0.10, CFO/PAT 0.95) but is in a Stage 1 base at ₹1,690.10, 22.8% below its 52-week high. The momentum gate fails because price is below a falling 50-day average of ₹1,845.83, and the base-case reward of ₹107 against a risk of ₹109 gives 0.98x. Verdict: REJECTED_BELOW_HURDLE. The rule that triggered it is the Core requirement of a Stage 1 breakout or Stage 2 advance with an asymmetry of at least 3.0. The view changes if the price closes above ₹1,714 with a flat 50-day average, or if EPS estimates rise above ₹58.1.

Standalone India business. Q1 FY27 standalone revenue was ₹6,738 Mn (+9%) with EBITDA of ₹920 Mn (+16%) and a margin of 13.7% against 12.8%, helped by price increases of about 10%. Domestic pending orders excluding small pumps are ₹2,375 Cr, with about two thirds executable in FY27. This KPI passes the 13% margin line. The rule behind it is the Layer 2 standalone margin threshold of 13%. If the margin falls below 13% for two quarters, or unless input costs ease, the one clean part of the story would be at risk and the status would move to WATCH.

Overseas group, KBI B.V. Q1 FY27 revenue was ₹4,018 Mn (+19%) but EBITDA was only ₹207 Mn, a margin of 5.2% against 7.0% a year earlier, and FY26 was 9.2% against 12.3%. The pending order book is ₹1,505 Cr. The KPI fails the 9% threshold. The rule behind it is the Layer 2 overseas margin recovery test. The status changes only if the margin returns to 9% or better in the Q2 or Q3 prints; until then, growth in this unit adds revenue but dilutes the group margin, which is why consolidated profit stayed flat at ₹676 Mn.

SPP USA and data centres. Q1 FY27 revenue was ₹1,598 Mn (+29%) at an EBITDA margin of 16.0%, which is above the group average of 11.8%. Data centres are about 23% of US revenue, and a multi-year framework contract with a major operator is in final stages; a hyperscale package is worth USD 7.5 to 10 million. This is the best growth lever in the file, but it has not been announced. The rule is that unannounced contracts do not enter the base case. If the contract is signed, or if US revenue growth stays above 25%, the bull case of EPS ₹55 gains weight.

SPP UK and the Dutch group. SPP UK posted an EBITDA of minus ₹9 Mn in Q1 FY27 and the Dutch group minus ₹57 Mn, with Rodelta delays cited. For FY26, SPP UK margin was 7.4% against 12.3% a year earlier. These are the weakest units and the main reason the overseas margin fails. The rule is the Layer 2 overseas margin test. Unless Rodelta deliveries catch up in the next two quarters, losses of this size offset about 5% of the group's quarterly EBITDA of ₹1,306 Mn, and the status stays FAIL. If both units return to positive EBITDA in the Q2 print, the drag on the group margin would ease.

Receivables and cash conversion. Trade receivables rose 35% to ₹6,627 Mn against ₹4,925 Mn while revenue rose 1%, and debtor days rose from 40 to 53. Cash from operations fell to ₹334 Cr from ₹386 Cr, giving CFO/PAT of 0.89. The three-year ratio of 0.95 still passes the Layer 1 floor of 0.70. Status: WATCH. The rule is the Layer 2 working-capital line of 50 debtor days or less. If debtor days do not fall below 50 by March 2027, or if CFO/PAT slips below 0.80 on a three-year basis, the Layer 1 cash gate would come under pressure.

Credit ratings. CRISIL (November 2025) and CARE (December 2025) both rate the group AA with a Positive outlook and A1+ for short-term borrowings, on ₹1,700 Cr of bank facilities for CRISIL. CARE reports gearing of 0.11x and interest cover of 26.8x. Their negative triggers are an EBITDA margin below 10% sustained, or gearing above 0.50x. Q1 FY27 margin was 11.8%, which leaves 1.8 points of headroom. Verdict: no credit concern. The rule is the agencies' own triggers. If the margin dips below 11% in the Q2 print, the Positive outlook would be at risk of reverting to Stable.

Elgi Equipments. Elgi trades at ₹583 with a P/E of 39.8, a market cap of ₹18,481 Cr and ROCE of 22.1%. The run's own screen puts it at Stage 3 and it is already on the watchlist. Compared with KBL, it earns a similar return on capital but at a 20% higher multiple. Verdict in this file: comparison only. The rule that matters is that a Stage 3 stock is a topping pattern and does not qualify for Core entry. If Elgi falls into a new base and passes its own Layer 1 and Layer 3 checks, it would be reviewed in its own dossier, not here.

Kirloskar Pneumatic. The stock is at ₹667 with a P/E of about 32, a market cap of ₹8,669 Cr and a ROCE of about 30%, which is half again KBL's return on capital for roughly the same multiple. It is outside the run's screened universe, so no stage is available. Verdict here: candidate for a future dossier. The rule is that a peer needs its own full underwriting before any recommendation. If its Layer 1 gates pass and its chart is a Stage 1 or 2 setup, it could replace KBL as the way to own this theme, and until then the figures above come from one Screener page and should be treated as unverified.

KSB. KSB trades at ₹784.55 with a P/E of 52.1, a market cap of ₹13,654 Cr and ROCE of 24.7%. The run's screen classes it as Stage 4, a declining trend. KSB is the closest listed pump peer by size, with a market cap within 2% of KBL's, yet it trades at a 57% higher multiple while its chart is in decline. Verdict here: comparison only. The rule is that Stage 4 is a hard reject. If KSB's multiple compresses toward the peer median of 40.3 while its profit holds, its relative value would improve.

Kirloskar Oil Engines. The stock is at ₹2,210 with a P/E of 56.5, a market cap of ₹32,176 Cr and ROCE of 14.6%, and the run's screen places it at Stage 2. It is the most expensive name in the group for the lowest return on capital, which makes KBL look cheap by comparison. Verdict here: no role in this run. The rule is that a Stage 2 chart alone does not qualify a stock without the Layer 1 gates, and its ROCE of 14.6% is below the 15% floor. If its ROCE were to rise above 15% and the multiple eased, the name could be underwritten on its own.

Cummins India. Cummins trades at ₹4,830 with a P/E of 54.9, a market cap of ₹133,888 Cr and ROCE of 39.5%, and the run's screen puts it at Stage 1. Its multiple is 65% higher than KBL's, and its ROCE is nearly double. This is the clearest example in the group of a premium that return on capital supports. Verdict here: comparison only. The rule is that a high ROCE earns a premium multiple but does not by itself pass Layer 3. If KBL's ROCE were to climb back toward 28% as in FY25, the gap between the two multiples would look less justified.

Shakti Pumps and WPIL. Shakti is at about ₹419 with a P/E of 24.4, a market cap of ₹5,170 Cr and ROCE of 23.6%, near its 52-week low. WPIL has a P/E of 24.8, a market cap of ₹4,209 Cr and ROCE of 14.6%. Both trade about 25% below KBL's multiple, and both are much smaller. Verdict here: comparison only. The rule is that a lower multiple is not a reason to prefer a stock when the chart is near its low. If either stock forms a Stage 1 base with a breakout on volume, it would deserve its own file; otherwise, the cheaper multiple reflects the lower scale and the less certain growth.

The thirteen notes agree on one thing: nothing in the group is cheap enough on both multiple and chart to earn a Core entry today, and KBL is no exception. Its relative strength of about −5% over both 28 and 63 sessions places it behind an index that is itself in a Downtrend. The notes also show where better evidence will come from: the overseas margin for KBL and the full underwriting of Kirloskar Pneumatic for the theme.

What would change this view

  • If any peer in the group clears Layer 1 and forms a Stage 1 breakout on volume, replace KBL in the shortlist queue with that name.
  • If KBL's overseas margin recovers to 9% while its standalone margin stays at 13.7% or above, upgrade the Layer 2 scorecard from one of five to three of five passes.
  • If the run's market regime moves out of Downtrend, the bar for a new Core entry is lower and the whole group should be re-screened.

The case notes close the company file, and the next two chapters record the market's strongest and weakest names from the same run.

What this means for you: the group comparison does not point to an alternative to buy today, only to the next names to underwrite.

Bull list: the 26 strongest charts in the run

This chapter and the next carry the run's market-wide lists, which are generated from the facts ledger for 9 October 2026 and are the same for every dossier in the batch. They do not recommend a trade. Their purpose here is context: they show what a stock that passes the chart screen looks like, so a reader can see how far KBL sits from that standard. KBL itself is on neither list, because it is a Stage 1 base below its averages and not a Stage 2 advance or a Stage 4 decline.

The bull list holds 26 names picked by market-cap tier: 10 large caps, 10 mid caps and 6 small caps. Every name is in a Stage 2 advance with RSI inside the screen's momentum band of 55 to 72, and the qualifier column says whether the volume test was also met. Of the 26, 5 are confirmed, 17 are awaiting volume confirmation and 4 are Stage 2 with RSI outside the 55 to 72 band. The median RSI is 59.4, the median volume is 0.89 times the 20-day average and the average name sits 7.2% below its 52-week high. Seven of the names are in the Capital Goods industry, the same broad group as KBL: CPPLUS, PTCIL, RRKABEL, TDPOWERSYS, MTARTECH, ACE and TEGA.

The contrast with KBL is useful. A typical name on this list sits within about 7% of its high with RSI near 59; KBL is below all three averages, has a falling 50-day line and is 22.8% off its high. The lists therefore work as a calibration: the stocks that qualify are not the ones that look cheap, they are the ones whose price is already trending up with at least some volume. A stock that has the qualifier "awaiting volume confirmation" has the right trend but has not yet shown the heavy buying that usually marks a durable advance.

The bull table shows a market where the best charts are few and mostly unconfirmed. Only 5 of the 26 carry full confirmation, and 17 more await a volume surge, which is a weak sign in a Downtrend regime where only 17.7% of stocks sit above their 50-day average. The four names with RSI outside the 55 to 72 band are either running hot or have lost momentum while keeping the Stage 2 label. The Capital Goods names on the list (seven of the 26) show that the theme KBL belongs to has leaders, but they are different companies from KBL. The practical reading is that a stock should earn a place on this list before it earns capital, and the list is short enough that nothing forces a purchase of a lagging name. Anyone wanting exposure in this regime should favour a confirmed name over a base like KBL, and the number to watch for any listed name is whether its volume multiple moves above 1.0 on up days.

The median volume of 0.89 times the 20-day average is worth a second look. A healthy advance normally shows volume on up days above the average, so a median below 1.0 says that most of these advances are happening on thin trading. That is common late in a drifting market and it is why the screen separates confirmed from unconfirmed names. The reading for KBL, at 0.51 times, is lower still, which means its latest bounce is weaker than the typical bull-list name's.

The tier split matters as well. Ten large caps, ten mid caps and six small caps is the screen's fixed allocation, and the small-cap slice is shorter because fewer small caps qualify in a Downtrend. That scarcity is a data point about the market: when only 6 small caps pass a chart screen among hundreds, the risk appetite behind the market is low. KBL, with a market cap of ₹13,421 Cr, would belong to the mid-cap tier if it qualified, and the 10 mid-cap names above are the stocks it would be compared against for a place in a portfolio.

What would change this view

  • If the number of confirmed names rises above 10 from 5, the market's leadership is broadening and the regime reading should be revisited.
  • If KBL closes above its 200-day average of ₹1,714 and its relative strength turns positive, it could enter the next run's screen as a Stage 2 candidate.
  • If the median volume multiple on the list rises above 1.2 from 0.89, buyers are returning and the awaiting-volume names are more likely to confirm.

The bull list shows where strength is; the bear list shows where it has gone.

What this means for you: KBL is not on the strength list, so there is no reason to treat it as a leader today. Check the confirmed names first if you want to add risk in this regime.

Bear list: the 30 weakest charts in the run

The bear list is the mirror image. It holds 30 names, 10 in each cap tier, all in a Stage 4 decline: every name carries the screen's Stage 4 label with a weak RSI. The qualifier column records whether heavy selling volume confirmed the decline. Of the 30, 26 show no distribution-volume confirmation and 4 are confirmed. The median RSI is 31.6, the median volume is 1.63 times the 20-day average and the average name sits 36.4% below its 52-week high, which is five times the 7.2% distance on the bull list. KBL is 22.8% below its high with a RSI of 38.59, so it is closer to the bear list's profile than the bull list's on distance, but it is a Stage 1 base and not a Stage 4 decline.

Six Capital Goods names appear here: BEL, MAZDOCK, THERMAX, COCHINSHIP, GRSE and INOXWIND. THERMAX is the closest peer in kind to KBL, as an engineering products company, and its row shows a Stage 4 reading with an RSI of 34.33, a close of ₹3,256.30 and a fall of 38.3% from its high. It makes the point that a capital-goods name can sit almost 40% below its high even when order books are full, and it is the outcome that a stop at ₹1,581 on KBL is designed to cap at roughly 6.4%.

The bear list matters for three reasons. It marks what a failed base looks like: a Stage 1 base that breaks its floor becomes a Stage 4 decline, and KBL's ₹1,640 base low is the line between those two labels. It also helps size the risk. If a stock breaks down, the typical name on this list has lost over a third of its value, so a stop is the only protection that works. And it shows breadth: 30 names with 10 in each tier means the weakness is not confined to small caps, which agrees with the regime reading that none of the 11 sectors is above its 50-day average.

Reading guide for the bear table: the columns are the same as the bull table, but the stage is 4 for every row, meaning a decline. RSI is low, usually below 40, and the volume multiple above 1.0 would suggest that selling is active. The Qualifier column says whether the decline is confirmed by heavy selling volume. Take a row such as THERMAX: the Stage is 4, the RSI is 34.33 and the close is ₹3,256.30, which shows a large cap in a persistent decline without a panic-level RSI.

The bear table shows a decline that is broad, deep and mostly quiet. Twenty-six of the 30 lack distribution-volume confirmation, which means these stocks are drifting lower on selling that is not dramatic enough to meet the volume test, and a drift of that kind tends to last longer than a single capitulation day. The average stock sits 36.4% below its high, and the median RSI of 31.6 is just above the oversold line of 30, so many of these names have already had the sharp part of the fall without bouncing. For KBL the table sets a clear boundary: a break under ₹1,640 would move it toward this list, and the typical loss from a high on this list is about 36%, while KBL is 22.8% below its high today. Anyone holding a name on this list needs a stop, and anyone considering a bounce trade should wait for a Stage 1 base to form first. The number to watch is the count of confirmed names, which is 4 now, because a rise in that count would signal that selling is accelerating.

Several of the names on the list are defence and shipbuilding companies (MAZDOCK, COCHINSHIP, GRSE, BEL) and power-equipment names (TORNTPOWER, SJVN, INOXWIND, THERMAX). That cluster says the capital-spending theme that supports KBL's nuclear, power and defence orders is under price pressure across the board, even where order books are healthy. It is a caution for the nuclear and power parts of KBL's growth story: the market is not paying up for capital-goods growth at present, and any KBL re-rating would be moving against the tide of its own sector.

What would change this view

  • If KBL closes below ₹1,640, below its 63-session base low, treat it as a Stage 4 candidate and stay out until a new base forms.
  • If the number of confirmed bear-list names rises above 10 from 4, selling is accelerating and the Downtrend regime is strengthening.
  • If fewer than 20 of the 30 bear names remain in Stage 4 in the next run, the market's weakness is starting to heal and the screen for new longs can be loosened.

With the market context set, the glossary fixes the terms and the methodology records how this file was built.

What this means for you: KBL sits between the two lists. A close under ₹1,640 would send it toward the bear list, and a close above ₹1,714 with volume would start the climb toward the bull list.

Glossary

ATR (average true range)
The typical daily price range of a stock in rupees. KBL's is ₹58.76, and the stop is placed one ATR below the base low.
Asymmetry ratio
Reward divided by risk. Reward is target price minus entry; risk is entry minus stop. The Core floor is 3.0.
Base low
The lowest price in the recent sideways range. For KBL it is ₹1,640 over 63 sessions.
CFO/PAT
Cash from operations divided by profit after tax. A ratio near or above 1 means profit is turning into cash.
Distribution day
A session when an index or stock falls on higher volume than the day before, a sign that large holders are selling.
EBITDA
Earnings before interest, tax, depreciation and amortisation; a measure of operating profit.
Gate
A pass-or-fail rule. A failed hard gate rejects the stock.
Moving average
The average closing price over a set number of days (20, 50 or 200 here), which smooths daily noise into a trend line.
OPM
Operating profit margin: operating profit divided by sales.
P/E
Price divided by earnings per share. KBL's is 33.2 on Screener's figure.
ROCE
Return on capital employed: operating profit divided by the capital used in the business.
RSI
Relative strength index, a 0 to 100 gauge of recent price momentum.
Stage
The Weinstein cycle: 1 base, 2 advance, 3 top, 4 decline. KBL is in Stage 1.
Stop-loss
A predetermined exit price. KBL's would be ₹1,581.
TTM
Trailing twelve months, the most recent four quarters added together.

Methodology, research record and data gaps

This dossier follows the three-layer underwriting method. Layer 1 tests ROCE, debt to equity and cash conversion; Layer 2 selects company KPIs; Layer 3 tests the chart and the reward-to-risk ratio. Technical values come from the run's facts ledger for the 9 October 2026 session, and financial values come from the Screener consolidated page read on 9 October 2026 and from the company's annual reports, investor presentations, call transcripts and rating rationales listed in the references. The three-year median P/E of 34.7 comes from Screener's chart data. The base-case EPS of ₹51.8 is my own estimate, shown with its working, because no consensus was retrievable.

Data gaps are stated and not filled. Forward consensus EPS is N/A: no sell-side figure was retrievable from public pages. The promoter pledge is N/A: not disclosed in the annual report or on Screener and not verified on the BSE shareholding pattern. FY22 and FY23 cash-flow and working-capital data are not used because Screener does not publish them in the format needed. The P/E discrepancy (35.9 computed from the close and TTM EPS against 33.2 shown) is unresolved, and both bases are tested in the asymmetry table. The ledger's 52-week low of ₹1,335 is used even though one web page quoted ₹1,333. The run's web-research record file was not present at the time of writing, so the sources in this dossier are the research record for this name, and the run's own record must be supplied by the orchestrator. Peer data for Kirloskar Pneumatic, Shakti and WPIL come from one Screener page each and are unverified against filings.
