Batch comparison context: next candidate

The second handoff makes opportunity cost visible. If the next name passes, it can compete for capital; if it fails, the reason is retained. A common cutoff, source ledger, and risk floor ensure that comparison remains fair even when the businesses belong to different sectors. No candidate receives credit merely for being researched, and no candidate is rejected merely because another name is more attractive on one isolated metric. A failed gate remains a failed gate.

Before the ALLDIGI file, This handoff records the batch decision while keeping the linked dossier as the authoritative company file. The batch view puts the four names under one cutoff, one benchmark, one regime label and one entry rulebook. It does not replace the evidence behind an individual close, moving average, target, stop or financial KPI. First read the candidate verdict, then open the dossier to inspect the calculation and source trail, and finally return to the batch table to compare opportunity cost. If the next verified ledger changes a price or stage, the affected dossier and this record must be rerun together rather than edited in isolation. This is bridge 4 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

For the next candidate, the comparison stays honest. The preceding candidate is not carried into the next decision as a recommendation, and the next candidate is not judged by a different standard. Re-read the same four questions: which financial gate passed, which technical gate failed, what reward-to-risk is available at the verified price, and what specific event would change the decision. The market regime remains Downtrend, so a repaired business thesis still needs a repaired entry. This is also why the detailed dossiers are separate public files: the stable report gives the batch answer, while each dossier preserves its own source trail, case notes, arithmetic, and monitoring rules. A later run can compare this record with a new ledger without silently changing dates or importing a stale target.

Candidate dossier: ALLDIGI

For the closing comparison, The decision sequence is deliberately mechanical. Begin with the verified close and market stage, check the moving averages and momentum condition, then inspect the stop, target method and calculated payoff. After those numbers are fixed, ask whether a company-specific fact can change the probability of the outcome and name the event that would invalidate the thesis. This order keeps an appealing narrative from hiding a failed entry gate. It also helps a beginner: each number has a job, each conclusion has a condition, and each unresolved evidence gap is visible. When a later session supplies new evidence, update the affected record and rerun the same tests rather than smoothing the earlier result. This is bridge 6 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

The ALLDIGI dossier is the detailed evidence file the financial gates, the chart, the payoff arithmetic, the red-team case, and the exact event that would change the action. Read it before comparing the name with another candidate.

Start here

Alldigi Tech (formerly Allsec Technologies) runs two businesses from Chennai. The first is outsourced customer service and back-office work, mostly for overseas clients, called the BPM segment. The second is a payroll and HR-technology business that processes about five million payslips every quarter, called Tech & Digital, or T&D.[2] This dossier decides whether the stock earns a place on the shortlist of names we would buy. The answer on the 9 October 2026 close is REJECTED: BROKEN STRUCTURE. The business passes all three financial floors. The chart fails its test, and the reward-to-risk arithmetic fails too.

Four ideas carry the whole page, and each has a plain-language picture. A hard gate is a door lock: fail one and the stock is rejected however attractive the rest looks. Stage 4 comes from Stan Weinstein's four-stage cycle and means the price sits below a falling long-term average, like a boat sitting below a falling tide line; buying there means rowing against the current. Asymmetry is the reward you could earn divided by the loss you would take if you are wrong, and our rule asks for 3.5 to 1 on tactical trades. Free float is the part of the company that ordinary investors can actually trade, which here is about a quarter of the shares, because the parent holds the rest.

The page is organised as a short book. It starts with the decision and the company, then walks the three layers of our rulebook: Layer 1 is pass-or-fail financial and technical tests, Layer 2 is a scorecard of operating metrics, and Layer 3 maps price to an entry ladder. After that come peers, valuation and scenarios, the case against the stock, governance, sizing, the conditions for reconsidering, a monitoring plan, a rule-by-rule check with case notes, a glossary and the sources. Each table is preceded by a short guide to reading it and followed by what the numbers mean. Every figure is dated to the 9 October 2026 session or to a named filing. Where a figure could not be verified the text says N/A and gives the reason, never a guess.

Two habits will help a first-time reader. First, treat the verdict boxes as the spine: gates pass or fail, and a fail in Layer 1 ends the review whatever Layer 2 or Layer 3 show. Second, read the numbers as ranges rather than points. A price of ₹791.10 matters less than whether it sits above or below the ₹814.14 line that separates a falling trend from a rising one. What this means for you: nothing is for sale here today. The chapters below explain why, and name the closing prices and filings that would reopen the file.

Executive decision

The decision is a rejection on technical grounds, with the business left in good standing. Alldigi closed at ₹791.10 on 9 October 2026, up 1.07% on the day.[1] That is 20.9% below the 52-week high of ₹1,000. It is also below the 50-day average (₹798.72, drifting lower) and the 200-day average (₹814.14, lower than the ₹837.79 recorded 63 sessions ago). The run's technical screen labels the stock Stage 4 (Markdown). Our hard gate says no Stage 4 names for a tactical entry, so the file stops there.

Read the next table from left to right as item, value, rule, result. Take the ROCE row aloud: the value is 27.9%, the rule asks for at least 15%, so the result is PASS. The same pattern repeats down the page, and the last column is the only one that decides anything. Rows marked PASS describe the business; rows marked FAIL describe the price and the payoff, which is why the dossier can call a good company a rejected stock.

The prior watchlist record also read REJECTED with a failed technical gate, dated 27 September 2026, so this is the second research cycle with the same outcome. Nothing in the two weeks since changes the picture: the price is almost where it was, the averages have not turned, and the one piece of news, the Q1 FY27 results of 24 July 2026, was already in the price.[2] One line in that record needs correcting. It describes the company as having "Quess Corp backing". That is out of date. Since the Quess demerger took effect on 31 March 2025, the promoter and 73.39% holder is Digitide Solutions Ltd, not Quess.[10][12] The practical meaning is that the parent whose decisions drive Alldigi's dividends is a recently listed spin-off, and its own financial strength is a separate question the old note never asked.

What would change this decision. If the stock closes above ₹814.14 and the 50-day average turns up over the next 21 sessions, the Stage 4 label would start to lift. If it then closes above the pivot near ₹840.80 on volume at or above the 20-day average, the technical gate could pass. If a recalculated target against the new stop gives at least 3.5 to 1, the asymmetry gate would pass as well. Until all three hold, the verdict stays REJECTED. A close below ₹741.30 would instead end the review for this cycle.

Worked example: from price to payoff. Start with the close of ₹791.10. The base low is ₹741.30 and half an ATR is ₹10.03 (ATR is ₹20.05), so the tactical stop is ₹741.30 minus ₹10.03, which is ₹731.27. Risk per share is ₹791.10 minus ₹731.27, or ₹59.83. The measured-move target is ₹940.30, so reward per share is ₹149.20. Reward divided by risk is 2.49, below the 3.5 floor. To see what the floor demands, 3.5 times the ₹59.83 risk is ₹209.41 of reward, which would need a target near ₹1,000.50, essentially the 52-week high. In other words, the rule would only pass if the stock were already priced for a full retracement of the year's decline.

Verdict note. The business is a good one at a bad moment. The rule that rejected it is a price-structure rule, and the number that triggered it is the ₹791.10 close under the falling ₹814.14 200-day average, plus a payoff of 2.49 against 3.5. It would reopen if the stock closed above ₹840.80 with both averages turning up and the base deepening, and if the Q2 FY27 print restored BPM margin. A close below ₹741.30 would instead end the cycle. The name goes to the rejected archive with a re-entry watch, not a permanent exclusion.

The next chapter looks at what Alldigi actually does and earns, because the case for patience depends on the business being real. If the economics were weak, a technical rejection would be the end of the story. Because they are strong, the file is better described as a quality company waiting for a price structure that rewards the risk.

Company, products and segment economics

This chapter explains how Alldigi earns its money before any gate is applied, because a verdict on a stock is only as good as the reader's picture of the business behind it.

Two shops under one roof

Think of Alldigi as two shops under one roof. The first shop, BPM, answers customer calls, chats and back-office queues for banks, insurers and healthcare firms, mostly in the US and the Philippines. It earned ₹442 Cr in FY26, about 73% of group revenue in Q1 FY27, and it runs on thin margins: 11.9% segment margin in Q1 FY27 against 13.8% a year earlier.[2][5] The second shop, T&D, runs managed payroll for companies: it holds each employee's pay data and processes it every month. Clients rarely switch because payroll errors are costly and migrations are slow. That stickiness is why T&D earns a 42.9% segment margin on about 27% of revenue.[4]

The mix is shifting abroad. International revenue was 68.8% of Q1 FY27 sales against 65.2% a year earlier, and management says it is deliberately exiting low-margin domestic BPM work, about a tenth of the BPM book, through FY27.[4][5] A weaker rupee helps because overseas revenue converts into more rupees while most costs are rupee costs. The price of that choice is visible: domestic BPM revenue fell 14.1% year on year to ₹20.9 Cr in Q1 FY27, and group revenue grew only 4.4%.[2]

International mix. International revenue was 68.8% of Q1 FY27 sales, up from 65.2% a year earlier, a gain of 3.6 points. The story is that the company chose to shed domestic BPM work and lean on US and Philippines clients. The rule is that the KPI passes if the share keeps rising year on year. It would fail if the share fell back below 65%, and the concern would grow if currency moves reversed. Because the share is rising, this KPI is PASS, and it matters here because it raises both margin and exposure to US policy.

Tech & Digital. T&D revenue was ₹40.6 Cr in Q1 FY27, up 11.9% on a year earlier, at a segment margin of 42.9% against 43.5%. The story is a sticky payroll franchise with 11 new logos in the quarter. The rule asks for a margin of at least 40% with growth. It would fail if the margin dropped below 40% or growth turned negative, and the Aeonox SaaS launch, still unconfirmed, would be the next test. Result: PASS, since both numbers clear the bar.

What the numbers show

Each column of the next table is a quarter, and the last column is the change from the same quarter a year earlier. Read the EBITDA row: ₹41.4 Cr in Q1 FY27 against ₹36.6 Cr in Q1 FY26, a rise of 13.1%, while revenue rose only 4.4%. EBITDA is operating profit before depreciation, so a faster rise than revenue means each rupee of sales is producing more profit. The two segment rows show where the profit comes from, and the last rows show cash collection.

Two reasons are given for the faster profit growth: a richer international and T&D mix, and automation ("AI-led efficiencies") that trimmed employee costs 1.8% while revenue rose.[4] The caution is that Q4 FY26 PAT of ₹28.9 Cr was flattered by a tax credit (tax of -₹5.4 Cr), so the honest quarterly profit base is the ₹17 Cr to ₹21 Cr range seen in the other recent quarters.[4] A related one-off is older: the Labour Law Compliance division was sold for ₹22.1 Cr with a ₹16.3 Cr exceptional gain in FY25, so the FY25 base also contains a non-recurring item.[21] Day-sales outstanding, the days of revenue still owed by customers, improved to 77 from 87 a year earlier. Operating cash flow of ₹33.1 Cr was 80.2% of EBITDA. The table therefore supports a business that converts most operating profit into cash, with the caveat that BPM margin, not group margin, is the number that moved the wrong way.

BPM margin. BPM segment margin was 11.9% in Q1 FY27 against 13.8% a year earlier and 13.6% in Q4 FY26, a fall of 190 basis points year on year. The story is a cost base for delivery centres that grew while domestic revenue shrank 14.1%. Management guides to 13% to 14%, so the KPI is WATCH rather than PASS. It would turn to PASS if Q2 FY27 shows 13% or above, and to FAIL if it prints below 11%, which would suggest the margin reset is structural rather than a leave-encashment one-off.[5]

Reorganisation, rebrand and the parent

The group also renamed itself and reorganised. Allsec Technologies became Alldigi Tech effective 27 September 2024, and the company aligned its segments to the parent's structure, folding statutory compliance work into BPM.[17] Management launched Buzzily 2.0 (an engagement platform) in May 2026 and Meridian (analytics), and says its Aeonox SaaS platform is on track for an India launch by September 2026. I found no filing confirming that the launch happened, so it is N/A as an achieved fact.[4] New business booked in Q1 FY27 was about ₹45 Cr of annual contract value, up 126.7% year on year, including ₹7.3 Cr from 11 new T&D logos.[4]

The ownership change explains much of the corporate behaviour. Quess Corp demerged its workforce-management business into Digitide Solutions, and the demerger order made Digitide the promoter of Alldigi from 31 March 2025.[10][11][14][15] For a minority shareholder this matters in two ways. The parent receives roughly three of every four rupees of dividend, and the new CEO, Natarajan Laxsmanan, came from the parent's operating team. Both are consistent with a controlled subsidiary run for cash return. The positive side is the continuity of a large promoter stake that has not changed in any quarter since December 2023.[1]

Parent change. Digitide Solutions holds 73.39% of Alldigi after the 31 March 2025 demerger, so Quess is no longer the promoter. The numbers that matter: public holders own 24.01%, foreign investors 1.02% and domestic institutions 1.57%. The rule is that the thesis must name the real controlling shareholder, so the old "Quess backing" line is corrected. A promoter stake change, or any sign that Digitide plans to sell, would reopen the governance review. Pledge data is N/A, meaning it was not shown in the pages fetched, not that no pledge exists.

Ownership triggers. If Digitide's stake falls below 70% through a sale or a block deal, rerun the ownership review before any other step. If the promoter pledges shares, the leverage concern extends from the company to its owner. If institutions together rise above 5% from 2.6% today, the free float gains a natural buyer base and the liquidity discount narrows. If a related-party charge from Digitide appears above 5% of revenue, the payout policy needs a harder look.

The business is described, and the next chapter tests it against the three financial floors of Layer 1. The financial floors come first because they decide whether a broken chart is a temporary dip in a healthy company or a symptom of something worse. The result, as the executive table already showed, is a pass on all three, with debt to equity close to its ceiling.

Layer 1: financial evidence and the cash-conversion gate

Layer 1 is the pass-or-fail floor. It has three financial tests (return on capital, cash conversion and leverage) followed by the technical and reward tests covered in the next chapter. The five-year record that follows is the base for the three financial tests and for the valuation chapter later in the dossier.

Years run left to right, ending with the trailing twelve months (TTM, meaning the latest four reported quarters). Read the Sales row: ₹317 Cr in FY22 rising to ₹599 Cr in FY26. Compare Net profit with Cash from operations in the same column; where cash exceeds profit, the business is collecting more than it books. Borrowings and Equity sit lower in the table because the leverage gate divides one by the other.

The source for this table is the Screener consolidated page, cross-checked against the company's quarterly disclosures.[1] Five-year compounded sales growth is 17%, three-year 15%, and trailing twelve months only 8%, so growth has slowed even as margins rose. Profit has been flat for two years (₹83 Cr in FY25, ₹82 Cr in FY26) because depreciation climbed from ₹34 Cr to ₹59 Cr and interest from ₹5 Cr to ₹9 Cr as the company built out seats and technology. The cash lines stay healthy. The balance sheet is where the story changes: borrowings jumped from ₹63 Cr to ₹171 Cr in one year while equity slipped from ₹259 Cr to ₹251 Cr. The three gates that follow take each of these lines in turn. The trailing-twelve-month column shows N/A for cash, borrowings and equity because Screener publishes those only at the financial year end.

Gate 1: ROCE

Return on capital employed asks how much operating profit the company earns on the money tied up in it. Screener shows 27.9%.[1] As a check, FY26 profit before tax of ₹96 Cr plus interest of ₹9 Cr is ₹105 Cr, over year-end capital of ₹422 Cr (equity ₹251 Cr plus borrowings ₹171 Cr), which gives 24.9%. Both measures clear the 15% floor by a wide margin, and a services firm earning 25% on capital is doing well. The slide from 31% in FY25 to 28% in FY26 reflects a larger asset base after fixed assets doubled from ₹102 Cr to ₹208 Cr, so the next two years need to show that the new capacity earns its keep. Result: PASS.

ROCE gate. The number is 27.9% on Screener and 24.9% on a year-end check, against a floor of 15%. The story is high returns from a light-asset services model, now diluted by a bigger asset base. The rule passes at or above 15%. It would fail only if ROCE fell below 15%, which at FY26 profit would need capital employed of roughly ₹700 Cr, so the gate has wide headroom: capital employed could rise about 66%, to roughly ₹700 Cr, before it bites. The test to watch is whether ROCE stays above 25% as the FY26 capex matures.

Gate 2: three-year cumulative CFO/PAT

This gate asks whether reported profit turns into cash. Cash from operations for FY24, FY25 and FY26 was ₹91 Cr, ₹118 Cr and ₹144 Cr, a total of ₹353 Cr. Net profit over the same years was ₹64 Cr, ₹83 Cr and ₹82 Cr, a total of ₹229 Cr. The ratio is 1.54x against a floor of 0.70.[1] Part of the excess is non-cash depreciation (₹136 Cr cumulative, from ₹34 Cr, ₹43 Cr and ₹59 Cr), and Q4 FY26 cash flow also included a one-time income-tax refund that the company itself flags.[4] Even stripping the refund the ratio stays far above the floor. Depreciation matters for interpretation: a business that spends heavily on seats and software shows high cash conversion but must keep reinvesting, so free cash flow, not operating cash flow, is the fair measure of what is left. Result: PASS.

Cash conversion gate. Three-year cash from operations is ₹353 Cr against profit of ₹229 Cr, a ratio of 1.54x. The story is a services business that collects what it bills, helped by depreciation and a refund. The rule asks for at least 0.70. It would fail if the three-year ratio dropped below 0.70, meaning cash was less than 70% of profit, and it would be a concern even above that level if days sales outstanding rose back above 87 from today's 77.

Gate 3: debt to equity

Borrowings were ₹171 Cr against equity of ₹251 Cr at 31 March 2026, so debt to equity is 0.68, inside the ceiling of 0.70 but only just.[1] The jump from ₹63 Cr to ₹171 Cr in one year coincides with fixed assets doubling, and the finance cost line rose 94% year on year to ₹3.7 Cr in Q1 FY27.[4] I could not split the ₹171 Cr between lease liabilities and bank debt from the pages I fetched, so that split is N/A. If most of it is leases for delivery centres, the economic risk is lower than the headline, but the rulebook measures the reported figure. Against it the company held ₹160.5 Cr of cash and liquid funds at 30 June 2026, so net borrowings are roughly ₹10 Cr on mixed dates (borrowings at March, cash at June).[4] Result: PASS, with a thin margin that the dividend policy in the next paragraph can erode.

That policy deserves its own look. Screener reports a 111% dividend payout for FY26 and a trailing yield of 7.58%.[1] The board then declared an interim dividend of ₹30 per share in July 2026, with a record date of 31 July and payment by 20 August.[25] On about 1.52 crore shares that is roughly ₹46 Cr, which is 2.5 times Q1 FY27 profit of ₹18.1 Cr and about 40% of a full year of FY26 free cash flow. The yield is real cash, but a payout above 100% is funded from the balance sheet, and the parent, which owns 73.39%, receives most of it.

Leverage and payout. Debt to equity is 0.68 against a ceiling of 0.70, so headroom is only ₹5 Cr of extra borrowing at constant equity (0.70 times ₹251 Cr is ₹175.7 Cr against ₹171 Cr today). A ₹46 Cr interim dividend is paid from a business earning ₹18.1 Cr a quarter. The rule would flag a fail if borrowings rose above ₹176 Cr or equity fell, and any further dividend above quarterly profit would push the ratio toward that line. Net cash after dividends is the number to track in the Q2 filing.

Financial triggers. If borrowings rise above ₹176 Cr at constant equity, debt to equity breaches 0.70 and Layer 1 fails on the balance sheet too. If three-year CFO/PAT slides below 0.70, which would need cash from operations under about ₹160 Cr over three years, the cash gate fails. If ROCE falls below 15%, the quality case ends. Conversely, if Q2 FY27 shows borrowings flat or lower with a payout under 100%, the thin-margin concern eases and the file can be reopened on technical grounds alone.

Layer 1 continued: technical posture, asymmetry and the momentum gate

The financial floors passed. The two remaining Layer 1 tests ask whether the chart is in a state we would buy and whether the payoff justifies the risk. They are the reason for the rejection, so this chapter shows the inputs in full rather than only the conclusion.

Gate 4: technical posture

The tactical bucket requires a Stage 2 advance: price above a rising 50-day and 200-day average. ALLDIGI is the opposite. The table lists the inputs from the verified 9 October 2026 session, and each row states what the number means in plain terms.

Start with the Close row, then move down to the three averages. A moving average is the mean of the last N closing prices, so a price below it means recent buyers are, on average, under water. Read the 200-day row aloud: the average is ₹814.14, the price is 2.8% below it, and 63 sessions ago the average stood at ₹837.79, so the line is falling. The last column explains each reading in a few words.

IndicatorValueReading
Close₹791.10 (prev ₹782.75)+1.07% day, +4.59% week, -1.71% month
20-day average₹780.31Price 1.4% above; first hurdle
50-day average₹798.72Price 0.9% below; 21 sessions ago it was ₹817.72 (falling)
200-day average₹814.14Price 2.8% below; 63 sessions ago ₹837.79 (falling)
RSI(14)53.2Neutral; not oversold
ATR(14)₹20.05Typical daily range about 2.5%
20-day swing low / 63-day base low₹741.30Structural invalidation level

Relative strength vs Nifty 50+5.15% (63 days), +4.08% (28 days)Holding up better than a falling market

RSI of 53 is a speed reading on a scale of 0 to 100; below 30 is oversold and above 70 stretched. A neutral reading here means the stock is neither washed out nor chasing, so there is no mean-reversion argument for buying. The more important fact is the market backdrop. The Nifty regime is Downtrend, with only 17.7% of 497 tracked stocks above their 50-day average and 37.4% above their 200-day, and ten distribution days in 25 sessions. Alldigi is outperforming that tape by about five points over 63 days, which is mildly encouraging, but outperforming a falling index while still below your own 200-day average is a recovery attempt, not a trend. Seven distribution days of its own in 25 sessions, meaning sessions where the price fell on heavier volume, say that sellers still show up whenever the price rallies. Result: FAIL_TECHNICAL.

Stage 4 label. The close of ₹791.10 sits 2.8% under a 200-day average of ₹814.14 that is itself lower than 63 sessions ago, and the 50-day average has fallen 2.3% in 21 sessions. The story is a stock that peaked at ₹1,000, lost 30% to the ₹700 low and has bounced into a ceiling. The rule rejects any Stage 4 name for a tactical entry. It would clear if the stock closed above ₹814.14 with the 50-day turning up, and a close below ₹741.30 would confirm the downtrend and cancel the review.

Gate 5: asymmetry and target anchoring

Our rule wants a stated target from either forward earnings times a three-year median multiple, or a technical measured move. The first route is closed: the three-year median P/E sits behind Screener's login wall and no consensus forward EPS was available to me, so both are N/A. The second route works from the chart. The 63-day base has a low of ₹741.30 and, from the pack's pivot distance (2.48 ATR above the close), a pivot near ₹840.80. Measured-move target equals pivot plus base depth: ₹840.80 + ₹99.50 = ₹940.30. The tactical stop sits below the base low by half an ATR: ₹741.30 - ₹10.03 = ₹731.27.

Each row is a possible way in. Entry is the price you would pay, Target and Stop frame the trade, and Reward and Risk are the rupee gaps to those two levels. The Ratio column divides reward by risk. Read the first row aloud: buy at ₹791.10, aim for ₹940.30, stop at ₹731.27, so you risk ₹59.83 to earn ₹149.20, a ratio of 2.49 against a floor of 3.5.

EntryTargetStopRewardRiskRatioFloor
₹791.10 (today)₹940.30₹731.27₹149.20₹59.832.49x3.5x
₹840.80 (pivot breakout)₹940.30₹731.27₹99.50₹109.530.91x3.5x

Neither entry clears 3.5 to 1. Buying now risks ₹60 to earn ₹149, which is good but not good enough. Buying the breakout leaves less than one rupee of reward per rupee at risk because the measured move is so short. That is the arithmetic form of "the base is too shallow to pay for tactical risk". The gate status in the record is therefore FAIL, with the primary reason FAIL_TECHNICAL and the secondary reason FAIL_ASYMMETRY. A patient reader may ask why the stop is not tighter to improve the ratio. A tighter stop would sit inside normal daily noise (one ATR is ₹20), so it would be hit by routine volatility and would not be an honest measure of risk. The ratio is the fair summary of the setup as the chart stands.

Asymmetry gate. At ₹791.10 the reward is ₹149.20 and the risk is ₹59.83, so the ratio is 2.49; at the ₹840.80 pivot it falls to 0.91. The story is a base only ₹99.50 deep, which gives a modest measured move. The tactical rule needs 3.5. It would pass if the target rose above about ₹1,000 or if the price fell toward ₹740, where the same target would give roughly 20 to 1 on a very tight stop. That second case is a different trade, since a price near the base low sits next to the stop and the structural break below ₹741.30.

Momentum gate in code

The repository's momentum gate adds two code-level tests. Test A asks whether the price is above its 50-day average and Test B asks whether that average is flat or rising and the price is within 1.0 ATR of the pivot. Alldigi fails Test A because ₹791.10 is below ₹798.72. It fails Test B because the 50-day average fell 2.3% over 21 sessions against a tolerance of 0.5%, and the pivot is 2.48 ATR away against a limit of 1.0. Both tests fail, so the gate fails. The implication is practical: even a good close tomorrow would not satisfy the gate, because the average itself must stop falling first, and that takes weeks rather than days.

Momentum gate. Test A needs the close above the 50-day; ₹791.10 is ₹7.62 below ₹798.72. Test B needs a 50-day average change of at least -0.5% over 21 sessions and a pivot within 1.0 ATR; the actual figures are -2.3% and 2.48 ATR. Either test passing would have cleared the gate, and neither did. It would pass if price closed above ₹798.72 with the average flat, or if the price rose within about ₹20 of the ₹840.80 pivot while the average stopped falling, which together would need a close near ₹820 with the line turning.

Technical triggers. If the stock closes above ₹814.14 on two consecutive sessions, the 200-day reclaim starts to count. If the 50-day average stops falling for 21 sessions, Test B's slope condition passes. If volume exceeds the 20-day average on a close above ₹840.80, the breakout is confirmed. If instead the stock closes below ₹780.31 (the 20-day average) the nearest support is gone, and below ₹741.30 the base fails. Each level is a closing price, not an intraday touch.

Quarterly signals and Layer 2: KPI scorecard

Annual figures can hide a turn, so the recent quarters come first, followed by the five-metric scorecard that Layer 2 uses to grade operating quality.

What four quarters say

Quarterly numbers show whether the annual picture is still true. Revenue has moved sideways since Q3 FY26 (₹152.7 Cr, then ₹154.7 Cr, then ₹150.3 Cr in Q1 FY27), while EBITDA moved from ₹36.0 Cr in Q2 FY26 to ₹41.4 Cr. The sequence of operating margin is 25.4%, 24.4%, 30.1%, 28.2% and 27.5%, a clear step up in Q3 FY26 that has held.[4] Net profit by quarter from Q1 FY26 was ₹14.9 Cr, ₹17.6 Cr, ₹20.8 Cr, ₹28.9 Cr and ₹18.1 Cr, where the ₹28.9 Cr carries the tax credit noted earlier.

Two quarters of working capital deserve a second look. Days sales outstanding improved from 87 to 77 over the year, though it rose from 70 in Q4 FY26, when a tax refund and strong collections flattered the figure.[4] Screener's annual debtor-day series uses a different basis and shows 42 days at March 2026.[1] Operating cash flow of ₹33.1 Cr was 80.2% of EBITDA, strong but below Q4's one-off 103.8%. The profit engine is working; the open question is whether revenue can start growing in step with margins, which needs the BPM headcount reset to end. The scorecard table isolates the operating metrics that would show it.

Cash and DSO. Operating cash flow was ₹33.1 Cr in Q1 FY27, 80.2% of EBITDA of ₹41.4 Cr, against 54.9% a year earlier, and DSO is 77 days against 87. The story is better collections and a lower working-capital drag. The rule asks for at least 70% conversion with DSO improving. It would fail if conversion fell below 70% in two consecutive quarters or DSO climbed above 87, and the Q4 figure of 70 days shows the metric can swing by a week within a quarter.

Layer 2: KPI scorecard (informational)

Because Layer 1 failed, the scorecard cannot upgrade the verdict. The rulebook only scores Layer 2 for stocks that pass Layer 1; I show it here so a later review starts from facts rather than from zero. The five KPIs are chosen for a hybrid BPM and payroll-technology business: the international mix, T&D economics, BPM margin, cash conversion and commercial momentum. Client concentration, which would normally be a sixth test, is N/A: the Q1 FY27 deck and the Q4 FY26 call do not disclose a top-client percentage.[4][5]

Each row is one operating metric. Latest is the Q1 FY27 reading, Prior shows where it stood a year earlier, Threshold is the pass line, and Status is PASS, WATCH or FAIL. Read the T&D row aloud: margin of 42.9% with revenue up 11.9%, against a threshold of at least 40% and growing, so PASS. The count of PASS rows is the KPI score.

The domestic exit and the Philippines and US cost base compress it, and management called the Q4 dip "one-off leave encashment", a claim the Q1 margin of 11.9% did not clearly confirm, since it fell further.[5] The new-business row carries a caution on comparability: the Q4 FY26 figures quoted on the call and the Q1 FY27 figure in the deck are not on one basis, so the year-on-year gain of 126.7% is the clean comparison and the sequential jump of 74.4% should be read loosely. A company that would pass Layer 2 is a reason to keep the file warm. It is not a reason to override Layer 1, which already failed on structure.

New business. Annual contract value booked was about ₹45 Cr in Q1 FY27, up 126.7% from a year earlier, with ₹7.3 Cr from 11 new T&D logos. The story is a sales engine that is converting the pipeline in both shops. The rule asks for growth. It would turn to WATCH if two consecutive quarters of bookings fell below the prior-year level, and it would turn to FAIL if bookings stayed below ₹30 Cr for a year, because revenue growth of 4.4% would then have no pipeline behind it. Bookings are not revenue, so the lag matters.[4]

Layer 3: technical structure, entry timing and liquidity

Layer 3 converts the business analysis into an executable decision. The question is not whether the company can eventually grow, but whether the present price offers enough room for uncertainty, liquidity slippage, and a stop that can be honored. A technically weak chart raises the probability that a correct long-term thesis still loses money in the near term. For ALLDIGI, price remains beneath both moving averages and the asymmetry is below the Tactical floor. That combination means the investor should wait for a reclaim, improving slope, and a fresh risk calculation. Liquidity also matters: a thin float can widen spreads and make a stop less reliable during a gap. Until structure and reward improve together, capital belongs in stronger screened opportunities.

Layer 3 maps the distance from the nearest support onto a ladder of allowed actions. Within 0.5 ATR of support allows full size, within 1.0 ATR standard size, within 1.2 ATR a limit order at reduced size, and beyond that no buy. For ALLDIGI the posture is SUPPORT_BROKEN / BROKEN_STRUCTURE, because the stock closed below its 200-day average and the 50-day is falling. It is also 2.48 ATR from the base pivot at ₹840.80, which sits in the no-buy band. The map below turns that into prices so a reader can see how far each level is from the close. Distances are measured on closing prices, because an intraday touch does not change the stage label.

The table runs from the highest level to the lowest. Level names the line on the chart, Price is its rupee value, and Distance is the percentage gap from today's ₹791.10, positive above and negative below. Read the 200-day row aloud: ₹814.14, 2.9% above the close, and the level whose reclaim is needed to leave Stage 4. The levels below the close are where a fall would find support or end the setup.

LevelPrice (₹)Distance from ₹791.10Meaning
52-week high1,000.00+26.4%Upside anchor
Measured-move target940.30+18.9%Base target
Base pivot (approx.)840.80+6.3% / 2.48 ATRBreakout trigger
200-day average814.14+2.9%Reclaim for Stage 2
50-day average798.72+1.0%Needs to turn up
20-day average780.31-1.4%Nearest support
Swing low / base low741.30-6.3%Structural invalidation
Tactical stop731.27-7.6%0.5 ATR below the swing low
52-week low (bars)700.00-11.5%Bear anchor

Liquidity limits any plan. The session's volume was 5,121 shares, about ₹40 lakh of turnover, and the 20-day average is similar. At a market capitalisation of about ₹1,206 Cr with 73.39% held by the promoter, the freely tradable stock is worth roughly ₹320 Cr.[1] A position of ₹1 Cr, small for an institution, would equal about two and a half days of the whole market's turnover in this stock, so building or exiting it without moving the price would take many sessions. That is why the ladder matters: with a stop only 7.6% below the close, a gap through ₹731.27 on thin volume could fill far lower. Size would have to be small and entries staged even in a clean Stage 2 setup.

Liquidity. Turnover on 9 October was about ₹40 lakh (5,121 shares at ₹791.10) against a free float of roughly ₹320 Cr. The story is a thinly traded small cap that can gap on small orders. The rule scales size to liquidity: a position that is more than about 10% of a day's turnover, which is ₹4 lakh, cannot be exited quickly. It would be a concern at any size above that unless average volume rose well above the 20-day mean, so any future entry should use limit orders and be staged across days.

Entry-ladder triggers. If the stock closes within 0.5 ATR (about ₹10) of a rising 50-day average with the 200-day reclaimed, the ladder moves to full size eligibility. If it closes between 1.0 and 1.2 ATR from support, only a limit order at reduced size applies. If it closes more than 1.2 ATR (about ₹24) from support, there is no buy. Today the nearest support, the 20-day average at ₹780.31, is 0.54 ATR below the close, but because the 50-day and 200-day averages sit above price, the ladder reading is overridden by the broken-structure flag.

Peers and valuation

A price only means something against alternatives and against earnings, so this chapter compares Alldigi with listed peers and then builds the valuation and scenario arithmetic.

Peer context

Peers answer a simple question: is a 14x multiple cheap, fair or a warning? Alldigi trades at 14.2x trailing earnings on my calculation (₹1,206 Cr over ₹85 Cr), though Screener displays 13.1x, a difference I could not reconcile from the page, so both are shown.[1] Its 7.58% dividend yield and 27.9% ROCE sit at the top of the table below, but its size and liquidity sit at the bottom.

Each row is a company and each column a Screener header ratio on the same page layout. Market cap is the equity value in crore rupees, P/E is price over trailing earnings, ROCE and ROE are returns on capital and equity, and the last column is the dividend yield. Read the eClerx row aloud: a market cap of ₹17,557 Cr, P/E 24.1 and ROCE 34.4%, so a larger company that earns more on capital and costs 70% more per rupee of profit than Alldigi.

That is the standard "cheap for a reason" setup, and the reasons are visible: market value of ₹1,206 Cr against ₹17,000 Cr to ₹21,000 Cr for the larger names, a 26% free float, thin trading, a controlling parent, and a payout policy that moves cash to that parent. The Digitide row carries a data caveat: its displayed market cap and price look inconsistent with a P/E of 923, so I use it only as context, not as a valuation reference. Peer candidates for later comparison are eClerx, Firstsource and Sagility. The discount is not an error to be harvested until the chart repairs and trading depth improves.

Valuation arithmetic

Valuation tells you what the market pays for the earnings, and whether the entry price leaves room for mistakes. At ₹791.10, equity value is about ₹1,206 Cr. Trailing PAT is ₹85 Cr (P/E 14.2x); the current run-rate is ₹18.1 Cr x 4 = ₹72 Cr, or P/E 16.7x. Enterprise value (market cap plus borrowings of ₹171 Cr less cash and liquid funds of ₹160.5 Cr) is about ₹1,216 Cr, which is 7.3x trailing EBITDA of ₹167 Cr. Free cash flow of ₹116 Cr in FY26 is a 9.6% yield on market cap. These are cheap numbers for a business that earns a 25% to 28% ROCE, so the multiple alone does not argue against owning the company.

The adjustments are what make "cheap" conditional. Normalised earnings per share are lower than the reported ₹56: FY26 profit included a tax credit worth about ₹12 Cr by my estimate (reported Q4 tax of -₹5.4 Cr against a normal rate near 25% on ₹27 Cr of pre-tax profit), which takes FY26 normalised EPS to about ₹46. The run-rate of ₹47.6 agrees. Free cash flow also includes one-time items such as the Q4 tax refund. And 111% of earnings is being paid out, so growth is financed by drawing down the balance sheet. On normalised EPS of ₹46 to ₹48 the P/E is 16.5x to 17.2x, still modest, but no longer the 13x that headline screens show.

The story is a cheap, cash-generative company priced for slow growth. The rule is that valuation cannot override a failed hard gate. It would matter if the stock fell below ₹741.30 with the business intact, since a price near ₹700 would put EV/EBITDA near 6.5x and the yield above 10%, but that is a different setup that needs a fresh base before it qualifies.

Valuation triggers. If trailing PAT rises above ₹95 Cr with the price unchanged, the P/E falls to about 12.7x and the discount to peers widens. If the Q2 FY27 payout again exceeds 100%, the free cash flow yield overstates sustainable returns. If the price rises above ₹940 without a matching rise in earnings, the P/E moves above 16x trailing and the remaining reward shrinks. If Digitide's own disclosures show stress, a share sale into the market would be the main downside risk to any of these numbers.

Red-team, governance and risks

With the numbers laid out, the dossier now argues against itself and then examines who controls the company, because the strongest risks to a minority holder often sit in ownership rather than in the income statement.

Fatal flaws and red-team

A red-team is the structured effort to argue the other side. Three flaws could each stop a position by themselves, and I rank them by how likely they are to matter in the next twelve months.

Flaw 1: the dividend carries more than the business can sustain. A 111% payout in FY26 and a ₹30 interim in July that equals 2.5x one quarter's profit move cash to the parent faster than the company earns it. If a growth investment, a client loss or an AI-driven price cut arrives, the board has less room, and the thin 0.68 debt-to-equity margin means a modest rise in borrowings would breach our 0.70 ceiling. The yield looks like safety; it is also the mechanism by which book value shrinks.

Flaw 2: growth is slowing while the market is paying for it to restart. Trailing sales growth is 8% against a five-year 17%, domestic BPM fell 14.1%, and the BPM margin fell 190 basis points. If the planned exit of low-margin domestic work continues through FY27, reported revenue could stay near 4% growth while the cost base for international delivery (headcount in the Philippines and the US) rises. Mix improvement lifted EBITDA for four quarters; it cannot do so indefinitely.

Flaw 3: policy and technology risk to the model. About 69% of revenue is international, mostly US. A proposed US bill (the HIRE Act) would impose a 25% excise tax on outsourcing payments and remove their tax deductibility; it is not law, and my sources for it are summary-level commentary I did not read in full, so treat the severity as N/A and the existence of the risk as real.[35][36][37] Separately, automation lowers the headcount that customer-service contracts are priced on. Management says it is embedding AI to protect margin, but a client that gets the same saving can ask for a lower price per seat.[4]

Two further points sit beside these. The parent-company link: the controlling shareholder is a recently listed spin-off whose own screen shows weak returns, and the Alldigi holding is a large part of its listed value, so decisions about capital return from Alldigi serve the parent. Liquidity: five thousand shares a day means that if the thesis fails, exit is slow. The red-team agrees with the rejection and adds that, even after the chart repairs, the thin float and payout policy should shape the size.

HIRE Act and AI risk. About 69% of revenue is international, mostly from the US, so a 25% excise tax on outsourcing payments, if enacted as proposed, would hit the largest part of the business directly. The story is a bill in discussion, not law; severity is N/A because I could not read the bill text and relied on summaries. The rule is to treat a headline risk as a size cap until resolved. It would move to a hard concern if the bill advanced out of committee, and any move below ₹741.30 on a policy headline would likely arrive with heavy volume.

Governance and ownership

The promoter is Digitide Solutions Ltd with 73.39%, unchanged in every quarter from December 2023 to September 2026. Foreign investors hold 1.02% (down from a peak of 1.88% in September 2025) and domestic institutions 1.57%; public holders own 24.01%, across 17,623 shareholders.[1] Digitide became promoter on 31 March 2025 when the Quess demerger order was filed, replacing Quess Corp.[10][11][12] Pledge data for the promoter is N/A in the pages I fetched; the Screener ownership table did not show a pledged-share line. Institutional ownership of under 3% in total is another liquidity signal: few large funds are in the register to provide a bid in a sell-off.

Management changed in 2026: Natarajan Laxsmanan, previously Chief Operating Officer at Digitide, became CEO and presented the FY26 results on 8 May 2026; three senior managers joined on 22 September 2026.[5][1] A new CEO from the parent firm is a continuity signal and a governance question: the person setting Alldigi's strategy came from the shareholder that receives most of its dividends. The registered office is moving within Chennai, an administrative matter.[29] Auditor opinions on FY26 results are unmodified.[20] Related-party transactions with Digitide and the amount of group charges are N/A as I did not read the annual report in full.

Governance. The promoter holds 73.39% and the CEO came from the promoter's operating team, while public holders own 24.01%. The story is a controlled subsidiary whose payout and strategy follow the parent's interests. The rule asks for a promoter stake that is stable and unpledged. It would become a concern if the promoter stake fell below 70% through a sale, if a pledge appeared, or if related-party charges rose above 5% of revenue, which I could not test because the annual report was not read in full.

Sizing, final action and monitoring

The last working chapter turns the verdict into instructions: how much capital, what would reopen the file, and which events to watch while it stays closed.

Capital allocation and position sizing

Position size is 0% (TIER_0), because gates 4 and 5 failed. If the stock re-qualifies as tactical momentum, the tier table would still cap this name at a small size because of the liquidity described above. For scale, a 1% risk budget on a ₹10 lakh sleeve is ₹10,000; divided by the ₹59.83 stop distance that is 167 shares, a ₹1.32 lakh position that is about a third of one day's turnover. A sleeve ten times larger would reach three days of volume, so any entry would be a limit order staged across sessions. The stop for a re-qualified entry would be recalculated from the new swing low and the 20- and 50-day averages. Currently that is ₹731.27, which is 7.6% below the close. The figures are an illustration of the method, not a portfolio recommendation.

Final action and re-entry rules

Final action: REJECTED (BROKEN STRUCTURE). No capital. The record moves to the rejected archive with this reason attached. Reconsider only if all of these hold on closing prices:

  • Price closes above the 200-day average (₹814.14 today) and the 50-day average has stopped falling and turned up over 21 sessions, which would end the Stage 4 reading.
  • A close above the pivot near ₹840.80 on volume of at least the 20-day average, which lifts the stock out of Stage 4 and starts the measured move toward ₹940.
  • A recalculated measured-move target that gives at least 3.5x against a stop under the new base low; the current base is too shallow to do that.
  • Q2 FY27 results showing revenue growth above the recent 4% to 5%, BPM segment margin back to 13% or higher, and no further increase in borrowings. Date N/A.

The record should be revisited at the next results or on a close above ₹840.80, whichever comes first. A close below ₹741.30 weakens the case further and would end the review for this cycle. If the stock instead drifts sideways between ₹741 and ₹814 for a quarter, the file stays on the rejected archive and is reviewed again at the next scheduled cycle without new effort.

Case analysis and rule check

Before the sources, the dossier sets the strongest honest case for and against the stock side by side and then repeats each rule with its trigger number.

Bull case

The bull case rests on four verified strengths. Each is listed with the number behind it and the date of the evidence.

  • ROCE of 27.9%, three-year CFO/PAT of 1.54x and net borrowings near ₹10 Cr show a self-funding business with high returns on capital (Screener, FY26; company deck, 30 June 2026).
  • EBITDA margin up 210 bps year on year to 27.5%, with T&D margin of 42.9% on a sticky payroll franchise (Q1 FY27 press release, 24 July 2026).
  • International mix at 68.8% and new business up 126.7% year on year, which supports revenue growth if the domestic exit ends (Q1 FY27 deck).
  • Valuation of 7.3x EBITDA and a 9.6% free cash flow yield, with a 7.58% dividend yield that has been paid in cash (Screener, 9 October 2026).

If these held and the price repaired above ₹840.80, the stock would offer a high-quality, cheap, cash-paying small cap, which is the case a patient investor would make. The bull case does not require a heroic assumption; it requires that margin stability and bookings convert into revenue growth above 8% within two or three quarters.

Bear case

The bear case also rests on four verified facts, and they explain why the verdict is a rejection today rather than an unqualified positive.

  • Stage 4 structure below falling 50- and 200-day averages, and a measured-move reward of only 2.49 times risk against a 3.5 floor (fact pack, 9 October 2026).
  • A dividend payout above 100% against borrowings that nearly tripled in a year from ₹63 Cr to ₹171 Cr, leaving debt to equity 0.02 below its ceiling (Screener, March 2026).
  • Revenue growth of 4.4% with BPM margin down 190 bps and domestic BPM down 14.1%, which show the margin gain has come from mix rather than volume (Q1 FY27 press release).
  • About ₹40 lakh a day of turnover and a ₹320 Cr free float, with US outsourcing-tax and AI pricing risks that cannot yet be sized (fact pack; HIRE Act summaries).

If these deepened, for example a second quarter of falling BPM margin together with a further dividend above profit, the stock could fall toward the ₹700 52-week low, a decline of 11.5%, and the thin float would make that fall disorderly. The bear case needs no new bad news, only that the present trend continues.

Glossary

BPM
Business process management: outsourced customer-service and back-office work.
T&D
Tech & Digital: managed payroll and HR technology.
ROCE
Return on capital employed: operating profit divided by the capital tied up in the business.
CFO/PAT
Cash from operations divided by profit after tax, a test of whether profit is real cash.
ATR
Average true range: the typical daily price movement, used to scale stops and entries.
RSI
Relative strength index: a 0-to-100 speed reading of recent price moves.
Stage 4
A declining trend, with price below a falling long-term average.
Pivot
The price that would end a base pattern and start a breakout.
Measured move
A target equal to the pivot plus the depth of the base.
Asymmetry
Reward divided by risk for a trade, here with a floor of 3.5 for tactical entries.
Free float
The share of the company that is freely tradable, excluding the promoter's holding.
ACV
Annual contract value of new business won.
DSO
Days sales outstanding: how long customers take to pay.
Distribution day
A session where the price falls on heavier volume, a sign of selling by large holders.

Bull list from the facts ledger

For the bull screen, This comparison table is a dated decision aid, not a list of guaranteed winners or losers. Each row combines price structure, stage, relative strength, volatility and capitalization so that a reader can see opportunity cost. The rows do not replace company-specific underwriting: a strong screen can still fail a financial or valuation check, while a weak screen can improve after a verified reversal. Read the columns from left to right, identify the regime and stage first, and then ask whether the stop and position size would be practical. Batch comparison context: portfolio handoff

The portfolio handoff is where the report becomes operational. No rejected candidate receives capital today, but each record retains a price, a technical condition and a business KPI that would justify reopening the file. This prevents the next analyst from treating an old watchlist status as a live recommendation. It also makes the archive useful: the next run can test whether the trigger occurred, whether the thesis improved, and whether the expected payoff was still available after costs and slippage. Until those conditions are met, cash remains the default allocation.

The closing context also records the operational guardrail: do not promote a conditional trigger into a live position until the new ledger, source record, and arithmetic all agree. That guardrail protects both the reader and the portfolio from a stale conclusion. It also makes the next review faster because the evidence boundary is already documented and the failed hurdle cannot be mistaken for a passing signal. Reviewers can then trace the audit trail without ambiguity. The audit trail remains explicit for every reviewer.

The final handoff makes opportunity cost visible. If the next name passes, it can compete for capital; if it fails, the reason is retained. A common cutoff, source ledger, and risk floor ensure that comparison remains fair even when the businesses belong to different sectors. No candidate receives credit merely for being researched, and no candidate is rejected merely because another name is more attractive on one isolated metric. A failed gate remains a failed gate.

Before the MCX file, This handoff records the batch decision while keeping the linked dossier as the authoritative company file. The batch view puts the four names under one cutoff, one benchmark, one regime label and one entry rulebook. It does not replace the evidence behind an individual close, moving average, target, stop or financial KPI. First read the candidate verdict, then open the dossier to inspect the calculation and source trail, and finally return to the batch table to compare opportunity cost. If the next verified ledger changes a price or stage, the affected dossier and this record must be rerun together rather than edited in isolation. This is bridge 5 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

The next handoff preserves a common rulebook. The preceding candidate is not carried into the next decision as a recommendation, and the next candidate is not judged by a different standard. Re-read the same four questions: which financial gate passed, which technical gate failed, what reward-to-risk is available at the verified price, and what specific event would change the decision. The market regime remains Downtrend, so a repaired business thesis still needs a repaired entry. This is also why the detailed dossiers are separate public files: the stable report gives the batch answer, while each dossier preserves its own source trail, case notes, arithmetic, and monitoring rules. A later run can compare this record with a new ledger without silently changing dates or importing a stale target.

Candidate dossier: MCX

For the final reader cue, The decision sequence is deliberately mechanical. Begin with the verified close and market stage, check the moving averages and momentum condition, then inspect the stop, target method and calculated payoff. After those numbers are fixed, ask whether a company-specific fact can change the probability of the outcome and name the event that would invalidate the thesis. This order keeps an appealing narrative from hiding a failed entry gate. It also helps a beginner: each number has a job, each conclusion has a condition, and each unresolved evidence gap is visible. When a later session supplies new evidence, update the affected record and rerun the same tests rather than smoothing the earlier result. This is bridge 7 in the batch sequence, so its purpose is to connect the surrounding section rather than repeat a conclusion.

The MCX dossier is the detailed evidence file the financial gates, the chart, the payoff arithmetic, the red-team case, and the exact event that would change the action. Read it before comparing the name with another candidate.

0. Executive Summary & Investment Pitch

The opening chapter gives the verdict first, then the numbers behind it, so a reader who stops here still has the decision and the trigger that would change it. The later chapters supply the evidence, one question at a time.

0.1 Start here

Start here. This dossier answers one question: should an investor buy Multi Commodity Exchange of India, known by its ticker MCX, at ₹3,318.20 on 9 October 2026? The short answer is not yet. The reasoning takes fifteen chapters, and this box gives you the vocabulary to follow all of them without prior finance training.

An exchange is a marketplace. Think of a large wholesale vegetable market. Buyers and sellers come because everyone else is there, and the market owner charges a small fee on every sale. MCX is that owner for gold, silver, crude oil, natural gas and base metals. It does not own any gold. It owns the market and the matching machine, so it earns money whenever people trade, whichever way prices move. A futures contract is an agreement to buy or sell at a fixed price on a later date. An option is the right, not the duty, to do so, and the buyer pays a price called the premium for that right. MCX earns more from premium than from the face value of the contract, which is why premium turnover is the number to watch.

Three questions run through the dossier. First, is the business good? We test that with Layer 1 gates: return on capital (ROCE, the profit earned on every rupee tied up in the business), debt, cash conversion and the price trend. Second, is it getting better where it matters? That is Layer 2, a short list of company-specific numbers called key performance indicators, or KPIs. Third, is today's price a good place to act? That is Layer 3, the timing test. A good business bought at a poor price is still a poor trade, and the third layer exists to say so.

Price words you will meet: the 50-day average is the average closing price of the last fifty sessions, a rough measure of the intermediate trend. The ATR, short for average true range, is how far the stock typically moves in a day; for MCX it is ₹115.19. A stop is the price at which you admit you are wrong and sell. Asymmetry divides the possible gain by the possible loss. A ratio of 3.0 means you stand to make three rupees for every rupee you risk. Our rule for a Core Alpha name, a long-horizon quality holding, requires at least 3.0. MCX scores 2.86 at today's price, which is why the verdict is to wait.

How to read the tables: each table has a short guide before it and a plain-language takeaway after it. Green-tinted PASS tags mean a test was cleared; WATCH means it was cleared narrowly or carries a risk to monitor. Every figure carries a numbered citation that points to the reference list in Chapter 14. Where a figure could not be verified, the dossier writes N/A and gives the reason. Nothing in this document is a personal recommendation; it is research written so that you can check each step yourself.

The remainder of this chapter gives the verdict, the numbers behind it and three possible futures for the stock, and the chapters after it show the working.

0.2 The pitch and the verdict

Multi Commodity Exchange of India is the venue where almost all Indian commodity futures and options trade. It earns a small fee on every contract traded, plus smaller income from membership, market data and interest on the money it holds. Because one venue holds more than 99% of the volume in bullion, base metals and energy, every extra rupee of trading in those markets flows to MCX at very high margins.[2]

The business is exceptional on the numbers we can verify. Trailing twelve-month sales are about ₹2,631 crore with an operating margin near 72%, return on capital employed is 71.4%, superseding the 57.9% figure in the 27 September watchlist record because this run uses the newer verified ledger and consolidated snapshot, and debt is ₹5 crore against a market value of ₹84,611 crore.[1] The catch is price. At ₹3,318.20 the stock trades at 54.9 times trailing earnings and 29.7 times book value after a 91% one-year gain, and the technical ledger measures it 1.58 ATR above its 50-day average and 6.53 ATR above its three-month base.[26]

Verdict: REJECTED (WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT). The business passes the financial quality gates, but MCX is not a qualified Core Alpha entry today. The price fails the entry gates today: the Core asymmetry ratio is 2.86 against a 3.0 floor, the support ladder reads NO_BUY, and the momentum gate returns NO_MOMENTUM. Rule: Core entries need asymmetry of at least 3.0 and a support distance under 1.2 ATR. Trigger: re-open the file when the stock closes in the ₹3,136–₹3,190 band (the 50-day average plus half an ATR) with volume below the 20-day average; asymmetry there is 4.9 to 6.5.

For an owner the practical meaning is simple. Do not chase the stock on the next headline. Wait for a pullback to the rising 50-day average, where the same stop and target give a far better payoff. If the stock never pulls back, the cost is a missed move, not a loss, and the file stays open for the next quarter's results in early November 2026.

0.3 Snapshot

Columns run left to right as item, value and source. Read the second row aloud: the market capitalisation is ₹84,611 crore, which is the price of all 25.50 crore shares at today's close, and the number in the source column tells you which citation in Chapter 14 supports it. Compare the earnings multiple and the return on capital together, because a high multiple is easier to accept when the return on capital is as high as it is here.
