Taken together, the snapshot shows a business that earns very high returns on almost no borrowed money, priced as if the recent earnings level is permanent. A trailing multiple of 54.9 means an investor pays ₹54.90 today for every ₹1 of last twelve months' profit, so a buyer needs profit to keep growing or at least hold near its present level for years. The price-to-book ratio of 29.7 says almost nothing here, because an exchange's value sits in its network and licences rather than its balance sheet. The dividend yield of 0.24% means almost none of the return will arrive as cash; it must come from the share price rising. The chapters that follow test whether that bet on a high, steady profit level is reasonable, starting with how the company got here.

0.4 Bull, base and bear cases

Each row is one possible future twelve months out. The second column is the share price in that future, the third is our judgement of how likely it is, and the last column lists what has to happen for it to come true. Read the base row aloud: ₹3,900 is about 17.5% above the close, and we give it a 45% chance. The three probabilities add to 100%, which keeps the scenarios honest.

Case12-month priceProbabilityWhat has to happen
Bull₹4,700 (+42%)30%Commodity volatility stays elevated, foreign investors are allowed deeper participation, new index options add premium, and the bank-guarantee rule costs little volume.
Base₹3,900 (+18%)45%Quarterly profit settles around ₹400–450 crore as volatility normalises; the stock holds its 50-day average and grows into part of its multiple.
Bear₹2,600 (−22%)25%Volatility fades, the collateral rule trims proprietary volume, and NSE or BSE take share; the multiple falls toward the 200-day average and the three-month base low.

Weighting the three prices by their probabilities gives about ₹3,815, a gain of roughly 15% from the close. These prices and probabilities are our own judgement, not a consensus forecast, so treat them as a way to test the idea and not as a forecast to bank. On the trailing EPS of ₹60.46 the three prices equal 77.7, 64.5 and 43.0 times earnings, which shows how much of the bull case rests on the multiple staying high. The probability-weighted scenario value is about ₹3,815, but the technical gate target is ₹4,397; using the scenario base of ₹3,900 at the current price gives only about 1.54x, while the technical target gives 2.86x. Neither clears the 3.0 Core floor at today’s price, so the conclusion is to wait. The ₹3,800 target reported for UBS in August 2026 is a useful outside cross-check and sits close to our weighted value.[25]

1. History & Corporate Milestones

A business with a monopoly in its main contracts did not arrive there by accident, and its history explains both the strength and the oddities in the profit record. The next two parts cover how the franchise formed and why FY24 looks so different from the years around it.

1.1 How MCX came to dominate

MCX began as a national electronic commodity exchange in 2003 and listed on the stock exchanges in 2012; the company's annual reports describe its regulated-exchange status and shareholding limits.[17] General background on the company is collected in an encyclopedia entry that we use only for orientation, never for a figure.[27] In 2015 the Forward Markets Commission merged into the Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI), so MCX now answers to the same regulator as the equity exchanges. That matters because SEBI can open commodity markets to foreign investors and to new products, which is the main source of upside optionality today.

The idea behind the moat is liquidity. Traders go where other traders are, because a crowded market lets them buy and sell at a fair price without waiting. A competitor has to persuade both sides to move at once, which is slow, expensive and often fails. That is why MCX holds more than 99% of the volume in its core commodities more than two decades after it began. The same moat also explains why regulators watch it closely: a single venue is a single point of failure, so rules on technology, risk and ownership are strict.

1.2 The technology rebuild and the FY24 trough

The recent history that explains today's numbers is the technology rebuild. MCX moved its trading and clearing platform to Tata Consultancy Services, and the new platform has been live since 16 October 2023.[2] The migration weighed on FY24: operating margin fell to 9% and profit to ₹83 crore as one-off costs and provisions landed in the second and third quarters.[1] Management now says the platform handles more than 3 billion transactions a day with capacity for more than double that.[3] The FY24 annual report records the migration period in the company's own words.[14]

The table has three columns: the year, the milestone and why it matters to someone who owns the shares. Read the October 2023 row aloud: the new platform went live, the cost of that change hit FY24 profit, and the gain from the lower cost and higher capacity showed up in FY25 and FY26. Use the last column to see the cause behind each date, not only the date.

YearMilestoneWhy it matters to an owner
2003Trading begins on a national electronic platformBuilds the liquidity network that competitors must now copy.
2012Listed on the stock exchangesPublic shareholding rules shape who can own it.[17]
2015Commodity regulator merged into SEBIOne regulator; product approvals follow the equity-market rulebook.
Oct 2023New TCS platform goes liveRemoved the capacity ceiling; FY24 margin took the hit, FY25–FY26 gained the operating gain.[14]
Sep–Oct 2026Leadership and tax disclosures listed; details N/A pending cache verificationLeadership and tax items to watch (Chapter 8).[9][10]

The history table carries one lesson for an investor: the jump in profit between FY24 and FY26 is partly a rebound from a one-off trough and partly a genuine step-up in options volumes, so FY24 is a poor base for judging growth. Measured from the FY24 profit of ₹83 crore, the FY26 profit of ₹1,332 crore looks like a sixteen-fold rise, but that overstates the underlying change because FY24 carried unusual costs. A fairer comparison starts from FY22 and FY23, when profit was ₹143 crore and ₹149 crore on much smaller sales. The platform rebuild also removes the excuse of a capacity limit, so any future slowdown has to come from volumes, rules or competition. The sections ahead take those three in turn.

Case note 1: the FY24 trough. MCX reported FY24 profit of ₹83 crore on sales of ₹684 crore, an operating margin of 9%, against 28% a year earlier.[1] The drop came from migration costs and provisions, with the second and third quarters of that year showing negative operating margins. Verdict: treat FY24 as a base-year distortion, not as evidence that the earnings power is fragile. Rule: a one-off trough is excluded when judging growth. Trigger: if another quarter shows an operating margin below 50% without a stated one-off cost, the one-off explanation fails and the margin gate needs a fresh review.

2. Operations & Revenue Model

To judge a trading venue you need to know exactly what it sells and what makes the revenue rise or fall. This chapter breaks the quarter into activity, concentration and costs, and each part ends with what it means for an owner.

2.1 What MCX sells and how it is paid

MCX charges members a fee on each contract traded. Futures are charged on traded value; options are charged on the premium paid, which is why a quiet options market earns far less than a volatile one with the same headline notional value.[2] Other income comes from membership and connectivity, data, and interest on clearing funds and cash. Interest on float was roughly ₹30 crore in Q1 FY27.[3]

Each row is one activity measure for the quarter ended June 2026, followed by the size and then the change against the same quarter a year earlier and the quarter before. Read the options row aloud: notional turnover was ₹9.90 lakh crore a day, up 266% on the year, while premium turnover was only ₹9,086 crore a day. Notional is the face value of the contracts; premium is what buyers actually paid, and revenue follows premium.

Driver (Q1 FY27)ValueChange
Futures average daily turnover₹59,674 crore+47% year on year; −34% quarter on quarter
Options notional average daily turnover₹9.90 lakh crore+266% year on year; +72% quarter on quarter
Options premium average daily turnover₹9,086 crore+114% year on year; −15% quarter on quarter
Operating revenue₹702 crore+88% year on year; −21% quarter on quarter
EBITDA, margin₹544 crore, 72%+98% year on year
Profit after tax, EPS₹413 crore, ₹16.21+103% year on year; −22% quarter on quarter

Read the table as one story: notional turnover exploded while premium fell, so the premium earned per rupee of notional dropped from roughly 1.03% to 0.35% as bullion volatility normalised, and management linked the fall in bullion options premium of 27% on the quarter to that normalisation.[2] Revenue follows premium, not notional, which is the reason revenue fell 21% on the quarter while the headline notional figure rose 72%. An owner should therefore track premium turnover, not the headline notional figure that exchanges like to quote. The year-on-year gains of 88% in revenue and 103% in profit show how large the step-up has been, while the quarter-on-quarter falls show how quickly the activity level can slip. The next sub-chapter shows how concentrated the source of that activity is.

2.2 Concentration and market share

Bullion and energy together make up about 97% of turnover, and gold plus silver alone are 77% of futures turnover (gold 43%, silver 34%).[2] Market share is above 99% in bullion, base metals and energy. That dominance is the moat, but it is also a single point of exposure: a quiet gold and crude market hurts MCX more than any competitor event.

Concentration has a second meaning for an investor. When two metals supply three-quarters of futures turnover, the company's profit follows the price behaviour of gold and silver more closely than it follows the Indian economy. A year of calm, range-bound bullion would reduce premium even if the number of traders stayed the same. Management's new products, such as electricity futures and a 100-gram silver contract, are meant to widen the base, but none of them is large enough yet to change the picture.[2]

2.3 Participation, cost base and operating leverage

Traded F&O clients were 13.72 lakh in Q1 FY27 (13.9 lakh in Q4 FY26 and 20.9 lakh for FY26 in total). The exchange has 597 members, 29,474 authorised persons and 4.05 crore unique client codes, and added 12 members and 35 foreign portfolio investors in the quarter (about 220 FPIs in total, around 2.5% of turnover).[2] Costs are mostly fixed, which is why margin went from 65% in the first half of FY26 to 75% when volumes peaked, and why it will fall faster than revenue on the way down.

The table splits the quarter's cash and non-cash costs into six lines, in rupees crore. Read the first row aloud: employee cost was ₹57.45 crore, of which management said roughly 8–9% is one-time. Add the five cash lines and you get ₹208.02 crore, which is the cost an exchange must pay whether it is a busy quarter or a quiet one, and depreciation of ₹20.70 crore comes on top.

Cost line (Q1 FY27)₹ croreComment
Employee57.45Roughly 8–9% flagged as one-time by management
Product licence fees39.68Paid to licensors of contract benchmarks and data
Technology28.12Platform operation and connectivity
Statutory fund contribution and regulatory fees54.19Rises with turnover and with regulator-set rates
Other28.58Administration and miscellaneous
Depreciation20.70Non-cash

The cost table explains operating leverage in plain numbers. Against operating revenue of ₹702 crore, cash costs of about ₹208 crore left an EBITDA of ₹544 crore on total income of ₹752 crore. If revenue fell by ₹100 crore with costs unchanged, EBITDA would fall by about 18%, which is sharper than the fall in revenue of 14%. In the other direction, each extra ₹100 crore of revenue adds close to ₹100 crore of pre-tax profit apart from the statutory contributions that rise with turnover. An investor should expect profit to swing more than sales in both directions, and should read a 72% margin as a feature of a good quarter, not as a floor. For a holder, the practical step is to watch the cost lines each quarter and ask whether any of them grows faster than turnover, because that is the first sign that leverage is starting to work against the owner.[2]

Case note 2: Q1 FY27 results. Operating revenue of ₹702 crore was up 88% on the year but down 21% on the quarter, and profit after tax was ₹413 crore (EPS ₹16.21) against ₹530 crore in Q4 FY26.[2] The quarter shows the business normalising after an exceptional spike, not breaking. Verdict: the earnings base is real but not constant. Rule: the base case assumes quarterly profit of ₹400–450 crore. Trigger: if Q2 FY27 profit falls below ₹350 crore, the base case moves toward the bear scenario; if it holds above ₹450 crore, the base moves toward the bull scenario.

Case note 3: concentration in two metals. Gold and silver are 77% of futures turnover and bullion plus energy are about 97% of total turnover.[2] The meaning is that MCX is a leveraged bet on the activity level of a handful of global markets, not on Indian consumption. Verdict: WATCH on the concentration KPI. Rule: a single product group above 75% of turnover keeps the KPI on WATCH. Trigger: if electricity futures and the new products lift non-bullion turnover above 5% of the total, the KPI would move to PASS; if bullion and energy rise above 98%, it would move to FAIL.

3. Sector-Specific Operating Dynamics

Four outside forces decide whether MCX can keep its current earnings: the regulator, the rival exchanges, the cycle in commodity volatility and the chance of new products being approved. Each is taken in turn below, with a number attached so the size of the risk is visible.

3.1 Regulation: the collateral reset

The biggest live regulatory item is the Reserve Bank of India's tightening on bank guarantees used as trading collateral, effective around 1 July 2026. Reports describe 100% collateral, at least half of it in cash or deposits, 40% haircuts and no bank funding for proprietary trading.[22] Dolat Capital estimates a ₹1.3–1.5 lakh crore guarantee-backed pool and says bank-guarantee-backed activity is about 15–20% of MCX's futures and options volumes, with the impact on MCX smaller than on BSE.[21] The Reserve Bank of India's notification pages are the primary home of the circular text; we point to them because the exact circular was not retrieved.[20][28]

Management, speaking on 5 August 2026, said the second-quarter impact was uncertain but not significantly detrimental so far.[3] The arithmetic of the exposure is straightforward. If 15–20% of volume is guarantee-backed and a third of that disappears, about 5–7% of volume goes, and at equal fee yield revenue is down roughly ₹35–47 crore a quarter, which is 7–9% of the quarter's pre-tax profit of ₹523 crore. If all of it disappeared, revenue would fall ₹105–140 crore, or 20–27% of pre-tax profit. The danger beyond the direct loss is that thinner proprietary liquidity worsens spreads and pulls away other participants.

3.2 Competition: NSE and BSE enter

NSE launched 10-gram gold futures on 18 March 2026 with a 10% margin and home delivery, and flagged electronic gold receipts, Brent crude and natural gas next; MCX fell up to 3% that day.[23] BSE has said it wants a 15% commodity share, using low fees and its low-latency platform.[24] Management said its core contracts held and the effect on a few days was short-lived.[3]

Exchange liquidity is a network effect, so share loss tends to be slow and then fast. A challenger first wins small, price-sensitive flows, and only when the depth of its order book looks adequate do large hedgers consider a switch. The warning sign to watch is therefore not a headline about a new contract but a measurable change in market share on gold and silver futures. A fee war would still cut earnings even if share holds, because MCX would have to match lower fees to keep its flow. The cost of a ten-point share loss is worked through in Chapter 8.

3.3 Volatility and the cycle

MCX earns most when gold, silver, crude and gas move sharply. Q4 FY26 was exceptional (income ₹925 crore, profit ₹530 crore, margin 76%) because of that volatility, and Q1 FY27 fell back 21% on revenue.[4] The stock rose about 41% in 2026 to mid-August on price and policy tailwinds, while UBS upgraded it on valuation after a correction.[25]

A sensible owner assumes a mid-cycle quarter rather than the peak. The reason is simple: premium income depends on how much traders are willing to pay for protection or speculation, and that willingness grows with price swings. After a year in which gold and silver have been unusually active, an average year looks like a decline even if nothing has gone wrong. That gap between a peak year and an average year is what the market multiple must absorb, and it is the reason a pullback is a better entry than a breakout at today's price.

3.4 Regulatory upside

Broker commentary expects foreign participation in deliverable contracts and new bullion and metals index options to lift premium by 20–25%, but that is an opinion, not an approved product.[25] Position limits and colocation access remain open items with SEBI.[19] The company reported 35 new foreign portfolio investors in the June 2026 quarter, about 220 in total, contributing around 2.5% of turnover, which shows the channel is open but small.[2]

Regulatory upside is valuable because it adds volume without a rise in costs, and because MCX, as the main venue, captures most of the new flow. It is also uncertain in timing, so we do not include it in the base target. If SEBI approves deeper foreign participation, the bull case of ₹4,700 becomes more likely, and that would be a reason to raise the bull probability from 30%.

Case note 4: the RBI collateral rule. Reports put guarantee-backed activity at 15–20% of MCX's futures and options volumes and describe a rule of 100% collateral, with at least half in cash or deposits and 40% haircuts.[21][22] If a third of that volume leaves, revenue falls about ₹35–47 crore a quarter, or 7–9% of pre-tax profit; if all of it leaves, 20–27%. Verdict: a dent, not a break. Rule: a volume-driven profit loss under 10% leaves the thesis intact. Trigger: if Q2 FY27 premium turnover falls below ₹7,500 crore a day with traded clients under 13 lakh, the dent becomes a thesis question and the file moves to review.

Case note 5: NSE and BSE competition. NSE launched 10-gram gold futures in March 2026 and BSE has stated a 15% commodity share target.[23][24] MCX still reports more than 99% share in its core contracts, and management said the effect on volumes was brief.[3] Verdict: no evidence of share loss yet. Rule: monitor share on gold and silver futures each quarter. Trigger: if reported share on those contracts falls below 95%, or if MCX announces fee cuts to defend flow, the moat score drops and the Layer 2 market-share KPI turns to WATCH.

What would change the view on the sector setting.

  • If SEBI approves deeper foreign participation or index options on bullion, then raise the bull probability from 30% and re-run the target.
  • If Q2 FY27 premium turnover holds above ₹9,000 crore a day despite the collateral rule, then the collateral risk is mostly priced out and the bear probability can fall.
  • If NSE or BSE report a commodity share above 10% in gold or silver, then the moat score falls and the base case needs a fee-cut assumption.
  • If gold and silver volatility fades for two straight quarters, then expect premium to fall further and use the bear case of ₹2,600 as the anchor.

4. Financial Performance & Forensic Audit

The financial chapter checks whether reported profit is real, whether cash backs it and whether the balance sheet carries hidden strain. The four parts run from results to cash flow to ownership.

4.1 Annual results

The table lists each year's sales, operating margin, net profit and earnings per share, with figures in rupees crore except the last column. Read the FY26 row aloud: sales were ₹2,302 crore at a 71% operating margin, and net profit was ₹1,332 crore, or ₹52.22 a share. Notice that profit rose almost nine-fold in two years while sales rose about three-fold, which is operating leverage showing up in the margin column.

Year (₹ crore)SalesOperating marginNet profitEPS (₹)
FY2236744%1435.63

FY246849%833.26

Sales compound at 43% a year over five years and 65% over three, and profit at 49% and 108%, according to the data aggregator.[1] Those growth rates are flattered by the FY24 trough as a starting point; the cleaner message is that FY26 profit was nearly 2.4 times FY25. The operating margin rose from 44% in FY22 to 72% on a trailing basis, but it passed through 9% in FY24, so the path was not smooth. Return on equity of 56.3% against a three-year figure of 36.4% shows that the latest year is far above the multi-year average. An investor should value MCX on an average of recent quarters, not on the best single quarter, and the next sub-chapter does exactly that.

4.2 Quarterly run-rate

This table has five quarters of data in the same four measures. Read the December 2025 row aloud: sales were ₹666 crore, operating margin 74% and profit ₹401 crore, nearly double the ₹197 crore of the September 2025 quarter. The step up happened in one quarter and has held, which is the observation that matters.

The step up occurred in the December 2025 quarter and has held at about double the earlier run-rate, with profit of ₹413 crore in the latest quarter.[1] Annualising that quarter gives about ₹65 of earnings per share, close to the trailing ₹60.46, so the trailing multiple is a fair reflection of the current run-rate and not a stale figure. The peak quarter was March 2026 at ₹530 crore, and the June 2026 figure is 22% below it. If profit settled at the average of the last three quarters, about ₹448 crore, annualised EPS would be about ₹70; if it reverted to the first-half FY26 level near ₹200 crore, EPS would be about ₹31. That spread is the earnings uncertainty a buyer at 54.9 times is carrying.

4.3 Cash flow quality

The table shows operating cash flow, investing cash flow, free cash flow and net profit for three years, in rupees crore. Read the FY26 row aloud: the business generated ₹3,035 crore of operating cash against ₹1,332 crore of profit, and invested ₹2,399 crore, mostly in investments rather than equipment. Compare the first and last columns: operating cash flow is more than twice profit in every year shown.

Year (₹ crore)Operating cash flowInvestingFree cash flowNet profit
FY24442−34629983
FY25950−751854560
FY263,035−2,3992,9621,332

Cumulative FY24–FY26 operating cash flow is ₹4,427 crore against ₹1,975 crore of profit, a ratio of 2.24, well above the 0.70 gate.[1] Forensic caution: this ratio is flattered, because FY26 other liabilities grew to ₹4,648 crore and include margin and settlement money from clearing members. That float is not owner earnings, and it can reverse if volumes shrink or collateral rules force members to withdraw. We therefore treat the gate as passed but do not read ₹2,962 crore of free cash flow as spendable. The audited FY26 annual report is the reference for the underlying liability definitions, and we did not extract those definitions line by line. A cautious owner would therefore value the business on profit, not on cash flow, until the split between member money and company money is confirmed from the notes to the accounts. That choice costs nothing in the base case and protects the investor in the case where the float reverses.[13]

4.4 Balance sheet, capital allocation and ownership

At March 2026 equity capital was ₹51 crore, reserves ₹2,797 crore, total assets ₹7,501 crore, investments ₹2,949 crore and borrowings ₹5 crore.[1] Working-capital days are negative (−465) because members pay margin upfront; debtor days are 10. The core settlement guarantee fund was ₹1,444 crore at Q1 FY27 (₹1,367 crore at March 2026) across 248 clearing members and 10 clearing banks, which is the shock absorber if a member defaults.[2] The dividend payout fell to 15% in FY26 from 27% in FY25, so cash is being retained rather than distributed; the yield is only 0.24%.[1]

Shareholding at June 2026: foreign institutions 29.85% (up from 26.07% in March), domestic institutions 50.80% (down from 54.35%) and the public 19.15%, across about 4.33 lakh shareholders.[1] The largest holders are Kotak Mahindra Bank 14.99%, Motilal Oswal Mutual Fund 4.48%, the Government Pension Fund Global 3.30% and Nippon Mutual Fund 3.00%.[2] There is no promoter, so there is no promoter pledge to test; this is an explicit N/A with a reason, because regulation caps any single holder.

A GST show-cause notice is listed in the source file, but the filing was not cache-verified in this run. The amount is therefore N/A and no verdict relies on it. The source list identifies leadership disclosures, but those filings were not cache-verified in this run; names and dates are therefore N/A and no verdict relies on them.

Case note 6: cash flow and the float. Three-year operating cash flow was ₹4,427 crore against profit of ₹1,975 crore, a ratio of 2.24, and other liabilities were ₹4,648 crore at March 2026.[1] The ratio passes the 0.70 gate easily, but part of it is clearing-member money that belongs to members. Verdict: PASS with a caveat. Rule: cash conversion is tested on a three-year total, and float growth is disclosed. Trigger: if other liabilities fall by more than a third while profit holds, or if the guarantee fund declines below ₹1,367 crore, the cash-conversion figure would need to be rebuilt without the float.

Case note 7: GST show-cause notice. The GST notice amount is N/A because its filing was not cache-verified in this run; it remains a monitoring item and does not drive the verdict. Verdict: not thesis-relevant at this size. Rule: a single-year tax dispute below 10% of annual profit is monitored, not priced. Trigger: if similar notices arrive for other years, the exposure could be four to five times larger, and if an adverse order is passed the file moves to a formal risk review.

Case note 8: leadership changes. The source list identifies a chief-business-officer disclosure and other senior-management changes, but the filings were not cache-verified in this run; the names and dates are N/A. Treat the item as an evidence gap, not as a confirmed event. Verdict: monitor, no score change. Rule: management churn is a risk flag if more than two top-team exits happen in a year. Trigger: if the managing director or chief financial officer resigns, or if three or more senior departures are filed within a quarter, the governance KPI would move to WATCH.

What would change the view on the financials.

  • If quarterly profit stays above ₹400 crore for two more quarters, then the ₹60 trailing EPS is a steady base and the base case holds at ₹3,900.
  • If operating margin falls below 65% without a stated one-off, then the operating leverage argument weakens and the multiple should come down.
  • If other liabilities fall by more than one third in a year, then rebuild the cash-conversion test without the float.
  • If the dividend payout rises above 30% of profit, then the capital return story improves and the low yield stops being a drawback.

5. Three-Layer Underwriting Framework

The three-layer framework separates business quality from timing. Layer 1 tests hard gates, Layer 2 tests company-specific indicators, and Layer 3 decides what to do at today's price. The sub-chapters follow in that order.

5.1 Layer 1: hard gates

The framework asks three questions in order: is the business good (Layer 1 gates), is it getting better where it matters (Layer 2 company KPIs), and is today's price a good place to act (Layer 3 posture). A name has to pass the first two before the third decides timing.

Each row is one test. The second column states the rule, the third the measurement and the last the result. Read the first row aloud: the rule is a return on capital of at least 15%, MCX measures 71.4%, so it passes with a large margin. Look at the last two rows, which are about price and trend rather than business quality; those are the two that fail.

GateRuleMeasuredResult
Return on capitalROCE at least 15%71.4%PASS
Debt levelDebt to equity below 0.70About 0.00 (₹5 crore debt)PASS
Cash conversion3-year cumulative CFO/PAT at least 0.702.24 (float-inflated, see 4.3)PASS
Technical stageStage 2 for Core, no Stage 4Stage 2 (markup); price above rising 50-day and 200-day averagesPASS
Momentum gateTest A or Test BTest A fails on extension (6.53 ATR above base against a 1.2 maximum); Test B fails on base pivot (6.53 ATR vs 1.0) and volume expansion (0.85 vs 1.0)NO_MOMENTUM
AsymmetryAt least 3.0 for Core2.86 at the current price (target ₹4,397, stop ₹2,941)FAIL AT CMP

The financial-quality gates and the business trend pass, but the entry-price gates fail. The last two describe the entry price. Rule: the asymmetry floor is (target − price) ÷ (price − stop) of at least 3.0. Here (4,397 − 3,318.2) ÷ (3,318.2 − 2,941.4) = 1,078.8 ÷ 376.8 = 2.86. The target is a measured move: base low ₹2,565.5 to the ₹3,481.4 high is ₹915.9 tall, added to the high gives ₹4,397. The earnings-multiple method (forward EPS times three-year median P/E) is N/A because neither a verified forward EPS nor a three-year median multiple exists in the evidence set. The measured-move target implies about 73 times trailing EPS, so it is generous, and that generosity is why a fail on this target is a strong signal rather than a close call.

The stop follows the Core rule: below the lower of the 20-day swing low (₹3,056.6) and the 50-day average (₹3,136.06), minus one ATR (₹115.19), which is ₹2,941.4. The Tactical stop would be ₹2,999.0, giving an asymmetry of 3.38 against a 3.5 Tactical floor, which also fails.

5.2 Layer 2: company KPIs

Five company-specific measures appear here, each with the reading, a status tag and the reason for it. Read the second row aloud: traded clients were 13.72 lakh against 13.9 lakh the quarter before, a small slip that earns a WATCH tag, not a failure. Use the reason column to see why a tag was given and which filing the number came from.

Three of five pass and two are on watch, meeting the 3-of-5 requirement but not the 4-of-5 that a strong pass would need. Rule: Layer 2 needs at least 4 of 5 for a clean pass; 3 of 5 qualifies the name only with a monitored risk, which is the collateral-rule effect on participation. Trigger: if traded clients fall below 13 lakh in Q2 FY27, the participation KPI turns to FAIL and the name drops from qualified to watch-only. The two WATCH tags are linked, because concentration in two metals and a fall in clients both point to the same fragility: activity depends on how excited traders are. The strong passes on premium, margin and share show that the franchise itself is intact.

5.3 Layer 3: posture and support ladder

The table measures how far the price sits above three support levels, in units of the ATR, plus the momentum readings. Read the first row aloud: the close is 1.58 ATR above the 50-day average, and the ladder allows entry only within 1.2 ATR, so that row says NO_BUY. A smaller distance means a safer entry, because the stop is close.

MeasureValueReading
Distance above 50-day average (₹3,136.06)(3,318.2 − 3,136.06) ÷ 115.19 = 1.58 ATRNO_BUY: beyond 1.2 ATR
Distance above 20-day swing low (₹3,056.6)2.27 ATRNO_BUY
Distance above 63-day base low (₹2,565.5)6.53 ATRExtended; this is the figure the momentum gate uses
RSI(14)54.0Not overbought; the stock is extended by structure, not by momentum
Volume0.85 times the 20-day average; 3 distribution days in 25No expansion on the rally

Posture: WAIT_FOR_SUPPORT, shortlist status REJECTED_BELOW_HURDLE. The ladder says buy at the rising 50-day average and not at the current price. Rule: support distance under 0.5 ATR is full size, under 1.0 standard, under 1.2 a reduced limit order and anything further is no buy. The RSI of 54 is a useful detail: the stock is not overheated, it simply sits well above its supports after a rise, which means a pullback is possible without any bad news and that an entry near support would not be fighting an overheated chart. Falling volume at 0.85 times average says the latest advance was not driven by heavy buying. Together those readings argue for patience, and the trade plan in Chapter 7 translates that patience into exact prices. In short, the result is a deferral and not a rejection, and the file stays on the shortlist with the same stop, target and entry zone until price or fundamentals change.

Case note 9: the asymmetry arithmetic. With a close of ₹3,318.2, a Core stop of ₹2,941.4 and a measured-move target of ₹4,397, the reward is ₹1,078.8 and the risk ₹376.8, a ratio of 2.86 against a floor of 3.0. If the price were ₹3,190 the ratio would be 4.85, and at ₹3,136 it would be 6.5. Verdict: the idea works, the price does not. Rule: Core entries need at least 3.0 and Tactical at least 3.5 (here 3.38). Trigger: the Core floor is met at any entry price below about ₹3,305; the support ladder, however, still blocks buying until the close is within 1.2 ATR of the 50-day average, at about ₹3,274.

Case note 10: the momentum gate. The facts ledger returns NO_MOMENTUM. Test A fails because the price is 6.53 ATR above the 63-day base low against a 1.2 limit, while the trend and relative-strength conditions pass (+26.73% against the Nifty 50 over 63 days).[26] Test B fails on base pivot and volume expansion (0.85 against 1.0). Verdict: not allocatable today. Rule: a name that fails both tests is not allocated however good the business. Trigger: if the price returns within 1.2 ATR of its support anchor while the 50-day average still rises, Test A passes and the gate opens.

What would change the view on the framework result.

  • If the close falls to ₹3,136–₹3,190 with the 50-day average still rising, then the ladder moves from NO_BUY to buyable and asymmetry becomes 4.9–6.5.
  • If the close breaks above ₹3,481.4 on volume above 1.5 times the 20-day average, then the stock enters a new base-and-breakout setup that needs a fresh measured move.
  • If traded clients fall below 13 lakh, then the participation KPI fails and Layer 2 drops to 2 of 5.
  • If the close falls below ₹2,941, then the Stage 2 structure and the Core stop are both broken, and the file moves to rejection review.

6. Industry Structure & Peer Comparison

A stock is cheap or dear only relative to something. This chapter places MCX beside the other listed market-infrastructure companies and explains what the comparison can and cannot show. The technical chapter that comes next then turns from value to timing, so keep the multiples in mind as you read it. It is worth saying plainly that peer tables are a sanity check on price, not a substitute for the earnings analysis in Chapter 4, and they can mislead when the companies sit at different points of their own cycles. Each peer was read from the same aggregator on the same date, which keeps the columns comparable, although the profit year behind each ratio differs slightly between companies.

6.1 Peer table

Each row is one listed market-infrastructure company, with size, price-to-earnings, return on capital and return on equity. Read the BSE row aloud: market value ₹1,34,080 crore, 47.4 times earnings, ROCE 60% and ROE 46%. Compare MCX with BSE on the multiple and the return, since they are the closest pair, and use the note column for the growth rates.

CompanyMarket cap (₹ crore)P/EROCEROENote
MCX84,61154.971.4%56.3%Commodity monopoly in the main contracts[1]
BSE1,34,08047.460%46%5-year sales growth 52%; trailing profit growth 79%[15]
CDSL26,33455.932%24.5%Trailing sales growth 9%; profit growth −5%[16]
IEX9,34318.451.4%39.4%Power exchange; dividend yield 3.34%[18]

MCX is priced at a premium to BSE on earnings (54.9 against 47.4) while having a higher ROCE and ROE but lower scale and a single-sector concentration. CDSL carries a similar multiple on a much weaker profit trend, which shows that the market pays exchange-like multiples for any market infrastructure with a franchise. IEX is the cheap exchange at 18.4 times, a reminder that a regulated, price-capped market earns a far lower multiple than a commodity-volatility one. NSDL was not compared because its page could not be fetched; it is an N/A for data availability. The comparison argues that MCX's multiple is not an outlier among exchanges, though it is at the top of the range, and that nothing here makes it cheap.

6.2 What the comparison does and does not prove

One further point deserves attention. The peers differ in business model: BSE earns mainly from equity derivatives and listing, CDSL from depository charges, and IEX from electricity trading, so only the broad idea of a market-infrastructure franchise is shared. MCX stands apart because its volumes depend on commodity volatility, which the others do not face to the same degree. That difference is the reason its earnings swing more from quarter to quarter and the reason a multiple copied from a steadier peer can overstate how safe the profit is.

Peer multiples answer a narrow question: is MCX priced like its neighbours? The answer is yes, near the upper end. They do not answer whether the neighbours are fairly priced, which matters because exchange stocks as a group have risen sharply. For that reason a peer comparison supports caution about paying more, but it cannot by itself justify a purchase.

The peers' own sites are the primary place for their disclosures, and we list them so that a reader can check how each company describes its business.[29][30] The market-regime ledger reads Downtrend for the broader Nifty universe, with only 17.7% of stocks above their 50-day average and 37.4% above their 200-day average, which adds timing risk to any high-multiple name.[26] MCX is a relative-strength leader in that tape, up 26.73% against the Nifty 50 over 63 days, but leaders fall too when breadth is poor.

Case note 11: valuation against peers. MCX trades at 54.9 times trailing earnings with ROCE of 71.4%; BSE trades at 47.4 times with ROCE of 60%, CDSL at 55.9 times with ROCE of 32%, and IEX at 18.4 times with ROCE of 51.4%.[1][15][16][18] MCX earns more per rupee of capital than every peer shown but is priced above all but CDSL. Verdict: fully valued, not mispriced. Rule: a premium multiple needs a return on capital to match, and MCX meets that test. Trigger: if the multiple rises above 60 times without an earnings increase, or if BSE closes the gap on commodity share, the premium becomes harder to defend and the bear probability should rise.

7. Technical Posture & Trade Architecture

The chart answers the timing question the business analysis cannot. The first part reads the trend and the second turns it into prices for entry, stop and target, with a worked sizing example.

7.1 Trend structure

The table lists the main price measures from the facts ledger. Read the 50-day average row aloud: it stands at ₹3,136.06, up from ₹3,034.09 ten days ago, so the medium trend is rising. Then compare the close with the three averages: it sits above all of them, which is the structure a Stage 2 stock should show.

IndicatorValue
Close₹3,318.20 (previous close ₹3,255.60)
20-day average₹3,275.60
50-day average₹3,136.06 (rising from ₹3,034.09 ten days ago)
200-day average₹2,775.32
52-week high / low₹3,481.40 / ₹1,596.00 (4.69% below the high)
ATR(14)₹115.19
Relative strength vs Nifty 50+26.73% over 63 days, +6.43% over 28 days
Returns1 week +3.56%, 2 weeks −3.19%, 1 month −0.71%

The structure is Stage 2: price above a rising 50-day average and above the 200-day average, with leadership against the index. The stock is only 4.7% from its high, so it is consolidating just under resistance and not correcting.[26] The one-month return of −0.71% and the two-week return of −3.19% show that the stock has paused after a rally, which is normal and often healthy. The close of ₹3,318.20 is 1.3% above the 20-day average of ₹3,275.60, so the short-term trend is intact. The weakness is the broader market: with the regime in Downtrend, a stock that holds its averages is rare, and that rarity is the reason it deserves a place on the watchlist. The same data also set the limits: the stock must keep closing above its rising 50-day average for the Stage 2 label to stay valid, and a close below ₹2,775 would end the uptrend argument altogether.

7.2 Support anchor and trade plan

This is the plan on paper: the entry zone, the stop, the target and the asymmetry at several prices. Read the entry-zone row aloud: limit orders only, between ₹3,136 and ₹3,190, which runs from the 50-day average to half an ATR above it. The asymmetry row then tells you how the payoff improves as the entry price falls.

ItemLevel (₹)Basis
Entry zone (limit orders only)3,136–3,19050-day average to half an ATR above it
Core stop2,941.4Below swing low and 50-day average, minus 1 ATR
Base target4,397Measured move from the 63-day base
Asymmetry at 3,136 / 3,163 / 3,1906.5 / 5.6 / 4.9(4,397 − entry) ÷ (entry − 2,941.4)
Asymmetry at the 3,318.2 close2.86Fails the 3.0 floor

This is a conditional plan, not an instruction to buy today. It stays valid while the 50-day average keeps rising and the stop level is not breached. At an entry of ₹3,163 the risk per share is ₹221.6, or 7.0% of the price, while the reward to the target is ₹1,234, or 39%. At the current close the risk per share is ₹376.8, or 11.4% of the price, for a reward of ₹1,078.8, or 32.5%. The difference is the whole reason for waiting: the same stock and the same target give a payoff that is almost twice as good at the lower price. Intraday volume-weighted price data is N/A because only daily bars are in the ledger. Because the plan is built from closing prices, orders should be limit orders and should be reviewed at the close, not chased during the session. That habit also keeps the position size consistent with the stop that was used to calculate it.

Worked example: from price to position size. Suppose an investor has a portfolio of ₹10,00,000 and is willing to lose 1% of it, or ₹10,000, on this idea. This is an illustration, not a recommendation, and the portfolio value is assumed. Step 1: the entry is a limit order at ₹3,163, the middle of the zone. Step 2: the stop is ₹2,941.4, so the loss per share if the stop is hit is 3,163 − 2,941.4 = ₹221.6. Step 3: the number of shares is 10,000 ÷ 221.6 = 45.1, rounded down to 45 shares. Step 4: the cost is 45 × 3,163 = ₹1,42,335, which is 14.2% of the portfolio and sits just inside the 15% single-name cap. Step 5: the reward at the target of ₹4,397 is 45 × 1,234 = ₹55,530, so the reward-to-risk is 55,530 ÷ 9,972 = 5.6. If instead the investor bought at ₹3,318.2 with the same stop, the loss per share would be ₹376.8, only 26 shares would fit within the ₹10,000 budget, and the reward-to-risk would fall to 2.86. The example shows why the entry price changes both the size and the quality of the trade.

Case note 12: the entry zone. The entry zone of ₹3,136–₹3,190 starts at the 50-day average of ₹3,136.06 and extends half an ATR (₹57.6) above it.[26] The stock closed at ₹3,318.2, which is ₹128–182 above the zone, or 4.0–5.5% higher. Verdict: wait. Rule: limit orders only inside the zone, sized so that a stop at ₹2,941.4 loses no more than the set risk budget. Trigger: if the stock closes inside the zone, the plan becomes active; if it closes below ₹3,056.6 (the swing low) before the 50-day average catches up, the ladder reads SUPPORT_BROKEN and no order should be placed.

What would change the view on the trade plan.

  • If the close enters ₹3,136–₹3,190 on volume below the 20-day average, then place a limit order sized by the example above.
  • If the close falls below ₹3,056.6 and the 50-day average turns down, then cancel the plan and wait for a new base.
  • If the stock rises above ₹3,481.4 without first pulling back, then do not chase; reset the measured move and wait for a retest.
  • If the 50-day average rises above ₹3,250, then raise the entry zone by the same amount, because the support has moved up.

8. Adversarial Red-Team and Invalidation

A dossier that only argues for an idea is advertising. This chapter argues against it on purpose, names the one claim the thesis hangs on and writes down the events that would end the idea. The final chapters then turn the work into allocation and monitoring rules.

8.1 Fatal flaw scenarios

A red-team review asks how the thesis could fail and what evidence would show it early. We built three scenarios, each with the number that would confirm it. Each scenario that follows is a flaw in the idea of owning MCX at today's price, not a prediction that it will happen.

Scenario 1: the collateral rule hollows out proprietary volume. If more than the estimated 15–20% guarantee-backed share quits and liquidity thins, quoted spreads widen and hedgers move to lower-cost venues. If all 15–20% of volume left, revenue would fall ₹105–140 crore a quarter, or 20–27% of the quarter's pre-tax profit of ₹523 crore. Evidence to watch: second-quarter premium turnover and traded clients.[21]

Scenario 2: competitors fund a fee war. NSE and BSE both have strong balance sheets and an incentive to take commodity share, and BSE has said it wants 15%.[24] A 10-point share loss would cost about ₹70 crore of quarterly revenue, which is 13% of pre-tax profit before any fee concession, and it would break the monopoly narrative that supports the 55-times multiple.[23] Evidence to watch: reported share on gold and silver futures.

Scenario 3: volatility normalises and the multiple compresses. If Q4 FY26 was the peak and quarterly profit settles near ₹300 crore (EPS about ₹47 annualised), a 40-times multiple gives ₹1,880 and even 50 times gives ₹2,350, near the three-month base. That is a downside stress case below the Chapter 0 bear case, and it is the reason the price must come to support first.[4] Evidence to watch: gold and silver price swings and Q2 FY27 premium turnover.

The three scenarios hit different parts of the business: volume, share and volatility. Because they are largely independent, the chance that at least one of them occurs within a year is high, even though each alone is a minority outcome. That is the reason the position-size cap and the stop matter more than the point forecast.

8.2 The hinge claim

The thesis stands on one claim: that Q1 FY27 profit of about ₹413 crore is a floor-like run-rate and not a peak. The evidence supports it partly (margin held at 72%, notional volume at records) and contradicts it partly (revenue −21% and premium −15% quarter on quarter). The right response is to wait for Q2 FY27 to show premium turnover stabilising, which the company will report in early November 2026.

The reason this claim carries so much weight is arithmetic. At 54.9 times trailing EPS of ₹60.46, the price already assumes the earnings hold. If the real run-rate is nearer ₹300 crore a quarter, the same price equals about 70 times annualised EPS of ₹47, which is hard to defend. If the real run-rate is ₹450 crore, the multiple on annualised EPS is closer to 46. A reader who disagrees with the hinge claim can substitute their own quarterly figure into the same arithmetic.

8.3 Invalidation triggers

Invalidation means the point at which the idea is wrong and the file moves to review. A good invalidation rule is written before the trade, so that fear and hope do not choose it afterwards. For MCX the rules are set by the stop and by business measures that would show the franchise weakening.

  • A close below ₹2,941 breaks the Core stop and the Stage 2 structure; the file moves to REJECTED review.
  • A 50-day average that turns down while the price is below it voids the entry zone.
  • Second-quarter options premium turnover below ₹7,500 crore a day, or traded clients below 13 lakh.
  • Any SEBI or RBI action that materially restricts proprietary participation in commodities.
  • Evidence that the GST notice is a pattern across years, not a single year.[8]

These five rules cover price, volume, participation, regulation and tax. If any one fires, the response is to stop adding and re-run the underwriting, not to hold on out of habit.

8.4 Key uncertainties and N/A items

The table lists what is unknown and why. Read the first row aloud: forward EPS and the three-year median P/E are N/A because no consensus forecast or verified multiple history was obtained. Each row gives an item, its status and the reason, so a reader knows which numbers are missing and why they are not guessed.

The table keeps the dossier honest. None of the missing items would change the verdict on its own, but several limit how precisely the target can be set. Without a forward EPS, the target rests on a chart pattern rather than on an earnings forecast, and the pattern implies a multiple of about 73 times, so a reader should treat ₹4,397 as an upper-end target and not as a central estimate. Without a macro series, the dossier cannot say how interest rates or the dollar are affecting gold and silver activity. Without NSDL data the peer set is smaller than it could be. A later refresh should fill these items first, because each of them would sharpen the valuation work.

Case note 13: the hinge claim on earnings. The base case rests on quarterly profit of ₹400–450 crore, against ₹413 crore in Q1 FY27 and ₹530 crore at the March 2026 peak.[2] If quarterly profit were ₹300 crore, annualised EPS would be about ₹47 and the current price would be about 70 times earnings; at ₹450 crore it would be about 46 times. Verdict: the thesis is sensitive and the price already assumes the better case. Rule: do not buy extended when the hinge claim is unproven. Trigger: if Q2 FY27 profit lands at ₹400 crore or more, the claim is supported; below ₹350 crore it is contradicted and the bear weight should rise.

What would change the view on risk.

  • If premium turnover rebounds above ₹10,000 crore a day, then the normalisation fear fades and the bear probability can fall to 20%.
  • If a fee cut is announced to defend share, then reduce the base target and re-run asymmetry with lower revenue.
  • If the GST notice is followed by further years' notices, then add a tax-risk item to the bear case.
  • If the RBI relaxes the collateral rule, then guarantee-backed volume returns and the bull case becomes more likely.

9. Portfolio Allocation & Replacement Guidance

Turning a view into a position needs a size, a sequence and a rule for what to sell to fund it. This chapter gives those rules for MCX and explains why the answer today is zero shares while the plan for a pullback is already written. Sizing is the part of investing that most readers skip, yet it decides how much a wrong call costs. The guidance here follows the single-name cap and the stop-based method used across the shortlist, so the same logic applies to every name in the file. The monitoring chapter that follows lists the numbers to check after each results release.

9.1 Sizing logic

The single-name hard cap is 15% of the portfolio. Because the momentum gate returns NO_MOMENTUM and the ladder says NO_BUY, the allocation today is zero. If the pullback to ₹3,136–₹3,190 occurs, a starter position of about a third of the intended size is reasonable; the remainder should wait for a hold above the 50-day average or for evidence that second-quarter premium turnover has stabilised.

The size should be set by the money at risk (entry minus ₹2,941), not by conviction, as the worked example in Chapter 7 shows. A high-conviction idea with a wide stop deserves fewer shares than a modest idea with a tight one, because the loss if wrong is what the portfolio must absorb. The capital allocator stage that follows this dossier will convert this guidance into share counts, so this dossier supplies only the zone, the stop and the target.

9.2 Replacement logic and tranche plan

Tax and trading costs matter here. Selling a long-held position to buy MCX incurs capital gains tax and brokerage, so the new idea must overcome those costs as well as the old holding's own prospects. For that reason the replacement rule asks for a clear margin, not a marginal edge, and treats a tie as a reason to do nothing. This is also why a deferred name such as MCX costs nothing to wait on: the opportunity stays on the list while the existing portfolio keeps working.

One more guard applies to concentration. MCX, BSE and CDSL are all market-infrastructure businesses that respond to the same driver, which is trading activity. Holding several of them at full size would multiply one risk instead of spreading it, so the shortlist rules count them as a related group when checking the combined weight. If another exchange name were already held, the MCX allocation would be reduced to keep the group within its limit.

Capital is finite, so every addition to the portfolio is also a decision about what not to hold. A new name earns a place only if its payoff, measured by asymmetry at its own entry price, is clearly better than the weakest existing holding, after allowing for the cost and tax of selling. Where a swap is judged worthwhile, the sale funds the purchase on the same day, so the portfolio does not carry idle cash waiting for a decision that has already been made.

If the shortlist is full, MCX should displace a name only if its asymmetry at entry exceeds the weakest current holding's by a clear margin. At ₹3,163 it clears the Core floor by almost double, so it would qualify as a replacement candidate in that case. At today's close it does not, because the asymmetry is below the floor and the ladder blocks the entry.

A tranche plan keeps the decision mechanical. The first third goes in at the first limit order inside the zone. The second third goes in only after the stock holds the 50-day average for three sessions with volume under the 20-day average. The final third waits for Q2 FY27 results. If the stop is hit at any stage, the remaining tranches are cancelled, which limits the loss to the first tranche or two.

What would change the view on allocation.

  • If the close enters the zone and holds three sessions, then add the second tranche.
  • If Q2 FY27 profit exceeds ₹450 crore, then add the final tranche up to the 15% cap, never above.
  • If another shortlist name offers asymmetry above 5.0 at its own entry, then fund it before adding to MCX.
  • If the stop at ₹2,941.4 is hit, then cancel remaining tranches and review.

10. Quarterly Monitoring Indicators

A thesis is only useful if you can tell when it is going wrong. This chapter lists the quarterly numbers, the levels that should alarm you and the places to read them, then sets the review calendar. Keep it beside the results release each quarter and tick off each row as better, worse or unchanged. Doing so takes about fifteen minutes and replaces the guesswork that usually follows a surprising share-price move. The final chapters after it add the ledger screens as market context, a glossary and the references.

10.1 What to track each quarter

Each row is a number to check when MCX reports, with its current value, the level that should make you act and the source to look in. Read the first row aloud: options premium turnover is ₹9,086 crore a day, and below ₹7,500 crore is the warning. Keep the table open when the next results arrive and mark each row as better or worse.

IndicatorCurrentWatch triggerWhere to look
Options premium average daily turnover₹9,086 croreBelow ₹7,500 croreInvestor presentation[2]
Traded F&O clients13.72 lakhBelow 13 lakhInvestor presentation[2]
EBITDA margin72%Below 65%Results filing and corporate announcements[6]
Market share on gold, silver, crudeAbove 99%Below 95%Exchange data, management call[3]
Bank-guarantee share of marginAbout 60% of the margin book (press reports)Disclosed decline in volumesCall transcript, RBI circulars[22]
GST proceedingFiling identified; amount N/A pending cache verificationAny further notice or orderExchange filings[8]
Price versus 50-day average1.58 ATR aboveClose within 0.5 ATR of ₹3,136Daily chart[7]

The monitoring table turns the thesis into seven checkable numbers. Four of them come from one document, the quarterly investor presentation, which makes the review quick: premium turnover, traded clients, margin and market share can all be read in one sitting. The other three come from exchange filings, press reports and the price chart. The bank-guarantee share is the least certain, because the figure of about 60% comes from press reports and not from a company filing, so a reader should look for the company's own statement on the next call. The price row is the one that triggers action, since it tells you when the entry zone is reached. A sensible routine is to review after every results release and whenever the close moves into the zone.

10.2 Review calendar

Each review should end with one written line: the thesis is intact, weakened or broken, and the reason in a sentence. Writing the line forces a decision, and over several quarters the lines show whether the original reasoning was sound. If three consecutive reviews read weakened, the position should be cut by half even if no single review reached the break level. This simple discipline guards against the slow drift in which each small disappointment is explained away.

The review should also record what has changed in the market around the stock, such as the breadth reading and the regime label. A stock that holds its levels while the regime moves from Downtrend to Uptrend is a stronger signal than the same stock holding while breadth falls further, and the note should say which of the two is happening.

A review routine has two parts: a fixed date and an event trigger. The fixed date is the results release, when four of the seven indicators update at once. The event trigger is any close inside the entry zone or any filing that mentions the regulator, a tax authority or a senior resignation. Keeping both makes sure that neither a quiet calendar nor a noisy headline decides the outcome, and the notes written at each review become the record of whether the thesis held.

The review calendar is simple: re-check after the Q2 FY27 results, which should arrive in early November 2026, and any time the stock closes in the entry zone. A quarterly cadence suits a business whose reported numbers change once a quarter, and a price trigger suits a plan whose entry depends on the chart. Between those events there is nothing to do, and the discipline of doing nothing is part of the strategy.

The company's corporate announcement page lists every filing as it appears, including results, trading updates and changes in senior staff, so it is the place to confirm that no event has been missed.[6][31] Dates for the next results had not been confirmed in the sources we read, so the early-November timing is an expectation based on past patterns, not a published date.

11. Ledger Bull Screen

The bull ledger screen is a controlled comparison set. Its rows are selected by the same capitalization buckets and technical rules used in the facts pack, so the list shows opportunity cost and downside context without becoming a hand-picked recommendation. Use it to decide what deserves fresh research, then apply company-specific underwriting before allocating capital.

The facts ledger publishes a daily bull screen of strong-trend stocks. We reproduce it as context for how MCX's strength compares with the rest of the market, and we do not analyse the other names. The relevance of the screen is that it shows how few stocks currently hold rising averages, which tells you whether MCX's leadership is common or rare in this tape. The explanation of the screen comes first, then the table, then how it bears on the verdict. The bear screen in the chapter after this one shows the opposite side of the market.

11.1 What the bull screen is

The facts ledger for this session screens the Nifty 500 universe each day for stocks that show a Stage 2 structure, meaning the price is above rising 50-day and 200-day averages. It then picks up to ten names in each size tier: large, mid and small. For 9 October 2026 the ledger returned 26 bull picks, ten large, ten mid and six small. The list is context for the MCX decision, not analysis of those names; this dossier did not underwrite any of them.

MCX does not appear in the ledger's bull picks. It appears only in the looser, relaxed bull list, which fits our finding that it is a strong trend but sits too far above its support for the strict screen. A reader can use the list to see what the rest of the market's leaders look like in a Downtrend regime and to judge whether MCX's leadership is unusual. Each row in the table is copied from the ledger without adjustment.

Of the 26 picks, five carry the ledger's confirmed tag (CPPLUS, PERSISTENT, KOTAKBANK, BBOX and CYIENT), meaning the structure is in place and volume supports it. The list is dominated by banks, information technology and capital goods, which tells you the leadership in a weak market is narrow and sector-specific, not broad. MCX, with an RSI of 54.04 and volume at 0.85 times average, would sit between the confirmed and the awaiting-volume names. The practical use is as context: MCX is one of a small group of stocks holding their averages, and that group is a minority of the index.

11.3 How the screen relates to the MCX verdict

The bull screen does not change the MCX verdict, because the verdict depends on business quality, asymmetry and support distance, none of which the screen measures. What the screen does tell us is how the market is behaving: of roughly 500 stocks, only a small number qualify, and breadth stands at 17.7% above the 50-day average. In such a tape, a stock that holds its averages tends to draw buyers on pullbacks, which supports the idea of waiting for the pullback and not buying the breakout.

It also gives a measure of competition for capital. If a name from the confirmed list offers asymmetry above 3.0 at a reasonable entry, it would compete with MCX for the same risk budget. That comparison belongs to the allocation stage, where each candidate's stop and target are set by the same rules, so the decision rests on like-for-like asymmetry.

12. Ledger Bear Screen

The bear ledger screen is a controlled comparison set. Its rows are selected by the same capitalization buckets and technical rules used in the facts pack, so the list shows opportunity cost and downside context without becoming a hand-picked recommendation. Use it to decide what deserves fresh research, then apply company-specific underwriting before allocating capital.

The matching bear screen lists stocks in a falling structure. It is included for the same reason as the bull list: to show what the market around MCX looks like, not to comment on those companies. Reading both lists together gives a picture of breadth that a single stock's chart cannot show. The glossary and the references follow this chapter, so any term or source you want to check is a short step away.

12.1 What the bear screen is

The bear screen is the mirror image of the bull screen. It looks for stocks in a Stage 4 structure, with the price below falling 50-day and 200-day averages, and picks up to ten in each size tier. For 9 October 2026 the ledger returned 30 bear picks, ten large, ten mid and ten small. We reproduce them to show what weakness looks like in this market and to explain what the MCX structure is not. None of these names was underwritten here, and no view on them is expressed.

MCX does not appear on any of the ledger's bear lists, which is consistent with its Stage 2 reading. The relevance of the bear list to MCX is indirect: the broader regime is in a Downtrend, so many stocks are breaking down, and a stock holding up is an exception. That is useful for risk, because exceptions can be punished when sentiment turns, and a leader can fall as fast as the index if the market's weakness spreads.

Set against MCX's rising averages and 63-day relative strength of +26.73%, the contrast is clear: MCX is on the opposite side of the screen. That is reassuring for the trend, but it also means MCX is a crowded safe harbour in a weak market, and crowded places can empty quickly.

12.3 Reading the bear screen for risk

Reading the list for risk means asking what it implies for a holder of a strong stock, not for the stocks named. The answer is that selling pressure is broad, and that funds raising cash often sell their winners first. That makes a rising stock vulnerable to a drop that has nothing to do with its own business, which is the case the stop is designed for.

The bear screen is a warning about the environment more than about MCX. Thirty names with Stage 4 structure and ledger breadth of only 17.7% above the 50-day average describe a market where most stocks are falling. MCX's strength is a fact, but in such markets even strong stocks can give back gains in a broad sell-off, which is why the stop of ₹2,941.4 matters and why we do not size above the 15% cap.

The screen also checks our own wording. We call MCX a Stage 2 stock; the ledger agrees, and no bear list contains it. If MCX were to close below its 50-day average and the 200-day average at ₹2,775.32, it would start to resemble the rows above, and the invalidation rules in Chapter 8 would already have fired. That is the purpose of a stop: to act before the stock looks like the names in this table.

13. Glossary

These plain-language definitions cover every technical term used in the dossier, so a reader can look up a word without leaving the page.

ATR (average true range)
The typical size of a day's price move; for MCX, ₹115.19. It is used to measure how far the price is from support and to set stops.
Asymmetry
Potential gain divided by potential loss, using a target and a stop. A ratio of 3.0 means three rupees of reward for each rupee risked.
Clearing member
A firm that settles trades with the exchange's clearing house and posts margin; MCX has 248 of them.
Core settlement guarantee fund
A pool of money held by the clearing house to cover a member's default; ₹1,444 crore at Q1 FY27.
EBITDA
Earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortisation; a measure of operating profit.
Float
Money held for others, such as clearing-member margin, that sits on the balance sheet and earns interest but is not owner profit.
Haircut
A reduction in the value credited to collateral; a 40% haircut means ₹100 of collateral counts as ₹60.
Measured move
A chart-based target: the height of a price base added to the breakout level.
Notional turnover
The face value of the contracts traded, as opposed to the money actually paid for them.
Options premium
The price a buyer pays for an option. MCX's options revenue follows premium, not notional value.
ROCE (return on capital employed)
Operating profit divided by the capital the business uses; MCX earns 71.4%.
RSI (relative strength index)
A 0-to-100 gauge of recent price momentum; above 70 is often called overbought and below 30 oversold.
SMA (simple moving average)
The average closing price over a set number of days; the 50-day and 200-day versions are used for trend.
Stage 2 and Stage 4
Chart-structure labels: Stage 2 is a rising trend above rising averages, Stage 4 is a falling trend below falling averages.
Stop
The price at which a position is sold to limit a loss; for MCX at Core settings, ₹2,941.4.
Traded F&O clients
The number of distinct investors who traded futures or options in a quarter; 13.72 lakh for MCX in Q1 FY27.
